Exact and Approximate Counting of Database Repairs
Marco Calautti, Ester Livshits, Andreas Pieris, Markus Schneider, [2mm] Department of Computer Science, University of Milan

Abstract
Database integrity constraints allow us to specify semantic properties that should be satisfied by all databases of a certain relational schema. However, real-life databases are often inconsistent, i.e., do not conform to their specifications in the form of integrity constraints. This leads to the problem of querying inconsistent databases, which aims at a mechanism that allows us to compute trustable answers to user queries, despite the inconsistency of the underlying database. Consistent query answering (CQA) is such a mechanism that goes back in the late 1990s. Its key elements are the notion of (database) repair, that is, a consistent database whose difference with the original inconsistent database is minimal according to some measure, and the notion of certain answers, that is, answers that are certainly true in all repairs. Although the notion of certain answers is conceptually meaningful, it is also very rigid as it may completely discard a candidate answer only because it is not entailed by few repairs. A more refined measure of certainty is the so-called relative frequency of a candidate answer, that is, the percentage of repairs that entail it. Of course, to compute the relative frequency of a candidate answer, we need a way to compute the number of repairs that entail it, and the total number of repairs. This brings us to the central problem of counting database repairs (with or without a query in place), which is the main theme of the present work. A key task in this context is to establish a complete complexity classification (a.k.a. dichotomy). When there is no query in place, this has been recently achieved for the central class of functional dependencies (FDs) via an FP / ♯ \sharp P -complete dichotomy. With a query in place, an analogous classification exists for primary keys and self-join-free conjunctive queries (CQs), but nothing is known once we go beyond primary keys. We lift the latter result to arbitrary FDs and self-join-free CQs. Another important task in this context is whenever the counting problem in question is intractable, to classify it as approximable, i.e., the target value can be efficiently approximated with error guarantees via a fully polynomial-time randomized approximation scheme (FPRAS), or as inapproximable. Although for primary keys and CQs (even with self-joins) the problem is always approximable, we prove that this is not the case for FDs (with or without a CQ in place). We show, however, that the class of FDs with a so-called left-hand side chain forms an island of approximability. These results, apart from being interesting in their own right, are crucial steps towards a complete classification of approximate counting of repairs in the case of FDs.



1
Introduction

In a relational database system, it is possible to specify semantic properties that should be satisfied by all databases of a certain relational schema such as “every social security number is associated with at most one person”. Such properties are crucial in the development of transparent and usable database schemas for complex applications, as well as for optimizing the evaluation of queries
[
1
]
.
Database semantic properties are typically specified via integrity constraints. For example, the above property about persons is a simple key constraint, which essentially states that in the relation that stores persons, the attribute “social security number” functionally determines all the other attributes of that relation.
Unfortunately, real-life databases are often
inconsistent
, i.e., do not conform to their specifications in the form of integrity constraints. The reason behind this is that data is not perfect and clean; it may come, for example, from several conflicting sources
[
5
]
.
Data cleaning attempts to fix this problem by resolving the inconsistencies
[
12
]
. However, this may lead to the loss of valuable information. Furthermore, there are scenarios like virtual data integration, where the data stays at the autonomous data sources, in which there is no way to modify the data without having ownership of the sources. This leads to the problem of querying inconsistent databases. In other words, we need a mechanism that allows us to compute meaningful and trustable answers to user queries, despite the fact that the underlying database is inconsistent.

1.1
Consistent Query Answering and Database Repairs

Consistent query answering (CQA) is an elegant framework introduced in the late 1990s by Arenas, Bertossi, and Chomicki
[
3
]
towards a mechanism for querying inconsistent databases that allows us to deliver meaningful answers to queries that can still be obtained from inconsistent data. The key elements underlying the CQA approach are:

1.

the notion of
(database) repair
of an inconsistent database
D
, that is, a consistent database whose difference with
D
is somehow minimal, and

2.

the notion of query answering based on
certain answers
, that is, answers that are entailed by every database repair.

A simple example, taken from
[
7
]
, that illustrates the above notions follows:

Example 1
.

Consider the relational schema consisting of a single relation name

\mathit{Employee}(\mathrm{id},\mathrm{name},\mathrm{dept})

that comes with the constraint that the attribute
\mathrm{id}
functionally determines
\mathrm{name}
and
\mathrm{dept}
. This means that the attribute
\mathrm{id}
is the key of
\mathit{Employee}
. Consider also the simple database
D

\mathit{Employee}

\mathrm{id}

\mathrm{name}

\mathrm{dept}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{HR}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{IT}

2

\mathtt{Alice}

\mathtt{IT}

2

\mathtt{Tim}

\mathtt{IT}

It is easy to see that
D
is inconsistent since we are uncertain about Bob’s department, and the name of the employee with id
2
. To devise a repair, we need to keep one tuple from each conflicting pair, which leads to a maximal subset of
D
that is consistent. Thus, we get the four repairs depicted below.

\mathit{Repair~1}

\mathrm{id}

\mathrm{name}

\mathrm{dept}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{HR}

2

\mathtt{Alice}

\mathtt{IT}

\mathit{Repair~2}

\mathrm{id}

\mathrm{name}

\mathrm{dept}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{HR}

2

\mathtt{Tim}

\mathtt{IT}

\mathit{Repair~3}

\mathrm{id}

\mathrm{name}

\mathrm{dept}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{IT}

2

\mathtt{Alice}

\mathtt{IT}

\mathit{Repair~4}

\mathrm{id}

\mathrm{name}

\mathrm{dept}

1

\mathtt{Bob}

\mathtt{IT}

2

\mathtt{Tim}

\mathtt{IT}

Observe now that the (Boolean) query that asks whether employees
1
and
2
work in the same department is true only in two out of four repairs, that is,
\mathit{Repair~3}
and
\mathit{Repair~4}
. Therefore, since the query is false in at least one repair, its final answer over the inconsistent database
D
is false. This is a conceptually meaningful answer as we are not entirely certain that employees
1
and
2
work in the same department, and thus, it will be misleading to evaluate the query to true.

Example
1
, despite its simplicity, illustrates one of the limitations of the CQA approach. The notion of certain answers only says that a candidate answer (i.e. a tuple of database values) is entailed by all repairs, or is not entailed by some repair. But, as discussed in
[
8
]
, the former is too strict, while the latter is not very useful in a practical context. Instead, we would like to know how often a tuple is an answer, that is, its
relative frequency
, or, in other words, the percentage of repairs that entail that tuple. For instance, in Example
1
, the relative frequency of the empty tuple, which corresponds to true and is the only candidate answer as the query is Boolean, is
\frac{1}{2}
since, out
of four repairs in total, only two of those entail the query. Of course, to compute the relative frequency of a tuple, we need a way to compute (i) the number of repairs that entail a tuple (the numerator), and (ii)
the total number of repairs (the denominator).

Note that another fundamental task where the number of repairs plays a crucial role is that of measuring the inconsistency of a database, a topic that has received significant attention over the years; see, e.g.,
[
6
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
17
,
20
,
21
,
27
,
28
]
.
Inconsistency measures serve as tools for assessing the reliability of a database and the amount of effort required for cleaning it. One particularly well-explored metric is based on the number repairs
[
14
,
15
,
21
,
27
,
28
]
. In this context, the task of counting repairs is that of estimating the extent of inconsistency in the database.

1.2
Counting Database Repairs

The problem of counting database repairs is indeed a central one in the context of querying inconsistent data, and has been extensively studied since the advent of the CQA approach. The two key counting problems of main concern are the following, with
\Sigma
being a set of integrity constraints and
Q
a query (note that, for the sake of clarity, we base our discussion on Boolean queries, but it can be naturally extended to non-Boolean queries):

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
: For a database
D
, compute the number of repairs of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
: For a database
D
, compute the number of repairs of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entail
Q

A significant amount of research work was dedicated to the task of pinpointing the complexity of the above problems, which is also the main concern of this work. It has been observed that both problems can be tractable or intractable depending on the syntactic shape of
\Sigma
and
Q
. This leads to the natural question whether we can establish a complete complexity classification, i.e., for every
\Sigma
and
Q
, classify
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
and
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
as tractable, i.e., in
FP
(the counting analogue of
PTime
), or intractable, i.e.,
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-complete (with
\sharp\text{\rm P}
being the counting analogue of
NP
), by simply inspecting
\Sigma
and
Q
.
Such a classification, apart from being very interesting from a theoretical point of view, can be also very useful in a more practical context as it can tell us precisely when the set of constraints and query at hand render the problem of interest tractable, which in turn guides the choice of the counting algorithm to be applied.

It is well-known that establishing such complexity classifications (a.k.a. dichotomies) is a highly non-trivial task. Nevertheless, despite the underlying technical difficulties, such classifications have been established for important classes of integrity constraints. In particular, there are interesting classifications for the central class of
functional dependencies
(FDs), which generalize key constraints and allow us to express useful properties of the form “some attributes functionally determine some other attributes”; the standard syntax of an FD is
R:X\rightarrow Y
, where
R
is a relation name and
X,Y
are sets of attributes of
R
, stating that the attributes in
X
functionally determine the attributes in
Y
. Here is a comprehensive summary of what we currently know:

•

The work
[
23
]
has recently established a complexity classification for the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
assuming that
\Sigma
is a set of FDs. More precisely, it has been shown that whenever
\Sigma
has a so-called left-hand side (LHS, for short) chain (up to equivalence),
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is in
FP
; otherwise, it is
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-complete. We also know that checking whether
\Sigma
has an LHS chain (up to equivalence) is feasible in polynomial time. Let us recall that a set
\Sigma
of FDs has an LHS chain if, for every two FDs
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1}
and
R:X_{2}\rightarrow Y_{2}
of
\Sigma
,
X_{1}\subseteq X_{2}
or
X_{2}\subseteq X_{1}
.

•

When it comes to
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, such a classification has been established in
[
25
]
assuming that
\Sigma
is a set of
primary keys
, i.e., at most one key constraint per relation name, and
Q
is a
self-join-free conjunctive query
(SJFCQ), i.e., a CQ that cannot mention a relation name more than once. In particular, it has been shown that whenever
Q
is a so-called safe query w.r.t.
\Sigma
, where safety is a technical notion introduced in
[
25
]
that can be checked in polynomial time,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
; otherwise, it is
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-complete.
An analogous classification for arbitrary CQs with self-joins was established by the same authors in
[
26
]
under the assumption that the primary keys are simple, i.e., they consist of a single attribute.

1.3
Main Research Questions

From the above summary, it is clear that for the central class of FDs, which is the main concern of this work, the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is well-understood.
However, for the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, once we go beyond primary keys we know very little concerning the existence of a complete complexity classification as the ones described above. In particular, the dichotomy result of
[
25
]
does not apply when we consider arbitrary FDs. This brings us to the following question:

Research Question 1:
Can we lift the dichotomy result for primary keys and SJFCQs to the more general case of functional dependencies?

Let us stress that the above question is deliberately stated for self-join-free CQs and not for arbitrary CQs. The reason is because providing a complete classification for arbitrary CQs with self-joins is an overly ambitious task that is currently out of reach. Indeed, it is well-known that self-joins in CQs cause several complications, and this is why the question whether such a classification exists even for primary keys and CQs with self-joins remains a challenging open problem ten years after the classification result for primary keys and CQs without self-joins was established.

Another key task is to classify the problems
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
and
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, for a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q
(with or without self-joins), as approximable, that is, the target value can be efficiently approximated with error guarantees via a fully polynomial-time randomized approximation scheme (FPRAS), or as inapproximable. This leads to the following question:

Research Question 2:
For a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q
(with or without self-joins), can we determine whether
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
and
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admit an FPRAS by inspecting
\Sigma
and
Q
?

Note that whenever
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
or
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is tractable, then it is trivially approximable. Therefore, the above question for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
trivializes whenever
\Sigma
has an LHS chain (up to equivalence). However, for FDs without an LHS chain (up to equivalence) this is not true since there exists a set
\Sigma
such that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is not approximable; the latter is actually a result of the present work.
Concerning
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, we only know that if
\Sigma
consists of primary keys and
Q
is a CQ (even with self-joins),
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is always approximable; this is implicit in
[
11
]
, and it has been made explicit in
[
7
]
.
Once we go beyond primary keys, the above question for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is a non-trivial one since, depending on the syntactic shape of
\Sigma
and
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
can be approximable or not; the latter is also a result of the present work.

1.4
Summary of Contributions

Our goal is to provide answers to the questions discussed above. For Question 1, we provide a definitive answer. For Question 2, we establish results that form crucial steps towards a definitive answer. As we explain, an answer to Question 2 will resolve a challenging graph-theoretic open problem. Our contributions can be summarized as follows:

•

Concerning Question 1, we lift the dichotomy of
[
25
]
for primary keys and SJFCQs to the general case of FDs (Theorem
2
). To this end, we build on the dichotomy for the problem of counting repairs (without a query) from
[
23
]
, which allows us to concentrate on FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) since for all the other cases we can inherit the
\sharp\text{P}
-hardness.
Therefore, our main technical task was actually to lift the dichotomy for primary keys and SJFCQs from
[
25
]
to the case of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence). Although the proof of this result borrows several ideas from the proof of
[
25
]
, the task of lifting the result to FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) is a non-trivial one. This is due to the significantly more complex structure of database repairs under FDs with an LHS chain compared to those under primary keys.
The tractable side of the dichotomy is proved by introducing a polynomial-time algorithm. For the intractable side, we first introduce certain rewrite rules for pairs consisting of a set
\Sigma
of FDs and an SJFCQ
Q
. A crucial property of those rules is that whenever
(\Sigma,Q)
can be rewritten into
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, there is a polynomial-time Turing reduction from
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
to
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
. We then show that every pair
(\Sigma,Q)
can be rewritten, by following a sequence of rewrite rules, into a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
that we call final. Intuitively, a final pair cannot be rewritten further without breaking its non-safety. For final pairs, we establish
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hardness via a reduction from the primary key case, and the
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hardness for all the non-final pairs is obtained from the sequence of rewritings; further details are discussed in Section
4
.

•

Concerning Question 2, although we do not establish a complete classification, we provide results that, apart from being interesting in their own right, are crucial steps towards a complete classification. We first reveal the difficulty underlying a proper approximability/inapproximability
dichotomy by discussing that such a result will resolve the challenging open problem of whether counting maximal matchings in a bipartite graph is approximable. We then show that, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and a CQ
Q
(even with self-joins),
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS (item (1) of Theorem
8
).
On the other hand, we show that there exists a very simple set
\Sigma
of FDs such that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
does not admit an FPRAS (item (2) of Theorem
8
), which in turn allows us to show that, for every SJFCQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
does not admit an FPRAS (item (3) of Theorem
8
). As usual, these inapproximability results hold under a standard complexity-theoretic assumption. The inapproximability of
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
exploits the interesting technique of gap amplification, used to prove inapproximability of optimization problems
[
16
]
.

Roadmap.

In Section
2
, we recall the basics on relational databases, FDs and CQs. The problems of interest are formally defined in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we introduce the class of FDs with a left-hand side chain that plays a crucial role in our analysis. A definitive answer to Question 1 stated above is provided in Section
5
, whereas a partial answer to Question 2 is given in Section
6
. We conclude in Section
7
by briefly discussing our results and giving directions for future research.
For the sake of readability, several technical details and proofs are given in a clearly marked appendix.

Figure 1:
An inconsistent database of a railroad company.

We recall the basics on relational databases, functional dependencies, and conjunctive queries. We consider the disjoint countably infinite sets
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{V}
of
constants
and
variables
, respectively. For an integer
n>0
, we write
[n]
for the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Relational Databases.

A
(relational) schema

\mathbf{S}
is a finite set of relation names with associated arity; we write
R/n
to denote that
R
has arity
n>0
. Each relation name
R/n
is associated with a tuple of distinct attribute names
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
; we write
\mathsf{att}(R)
for the set of attributes
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\}
. A
position
of
\mathbf{S}
is a pair
(R,A)
, where
R\in\mathbf{S}
and
A\in\mathsf{att}(R)
, that essentially identifies the attribute
A
of the relation name
R
.
A
fact
over
\mathbf{S}
is an expression of the form
R(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
, where
R/n\in\mathbf{S}
, and
c_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
for each
i\in[n]
; we may say
R
-fact to indicate that the relation name is
R
. A
database

D
over
\mathbf{S}
is a finite set of facts over
\mathbf{S}
. We write
D_{R}
, for
R\in\mathbf{S}
, for the database
\{f\in D\mid f\text{ is an }R\text{-fact}\}
. The
active domain
of
D
, denoted
\mathsf{dom}(D)
, is the set of constants occurring in
D
.
For a fact
f=R(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
, with
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
being the tuple of attribute names of
R
, we write
f[A_{i}]
for
c_{i}
, that is, the value of the attribute
A_{i}
in the fact
f
.
We say that a constant
c
occurs in
f
at position
(R,A)
if
f[A]=c
.

Example 2
.

Figure
1
depicts a
database, partially taken from
[
22
]
, over a schema with two relation names:
\mathit{Schedule}
(storing a train schedule) and
\mathit{Station}
(storing the locations of train stations). The relation name
\mathit{Schedule}
is associated with the tuple
(\mathrm{train},\mathrm{origin},\mathrm{destination},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration})
of attribute names,
and the relation name
\mathit{Station}
is associated with
(\mathrm{name},\mathrm{city})
. The fact

f_{1}\ =\ \mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{NYP},\mathtt{BBY},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{315})

states that train number 16 is scheduled to depart from the New York Penn (NYP) Station at 10:30, and arrive at the Boston Back Bay (BBY) Station 315 minutes later. Hence, for example, we have that
f_{1}[\mathrm{origin}]=\mathtt{NYP}
. The first and the third fact of
\mathit{Station}

g_{1}\ =\ \mathit{Station}(\mathtt{NYP},\mathtt{NewYork})\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad g_{3}\ =\ \mathit{Station}(\mathtt{BBY},\mathtt{Boston})

state that the NYP station is located in New York and the BBY station is located in Boston.

Functional Dependencies.

A
functional dependency
(FD)
\phi
over a schema
\mathbf{S}
is an expression

R\ :\ X\rightarrow Y

where
R/n\in\mathbf{S}
and
X,Y\subseteq\mathsf{att}(R)
;
we may call
\phi
an
R
-FD to indicate that it is defined over the relation name
R
. We call
\phi
a
key
if
X\cup Y=\mathsf{att}(R)
. When
X
or
Y
are singletons, we may avoid the curly brackets, and simply write the attribute name.
Given a set
\Sigma
of FDs over
\mathbf{S}
, we write
\Sigma_{R}
, for
R\in\mathbf{S}
, for the set
\{\phi\in\Sigma\mid\phi\text{ is an }R\text{-FD}\}
.
We call
\Sigma
a set of
primary keys
if it consists only of keys, and
|\Sigma_{R}|\leq 1
for each
R\in\mathbf{S}
.
A database
D
over
\mathbf{S}
satisfies an FD
\phi
over
\mathbf{S}
, denoted
D\models\phi
, if, for every two
R
-facts
f,g\in D
the following holds:
f[A]=g[A]
for every
A\in X
implies
f[B]=g[B]
for every
B\in Y
. In simple words,
D
satisfies
\phi
if every two
R
-facts
f,g
of
D
either agree on the values of all the attributes of
X\cup Y
, or disagree on the value of at least one attribute of
X
.
We say that
D
is
consistent
w.r.t. a set
\Sigma
of FDs, written
D\models\Sigma
, if
D\models\phi
for every
\phi\in\Sigma
; otherwise,
D
is
inconsistent
w.r.t.
\Sigma
.
Two sets of FDs
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are
equivalent
if, for every database
D
,
D\models\Sigma
iff
D\models\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Example 3
.

Consider again the database depicted in Figure
1
and the FDs

\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin}\quad\quad\quad\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}

stating that the train number and departure time determine the origin station, and the train number, departure time, and duration determine the destination station. We also consider the FD

\mathit{Station}\ :\ \mathrm{name}\rightarrow\mathrm{city}

which is actually a single (primary) key over
\mathit{Station}
. Note that there are no violations of this key in the database; however, the first and third fact of
\mathit{Schedule}
, for example, jointly violate the first FD given above. Therefore, the database is inconsistent.

Conjunctive Queries.

A
(relational) atom

\alpha
over a schema
\mathbf{S}
is an expression
R(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
, where
R/n\in\mathbf{S}
, and
t_{i}\in\mathbf{C}\cup\mathbf{V}
for each
i\in[n]
; we may say
R
-atom to indicate that the relation name is
R
. Note that a fact is an atom without variables. As for facts, we may say that
a term
t\in\mathbf{C}\cup\mathbf{V}
occurs in
\alpha
at a certain position
(R,A)
with
A\in\mathsf{att}(R)
, and we may write
\alpha[A]
for the term
t
occurring in
\alpha
at position
(R,A)
.
A
conjunctive query
(CQ)
Q
over
\mathbf{S}
is an expression

\textrm{Ans}(\bar{x})\ \mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}\ R_{1}(\bar{y}_{1}),\ldots,R_{n}(\bar{y}_{n})

where each
R_{i}(\bar{y}_{i})
, for
i\in[n]
, is an atom over
\mathbf{S}
,
\bar{x}
are the
answer variables
of
Q
, and each variable in
\bar{x}
is mentioned in
\bar{y}_{i}
for some
i\in[n]
. We may write
Q(\bar{x})
to indicate that
\bar{x}
are the answer variables of
Q
. When
\bar{x}
is empty,
Q
is called
Boolean
.
A subclass of CQs that will play a crucial role in our analysis is that of self-join-free CQs. Formally, a
self-join-free
CQ (SJFCQ) over a schema
\mathbf{S}
is a CQ over
\mathbf{S}
that mentions every relation name of
\mathbf{S}
at most once. We may treat a CQ as the set of atoms in the right-hand side of the
\mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}
symbol. Let
\mathsf{var}(Q)
and
\mathsf{const}(Q)
be the set of variables and constants in
Q
, respectively. The semantics of CQs is given via homomorphisms.
A
homomorphism
from a CQ
Q
as the one above to a database
D
is a function
h:\mathsf{var}(Q)\cup\mathsf{const}(Q)\rightarrow\mathsf{dom}(D)
, which is the identity over
\mathbf{C}
, such that
R_{i}(h(\bar{y}_{i}))\in D
for
i\in[n]
.
A tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D)^{|\bar{x}|}
is an
answer to
Q
over
D
if there is a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D
with
h(\bar{x})=\bar{t}
. Let
Q(D)
be the answers to
Q
over
D
. For Boolean CQs, we write
D\models Q
, and say that
D

entails

Q
, if
()\in Q(D)
.

Example 4
.

We consider the following CQs over the schema of the database of Figure
1
:

Q_{1}\ =\ \textrm{Ans}(x,y)\ \mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}\ \mathit{Schedule}(x,\mathtt{BBY},z,y,w),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})

that asks for all the train numbers and departure times of trains that depart from the BBY station and arrive to Washington, and

Q_{2}\ =\ \textrm{Ans}(x,y,w)\ \mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}\ \mathit{Schedule}(x,\mathtt{BBY},z,y,w),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})

that asks for all the train numbers, departure times, and ride durations of such trains. Note that both queries are self-join free.
Note that the tuple

(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})
is an answer to
Q_{1}
over the database, as witnessed by the homomorphism
h
with
h(x)=\mathtt{16}
,
h(y)=\mathtt{1030}
,
h(z)=\mathtt{WAS}
, and
h(w)=\mathtt{420}
. The same homomorphism,
this time with respect to the variables of
Q_{2}
, witnesses that
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})
is an answer to
Q_{2}
over the database.

Database Repairs.

Informally, a repair of an inconsistent database
D
w.r.t. a set of integrity constraints is a consistent database
D^{\prime}
that minimally deviates from
D
. Here,
minimality refers to the symmetric difference between
D
and
D^{\prime}
. In the
case of anti-monotonic constraints (e.g., FDs), where consistency
cannot be violated by removing facts, a repair is a maximal consistent subset (i.e., subset repair
[
2
]
).

Formally, given a database
D
and a set
\Sigma
of FDs, both over a schema
\mathbf{S}
, a
repair
of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
is a database
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
such that

1.

D^{\prime}\models\Sigma
, and

2.

for every
D^{\prime\prime}\supsetneq D^{\prime}
,
D^{\prime\prime}\not\models\Sigma
.

In simple words, a repair of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
is a maximal subset of
D
that is consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma
. Note that if
D
is consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma
, then there is only one repair, that is,
D
itself. Let
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
be the set of repairs of
D
w.r.t
\Sigma
.
Given a CQ
Q(\bar{x})
over
\mathbf{S}
, and a tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D)^{|\bar{x}|}
, we write
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)
for
\{D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\mid\bar{t}\in Q(D^{\prime})\}
, that is, the set of repairs
D^{\prime}
of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
such that
\bar{t}
is an answer to
Q
over
D^{\prime}
.
If
Q
is Boolean, and thus, the only possible answer is the empty tuple
()
, we write
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)
instead of
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,()}(D)
.
For brevity, we write
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)
for the cardinality of
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
and
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)
, respectively.

Example 5
.

It is easy to verify that the database
D
of Figure
1
has five repairs w.r.t. the set
\Sigma
consisting of the FDs given in Example
3
:

1.

\mathord{\{f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},g_{7}\}}
,

2.

\mathord{\{f_{3},f_{4},f_{5},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},g_{7}\}}
,

3.

\mathord{\{f_{6},f_{8},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},g_{7}\}}
,

4.

\mathord{\{f_{7},f_{8},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},g_{7}\}}
,

5.

\mathord{\{f_{9},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},g_{7}\}}
.

Therefore, it holds that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)=5
. Now, consider the query
Q_{1}
of Example
4
and the answer
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})
. Only the third and fourth repairs belong to
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{1},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})}(D)
, as these are the only repairs that contain the fact
f_{8}
that mentions a train departing from the BBY station and arriving at the WAS station in Washington. Hence, we have that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{1},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})}(D)=2
. The same holds for the query
Q_{2}
and the answer
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})
; that is,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{2},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})}(D)=2
.

A useful observation is that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D^{\prime})
, for some easily computable database
D^{\prime}
and tuple
\bar{t}
, providing that
Q
is self-join-free.

Lemma 1
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs, and an SJFCQ
Q(\bar{x})
. We can compute in polynomial time a database
D^{\prime}
and a tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D^{\prime})^{|\bar{x}|}
such that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We assume, w.l.o.g., that
\mathsf{const}(Q)\cap\mathsf{dom}(D)=\emptyset
; indeed, if this is not the case, then we can simply rename all the constants in
D
with fresh ones not occurring in
Q
without affecting the number of repairs of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
.
Let
D_{Q}
be the database obtained by replacing each variable
x
in
Q
with a fresh constant
c_{x}\in\mathbf{C}
not occurring in
\mathsf{dom}(D)
. Since
\mathsf{dom}(D)\cap\mathsf{dom}(D_{Q})=\emptyset
, and
D_{Q}
is consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma
since it contains at most one
R
-fact for each relation name
R
occurring in
Q
, we get that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup D_{Q})
. Moreover, for every repair
D^{\prime}
of
D\cup D_{Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
,
c(\bar{x})\in Q(D^{\prime})
with
c(\bar{x})
being the tuple of constants obtained by replacing each variable
x
in
\bar{x}
with
c_{x}
. Therefore,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,c(\bar{x})}(D\cup D_{Q})
. It is clear that
D_{Q}
and
c(\bar{x})
can be constructed in polynomial time, and the claim follows with
D^{\prime}=D\cup D_{Q}
and
\bar{t}=c(\bar{x})
.

3
Problems of Interest

The two counting problems of interest, already discussed in Section
1
, are formally defined as follows. Fix a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q(\bar{x})
. We consider the problems

PROBLEM
:

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)

INPUT
:

A database
D

OUTPUT
:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)

PROBLEM
:

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q(\bar{x}))

INPUT
:

A database
D
and a tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D)^{|\bar{x}|}

OUTPUT
:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)

Exact Counting of Database Repairs.

Recall that one of our goals, towards a definitive answer to Research Question 1, is to provide a complete complexity classification for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q(\bar{x}))
. In other words, for each set
\Sigma
of FDs and CQ
Q(\bar{x})
, the goal is to classify it as tractable (i.e., place it in FP) or as intractable (i.e., show that it is
\sharp
P-complete). Recall that FP and
\sharp
P are classes of
counting functions

\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. In particular, FP (the counting analog of
PTime
) is defined as

\text{\rm FP}\ =\ \{f\mid f\text{ is a polynomial-time computable counting function}\}

whereas
\sharp
P (the counting analog of NP) is defined as

\sharp\text{\rm P}\ =\ \{\mathsf{accept}_{M}\mid M\text{ is a polynomial-time non-deterministic Turing machine}\}

where
\mathsf{accept}_{M}
is the counting function
\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
that assigns to each
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
the number of accepting computations of
M
on input
x
.
Typically, hardness results for
\sharp
P rely on polynomial-time Turing reductions (a.k.a. Cook reductions). In particular, given two counting functions
f,g:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, we say that
f
is
Cook reducible
to
g
if there exists a polynomial-time deterministic transducer
M
, with access to an oracle for
g
, such that, for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,
f(x)=M(x)
.
As discussed in Section
1
, Maslowski and Wijsen
[
25
]
established the following classification:

Theorem 1

(
[
25
]
)
.

For a set
\Sigma
of primary keys and an SJFCQ
Q
, the following statements hold:

1.

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is either in
FP
or
\sharp
P
-complete.

2.

We can decide in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
whether
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
or
\sharp
P
-complete.
1
1

1

As usual,
||o||
denotes the size of the encoding of a syntactic object
o
.

As we shall see in Section
5
, one of the main results of the present work is a generalization of the above result to arbitrary functional dependencies.

Approximate Counting of Database Repairs.

Another key task is whenever
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
or
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is
\sharp
P
-complete to classify it as approximable, i.e., the target value can be efficiently approximated with error guarantees via a
fully polynomial-time randomized approximation scheme
(FPRAS, for short), or as inapproximable.
Formally, an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is a randomized algorithm
\mathsf{A}
that takes as input a database
D
,
\epsilon>0
, and
0<\delta<1
, runs in polynomial time in
||D||
,
1/\epsilon
and
\log(1/\delta)
, and produces a random variable
\mathsf{A}(D,\epsilon,\delta)
such that

\mathsf{Pr}\left(|\mathsf{A}(D,\epsilon,\delta)-\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)|\ \leq\ \epsilon\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\right)\ \geq\ 1-\delta.

Analogously, an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q(\bar{x}))
is a randomized algorithm
\mathsf{A}
that takes as input a database
D
, a tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D)^{|\bar{x}|}
,
\epsilon>0
, and
0<\delta<1
, runs in polynomial time in
||D||
,
||\bar{t}||
,
1/\epsilon
and
\log(1/\delta)
, and produces a random variable
\mathsf{A}(D,\bar{t},\epsilon,\delta)
such that

\mathsf{Pr}\left(|\mathsf{A}(D,\bar{t},\epsilon,\delta)-\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)|\ \leq\ \epsilon\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)\right)\ \geq\ 1-\delta.

The only known non-trivial result concerning approximate counting is that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q(\bar{x}))
always admits an FPRAS whenever
\Sigma
is a set of primary keys; this is implicit in
[
11
]
, and it has been made explicit in
[
7
]
.
The picture concerning approximate counting for arbitrary FDs is rather unexplored. We make crucial steps of independent interest towards a complete classification of approximate counting for FDs.
We show that (under a reasonable complexity-theoretic assumption) the existence of an FPRAS for both problems is not guaranteed whenever we focus on FDs. On the other hand, it is guaranteed for FDs with a so-called left-hand side chain (up to equivalence); the class of FDs with left-hand side chain is discussed in Section
4
.

Boolean vs. Non-Boolean CQs.

For technical clarity, both exact and approximate counting are studied for Boolean CQs, but all the results can be easily generalized to non-Boolean CQs.
Indeed, given a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q(\bar{x})
,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q,\bar{t}}(D)
, for some database
D
and tuple
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{dom}(D)^{|\bar{x}|}
, coincides with
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q(\bar{t})}(D)
, where
Q(\bar{t})
is the Boolean CQ obtained after instantiating
\bar{x}
with
\bar{t}
.
Therefore, in the rest of the paper, by CQ or SJFCQ we silently refer to a Boolean query.

Example 6
.

Consider again the CQs
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
given in Example
4
. Consider also the tuples
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})
and
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})
. As mentioned in Example
5
, we have that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{1},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})}(D)=2
and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{2},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})}(D)=2
for the database
D
of Figure
1
and the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
. By instantiating the output variables of
Q_{1}
with the values of
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})
(i.e., replacing the variable
x
with the constant
\mathtt{16}
and the variable
y
with the constant
\mathtt{1030}
), we obtain the Boolean CQ

Q_{3}\ =\ \textrm{Ans}()\ \mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}\ \mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},w),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington}).

It is easy to see that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{1},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030})}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{3}}(D)
. Similarly, from the CQ
Q_{2}
and the tuple
(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})
we obtain the Boolean CQ

Q_{4}\ =\ \textrm{Ans}()\ \mathrel{{:}{\text{-}}}\ \mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420}),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})

by replacing the variable
x
with the constant
\mathtt{16}
, the variable
y
with the constant
\mathtt{1030}
, and the variable
w
with the constant
\mathtt{420}
. We then have that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{2},(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{4}}(D)
. Henceforth, in our examples, we will exploit the Boolean CQs
Q_{3}
and
Q_{4}
.

4
LHS Chain Functional Dependencies

We recall the class of FDs with a left-hand side chain together with some basic notions that will be useful for obtaining our main results.

Definition 1

(
LHS Chain FDs
,
[
23
]
)
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs over a schema
\mathbf{S}
and a relation name
R\in\mathbf{S}
. We say that
\Sigma_{R}
has a
left-hand side chain
(LHS chain, for short) if, for every two FDs
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1}
and
R:X_{2}\rightarrow Y_{2}
from
\Sigma_{R}
,
X_{1}\subseteq X_{2}
or
X_{2}\subseteq X_{1}
.
We further say that
\Sigma
has an LHS chain if, for every
R\in\mathbf{S}
,
\Sigma_{R}
has an LHS chain.

Note that the existence of an LHS chain means that the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
can be arranged in a sequence
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1},\ldots,R:X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
such that
X_{1}\subseteq X_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq X_{n}
; it is easy to verify that this sequence is not always unique. We call such a sequence an LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
.

Example 7
.

The set of FDs consisting of those given in Example
3
has an LHS chain since
\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\subseteq\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}
for the FDs over the relation name
\mathit{Schedule}
, and there is a single FD over the relation name
\mathit{Station}
(hence, this FD trivially forms an LHS chain). Moreover,

\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin},\,\,\,\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}

is an LHS chain of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
. If we had, in addition, the FD

\mathit{Station}\ :\ \mathrm{city}\rightarrow\mathrm{name}

that essentially states that every city has a single train station, then the FD set
\Sigma_{\mathit{Station}}
would not have an LHS chain since
\{\mathrm{name}\}\not\subseteq\{\mathrm{city}\}
and
\{\mathrm{city}\}\not\subseteq\{\mathrm{name}\}
.

Building Database Repairs.

When we focus on sets of FDs with an LHS chain, there is a convenient way to build database repairs, which is in turn the building block for showing that repairs can be counted in polynomial time in the size of the database
[
23
]
.
Given a database
D
, and an FD
\phi
of the form
R:X\rightarrow Y
, a
block
(resp.,
subblock
) of
D
w.r.t.
\phi
is a maximal subset
D^{\prime}
of
D_{R}
such that
f,g\in D^{\prime}
implies
f[A]=g[A]
for every
A\in X
(resp.,
A\in X\cup Y
). In simple words, a block (resp., subblock) of
D
w.r.t.
\phi
collects all the
R
-facts of
D
that agree on the attributes of
X
(resp.,
X\cup Y
). We write
\mathsf{block}_{\phi}(D)
and
\mathsf{sblock}_{\phi}(D)
for all the blocks and subblocks, respectively, of
D
w.r.t.
\phi
.
By exploiting the existence of an LHS chain, we can arrange blocks and sublocks in a rooted tree
[
22
]
, which will then guide the construction of database repairs.

Definition 2

(
Blocktree
)
.

Consider a database
D
over
\mathbf{S}
, and a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain over
\mathbf{S}
, and let
\Lambda=\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
be an LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
for some
R\in\mathbf{S}
. The
(R,\Lambda)
-blocktree
of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
is a labeled rooted tree
T=(V,E,\lambda)
of height
2n
(with the root being at level
0
), with
\lambda
being a function that assigns subsets of
D_{R}
to the nodes of
V
, such that the following hold:

•

If
v
is the root node, then
\lambda(v)=D_{R}
.

•

If
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
, for
k>0
, are the nodes at level
2i+1
for some
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
with parent
u
, then
|\mathsf{block}_{\phi_{i+1}}(\lambda(u))|=k
and
\mathsf{block}_{\phi_{i+1}}(\lambda(u))=\{\lambda(v_{1}),\ldots,\lambda(v_{k})\}
.

•

If
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
, for
k>0
, are the nodes at level
2i+2
for some
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
with parent
u
, then
|\mathsf{sblock}_{\phi_{i+1}}(\lambda(u))|=k
and
\mathsf{sblock}_{\phi_{i+1}}(\lambda(u))=\{\lambda(v_{1}),\ldots,\lambda(v_{k})\}
.

In simple words, each node of
T
is associated with a subset of
D_{R}
in a way that the root gets the database
D_{R}
itself, a node that occurs at an odd level
2i+1
, for
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
, gets a block of the database assigned to its parent w.r.t.
\phi_{i+1}
, and a node that occurs at an even level
2i+2
, for
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
, gets a subblock of the database assigned to its parent w.r.t.
\phi_{i+1}
.

It is straightforward to verify that, for every node
v
of an
(R,\Lambda)
-blocktree
T=(V,E,\lambda)
of a database
D
w.r.t. a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and for every two distinct children
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
of
v
, the following statements hold: (i) if
v
appears at an even level of
T
(including level
0
), then
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma_{R}
for every
f\in\lambda(u_{1})
and
g\in\lambda(u_{2})
, and (ii) if
v
appears at an odd level of
T
, then
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
for every
f\in\lambda(u_{1})
and
g\in\lambda(u_{2})
.
This easy observation leads to a simple recursive procedure, which operates on
T
, for constructing repairs of
D_{R}
w.r.t.
\Sigma_{R}
:

1.

Select the root node.

2.

For every selected node at an even level, select
all
of its children.

3.

For every selected node at an odd level, select
one
of its children.

4.

Output the union of the labels of the selected leaf nodes.

It should be clear that the obtained database is a repair of
D_{R}
w.r.t.
\Sigma_{R}
.
This is essentially the building block underlying the recursive procedure obtained from
[
23
]
for counting repairs in polynomial time in the size of the database:

Proposition 1

(
[
23
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence). Given a database
D
,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
is computable in polynomial time, and thus,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is in
FP
.

Let us clarify that blocktrees have not been used in
[
23
]
. Instead, a connection is established with the class of
P_{4}
-free graphs, which are also known as cographs. These graphs are characterized by the absence of a path of length four as an induced subgraph. This connection is made through the notion of conflict graph; the conflict graph of a database
D
with respect to a set
\Sigma
of FDs is a graph that has a node for each fact in the database, and there is an edge between two nodes if the corresponding facts jointly violate an FD of
\Sigma
. In
[
23
]
, it was shown that a set of FDs has an LHS chain (up to equivalence) if and only if the conflict graph of
every
database
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
is
P_{4}
-free. The notion of blocktree introduced above essentially corresponds to the cotree of the associated conflict graph; thus, these two representations of sets of FDs with an LHS chain are equivalent, and we consider blocktrees for technical convenience.

(\mathit{Schedule},\Lambda)
-blocktree of the database of Figure
1
w.r.t. the FD set of Example
3
.

Example 8
.

Figure
2
depicts the
(\mathit{Schedule},\Lambda)
-blocktree of the database
D
of Figure
1
w.r.t. the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
. Recall that the LHS chain
\Lambda
of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
is

\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin},\,\,\,\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}.

The root
r
is associated with the entire database
D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
(that is,
\lambda(r)=D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
). Since all the facts of
D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
agree on the values of the
\mathrm{train}
and
\mathrm{time}
attributes, there is a single block w.r.t. the first FD in the chain that contains all the facts; hence,
\lambda(v_{1})=D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
as well. Then, the node
v_{4}
, for example, is associated with all the facts
f
with
f[\mathrm{origin}]=\mathtt{BBY}
; hence,
\lambda(v_{4})=\{f_{6},f_{7},f_{8}\}
. This set of facts is a subblock of
D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
w.r.t. the FD
\mathit{Schedule}:\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\{\mathrm{origin}\}
. The node
v_{10}
collects all the facts
f\in\lambda(v_{4})
with
f[\mathrm{duration}]=\mathtt{260}
; thus,
\lambda(v_{4})=\{f_{6},f_{7}\}
. Here,
\{f_{6},f_{7}\}
is a block of
\lambda(v_{4})
w.r.t. the FD
\mathit{Schedule}:\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\{\mathrm{destination}\}
as these facts agree on the values of the attributes
\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}
, and
\mathrm{duration}
, while the fact
f_{8}
has a different value for the attribute
\mathrm{duration}
and belongs to a different block.

The Case of Primary Keys.

Before we proceed further, let us focus for a moment on the simpler case of primary keys, and briefly comment on how database repairs are constructed via the machinery described above. Given a database
D
and a set
\Sigma
of primary keys, by definition,
\Sigma_{R}
consists of a single
FD
\phi_{R}
of the form
R:X\rightarrow Y
with
X\cup Y=\mathsf{att}(R)
.
This means that the
(R,\Lambda)
-blocktree
T=(V,E,\lambda)
of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma_{R}
, where
\Lambda
is the trivial LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
mentioning only
\phi_{R}
, is of height two, and, for each leaf node
v
,
\lambda(v)
contains a single
R
-fact. This implies that a repair of
D_{R}
w.r.t.
\Sigma_{R}
can be constructed by simply selecting, for each node at level one of
T
, one of its children (which are leaf nodes), and then collect the
R
-facts that label the selected leaf nodes.
This illustrates that the underlying structure of database repairs in the case of primary keys is significantly simpler compared to that of repairs in the case of FDs with an LHS chain.

Let us further note that the case of primary keys is closely related to the block-independent-disjoint probabilistic databases
[
9
,
10
]
, where the repairs coincide with the possible worlds of the probabilistic database (see
[
25
]
for further details). However, as discussed in
[
25
]
, these are different models that require different techniques. Moreover, it remains unclear how the block-independent-disjoint probabilistic model can be adapted to align with the case of an LHS chain.

5
Exact Counting

We now concentrate on exact counting and show that Theorem
1
can be extended to arbitrary FDs. In particular, we establish the following classification:

Theorem 2
.

For a set
\Sigma
of FDs and an SJFCQ
Q
, the following statements hold:

1.

\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is either in
FP
or
\sharp
P
-complete.

2.

We can decide in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
whether
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
or
\sharp
P
-complete.

We start by observing that, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs and an SJFCQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
\sharp
P
: guess a subset
D^{\prime}
of the given database
D
, and verify that
D^{\prime}
is a repair of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
. Since both the guess and the verify steps are feasible in polynomial time in
||D||
, we conclude that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
\sharp
P
.
We further observe that the non-existence of an LHS chain (up to equivalence) is a preliminary boundary for the hard side of the target dichotomy, no matter how the CQ looks like. We know from
[
23
]
that, for a set
\Sigma
of FDs,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is
\sharp
P-hard if
\Sigma
does not have an LHS chain (up to equivalence). From the above discussion and Lemma
1
, we get that:

Proposition 2
.

For a set
\Sigma
of FDs without an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and an SJFCQ
Q
, it holds that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is
\sharp
P
-complete.

From Proposition
2
, and the fact that checking whether a set
\Sigma
of FDs has an LHS chain (up to equivalence) is feasible in polynomial time
[
23
]
, we conclude that to obtain Theorem
2
for FDs, it suffices to provide an analogous result for FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence).
To this end, following what was done for primary keys in
[
25
]
, we are going to concentrate on the problem of computing the relative frequency of the query. The
relative frequency
of a CQ
Q
w.r.t. a database
D
and a set
\Sigma
of FDs is the ratio that computes the percentage of repairs that entail it, i.e.,

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

We then consider the following problem for a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q
:

PROBLEM
:

\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)

INPUT
:

A database
D

OUTPUT
:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)

We can indeed focus on
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
since we know from Proposition
1
that the problem of computing
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
is feasible in polynomial time in
||D||
whenever
\Sigma
has an LHS chain (up to equivalence), which implies that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
and
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
have the same complexity, that is, both are either in
FP
or
\sharp\text{P}
-hard. Hence, to obtain Theorem
2
it suffices to show the following:

Theorem 3
.

For a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and an SJFCQ
Q
, the following statements hold:

1.

\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is either in
FP
or
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hard.

2.

We can decide in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
whether
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
or
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hard.

To establish the above dichotomy result, we first introduce the central notion of safety for an SJFCQ
Q
w.r.t. a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, inspired by a similar notion for the case of primary keys from
[
25
]
, and show that the problem of deciding whether
Q
is safe w.r.t.
\Sigma
is feasible in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
(see Theorem
4
below).
We then proceed to show that safety exhausts the tractable side of the dichotomy (item (1)) stated in Theorem
3
.
In particular, we show that if
Q
is safe w.r.t.
\Sigma
, then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
(see Theorem
5
below), and if
Q
is not safe w.r.t.
\Sigma
, then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hard (see Theorem
6
below).
The rest of this section is devoted to introducing the notion of safety for an SJFCQ w.r.t. a set of FDs, and establishing Theorems
4
,
5
, and
6
, which will imply Theorem
3
.
We start by introducing some auxiliary notions.

5.1
Auxiliary Notions

Canonical Covers.

A set
\Sigma
of FDs is called
canonical
if (i) it is minimal in the sense that it does not contain redundant FDs (i.e., FDs
\phi
such that every database
D
that satisfies
\Sigma\setminus\{\phi\}
also satisfies
\phi
) and its FDs do not mention redundant attributes (i.e., there is no FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
with some
B\in X
such that every database
D
that satisfies
\Sigma
also satisfies
R:(X\setminus\{B\})\rightarrow Y
), and (ii) every FD of
\Sigma
is of the form
R:X\rightarrow A
for some attribute
A\in\mathsf{att}(R)
(i.e., it has a single attribute on its right-hand side); for further details we refer the reader to
[
24
]
.

A
canonical cover
of a set
\Sigma
of FDs is a canonical set of FDs that is equivalent to
\Sigma
. Here is a simple example that illustrates this technical notion.

Example 9
.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
be the set of FDs consisting of

\displaystyle\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin}

\displaystyle\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}

\displaystyle\phi_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin}.

Observe that
\phi_{3}
is redundant since every database
D
with
D\models\Sigma_{1}\setminus\{\phi_{3}\}
satisfies
\phi_{1}
; hence, it clearly also satisfies
\phi_{3}
.
Now, let
\Sigma_{2}
be the set of FDs
\{\phi_{1},\phi_{4}\}
, where

\phi_{4}\ =\ \mathit{Schedule}\ :\ \{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration},\mathrm{origin}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}.

Here, the FD
\phi_{4}
has a redundant attribute (that is,
\mathrm{origin}
) since the FD
\phi_{1}
implies that every two facts
f_{1},f_{2}
that agree on the values of the attributes
\mathrm{train}
and
\mathrm{time}
also agree on the value of
\mathrm{origin}
. Hence, if two facts
f_{1},f_{2}
jointly satisfy the FDs of
\Sigma_{2}
, then they also satisfy the FD
\phi_{2}
. In particular, due to
\phi_{1}
, it cannot be the case that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
disagree on the value of
\mathrm{origin}
(hence, jointly satisfy
\phi_{4}
) while they agree on the values of
\mathrm{train}
and
\mathrm{time}
(hence, might violate the FD
\phi_{2}
). We conclude that for every database
D
such that
D\models\Sigma_{2}
it holds that
D\models\{\phi_{2}\}
.
From the above discussion, we get that the set of FDs
\Sigma
of Example
3
is a canonical cover of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.

Assuming that a set
\Sigma
of FDs has an LHS chain (up to equivalence), we know that it has a single canonical cover
\Sigma^{\prime}
that has an LHS chain
[
23
]
. Furthermore, for the canonical cover
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
, it can be verified that, for every relation name
R
occurring in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, there exists a unique sequence
R:X_{1}\rightarrow A_{1},\ldots,R:X_{n}\rightarrow A_{n}
of the FDs of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R}
, which we call
the LHS chain
of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R}
, such that (i)
X_{i}\subsetneq X_{i+1}
for each
i\in[n-1]
, (ii)
X_{i}\cap Y_{j}=\emptyset
for each
i,j\in[n]
, and (iii)
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}=\emptyset
for each
i,j\in[n]
with
i\neq j
.
Since a canonical cover of a set of FDs can be computed in polynomial time, to establish Theorem
3
it suffices to concentrate on sets of FDs with an LHS chain that are canonical.
In what follows, for convenience, we group together all the FDs of a canonical FD set
\Sigma
that share the same left-hand side. In other words, if
R:X\rightarrow A_{1},\dots,R:X\rightarrow A_{n}
are all the FDs of
\Sigma
with
X
on their left-hand side, we replace these FDs with the single FD
R:X\rightarrow\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
. Clearly, this has no impact on the satisfaction of the FDs or the LHS chain.

Primary FDs and Positions.

Consider now an SJFCQ
Q
. Let
\alpha_{R}
be the
R
-atom in
Q
, and let
\Lambda_{R}=R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1},\ldots,R:X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
be the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
. We call an FD
R:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
, for some
i\in[n]
, the
primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
if
X_{i}\cup Y_{i}
contains an attribute
A
such that at position
(R,A)
in
\alpha_{R}
we have a variable, whereas at each position of
\{(R,B)\mid B\in X_{j}\cup Y_{j}\text{ for }j<i\}
in
\alpha_{R}
we have a constant.
Note that there is no guarantee that the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
exists.
If the primary FD
R:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
exists, for some
i\in[n]
, then the
primary-lhs positions of
\alpha_{R}
(w.r.t.
\Sigma
)
are the positions
\{(R,A)\mid A\in X_{i}\}
, while the
non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha_{R}
(w.r.t.
\Sigma
)
are the positions
\{(R,B)\mid B\in\mathsf{att}(R)\setminus X_{i}\}
. We further call the sequence of FDs
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1},\ldots,R:X_{i-1}\rightarrow Y_{i-1}
the
primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
.
If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
does not exist, then, by convention, the primary-lhs positions of
\alpha_{R}
are the positions
\{(R,A)\mid A\in\mathsf{att}(R)\}
, i.e., all the positions in
\alpha_{R}
are primary-lhs, which in turn implies that
\alpha_{R}
has no non-primary-lhs positions. Moreover, the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
is the sequence
\Lambda_{R}
itself.
We denote by
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha_{R})
the set of variables in
\alpha_{R}
at primary-lhs positions of
\alpha_{R}
.

Example 10
.

Consider the FDs
\Sigma
of Example
3
and the CQs
Q_{3},Q_{4}
of Example
6
. Recall that

\phi_{1}=\mathit{Schedule}:\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin},\,\,\,\,\,\phi_{2}=\mathit{Schedule}:\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time},\mathrm{duration}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{destination}

is the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
and

\phi_{3}\ =\ \mathit{Station}\ :\ \mathrm{name}\rightarrow\mathrm{city}

is the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Station}}
. In both
Q_{3}
and
Q_{4}
, all the attributes of the FD
\phi_{1}
are associated with constants (
\mathtt{16},\mathtt{1030}
, and
\mathtt{BBY}
, respectively). The attribute
\mathrm{destination}
, on the other hand, is associated with the variable
z
in both queries. Hence, we have that
\phi_{2}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
, and
\{\phi_{1}\}
is the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
.
The primary-lhs positions
of the atom over
\mathit{Schedule}
in both queries are the first, fourth, and fifth positions, corresponding to the attributes
\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}
, and
\mathrm{duration}
, respectively, that occur on the left-hand side of the primary FD.
As for the relation name
\mathit{Station}
, in both queries the attribute
\mathrm{name}
is associated with the variable
z
; hence,
\phi_{3}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Station}}
, and its primary prefix is empty. There is a single primary-lhs position
of the atom over
\mathit{Station}
in both queries, that is, the first position, corresponding to the attribute
\mathrm{name}
that occurs on the left-hand side of the primary FD.

Query Complex Part.

A variable
x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)
is a
liaison variable (of
Q
)
if it occurs more than once in
Q
. The
complex part of
Q
w.r.t.
\Sigma
, denoted
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, is the set of atoms
\alpha
of
Q
in which we have a constant or a liaison variable at a non-primary-lhs position
(R,A)
, with
\alpha
being the
R
-atom of
Q
, such that, for every FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
,
A\not\in X\cup Y
.

Example 11
.

Consider again the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
and the CQ
Q_{3}
of Example
6
. The variable
z
is a liaison variable of
Q_{3}
as it occurs in both atoms of the query. As discussed in Example
10
, the primary-lhs positions of the atom
\mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},w)
in
Q_{3}
are the ones corresponding to the attributes
\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}
, and
\mathrm{duration}
. The attribute
\mathrm{origin}
occurs in the primary prefix, and so the only non-primary-lhs position associated with an attribute that does not occur in the primary prefix is the one corresponding to the attribute
\mathrm{destination}
. This position is associated with the liaison variable
z
; hence, this atom belongs to complex part. The only non-primary-lhs position of the atom
\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})
is the one corresponding to the attribute
\mathrm{city}
and it is associated with a constant; hence, this atom also belongs to the complex part of
Q_{3}
. We can similarly show that both atoms of
Q_{4}
belong to its complex part.

Interestingly, whenever
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, we can count the number of repairs that entail
Q
in polynomial time in the size of the database. This is shown by first observing that the number of repairs of a database
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entail
Q
coincides with the number of repairs of the subset of
D
obtained after removing the facts of
D
that are somehow in a conflict with
Q
, and thus, they cannot appear in a repair that entails
Q
.
Let
R
be a relation name in
Q
with
\alpha_{R}
being the
R
-atom of
Q
. We define the database
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma_{R},Q}
as the subset of
D
that collects all the
R
-facts
f
of
D
such that, for some FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
,
f[A]=\alpha_{R}[A]
for each
A\in X
, and
f[B]\neq\alpha_{R}[B]
for some
B\in Y
.
Now, assuming that
R_{1},\ldots,R_{m}
are the relation names in
Q
, we define the database

D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\ =\ \bigcup_{R\in\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{m}\}}D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma_{R},Q}.

For a database
D
with
D\models Q
,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
allows us to show that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}\left(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\right)
.

Since
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
can be constructed in polynomial time in
||D||
, Proposition
1
implies the following (the full proof can be found in the appendix):

Proposition 3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of FDs, and
Q
an SJFCQ with
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
. Given a database
D
, it holds that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is computable in polynomial time.

Input:

A set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q

Output:

\mathsf{true}
if
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe and
\mathsf{false}
otherwise

1

if

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset

then

2

return

\mathsf{true}

3

if

Q=Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2}

then

4

return

\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{1})\wedge\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{2})

5

if

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
,
and there is a variable

x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)

with

x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)

for every

\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)

then

6

return

\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
for an arbitrary constant
c

7

if

there is

\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)

of the form

R(\bar{y})

such that

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset

and the variable

x

occurs in

\alpha

at a position of

\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}

with

R:X\rightarrow Y

being the primary FD of

\Sigma_{R}

w.r.t.

Q

then

8

return

\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
for an arbitrary constant
c

9

return

\mathsf{false}

10

Algorithm 1

The recursive procedure
\mathsf{IsSafe}

Example 12
.

Consider the database
D
of Figure
1
, the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
, and the CQ

Q_{5}\ =\ \textrm{Ans}()\ \text{:-}\ \mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},w)

asking whether train
16
departs from the BBY station at 10:30. Note that the single atom of
Q_{5}
also occurs in
Q_{3}
, and, as mentioned in Example
10
, the only non-primary-lhs position of this atom is the one associated with the variable
z
. In this case, the variable
z
is not a liaison variable as it only occurs once in
Q_{5}
; hence,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q_{5})=\emptyset
. Thus, by Proposition
3
,
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{5})
is computable in polynomial time. Note that every repair that entails
Q_{5}
must contain at least one of the facts
f_{6},f_{7}
, or
f_{8}
, as these are the only facts mentioning trains leaving the BBY station. All the other facts of
D_{\mathit{Schedule}}
are in conflict with each one of these facts due to the FD
\mathit{Schedule}:\{\mathrm{train},\mathrm{time}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{origin}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
. Each one of these facts disagrees with the atom of
Q_{5}
on the value of the attribute
\mathrm{origin}
; hence, they will all be collected in the database
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}
.

5.2
Query Safety

We now proceed to introduce the central notion of safety for an SJFCQ
Q
w.r.t. a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain.
This notion essentially tells us that after simplifying
Q
into basic subqueries by recursively applying three simplification steps, each such basic subquery has an empty complex part w.r.t.
\Sigma
.
We write
Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2}
for the CQ consisting of the atoms of two non-empty CQs
Q_{1},Q_{2}
that share no atoms and variables, i.e.,
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset
.
We also write
Q_{x\mapsto t}
, where
x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)
and
t\in\mathbf{C}\cup\mathbf{V}
, for the CQ obtained from
Q
after replacing
x
with
t
.

Definition 3

(
Query Safety
)
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. We inductively define when
Q
is
safe w.r.t.
\Sigma
, or simply
\Sigma
-safe
, as follows:

-

If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.

-

If
Q=Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2}
and
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are
\Sigma
-safe, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.
(Note that such a decomposition is not necessarily unique, and we can choose an arbitrary one.)

-

If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, there exists a variable
x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)
such that
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, and
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe for some arbitrary constant
c\in\mathbf{C}
, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.

-

If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, there is an
R
-atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
such that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and a variable
x
occurs in
\alpha
at a position of
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}
assuming that
R:X\rightarrow Y
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, and
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe for some arbitrary constant
c\in\mathbf{C}
, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.

Example 13
.

As mentioned in Example
11
, we have that

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q_{3})\ =\ \{\mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},w),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})\}

for the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
and the query
Q_{3}
of Example
6
. Hence, the first condition of Definition
3
does not apply to
Q_{3}
. The second condition also does not apply to
Q_{3}
, as the two atoms of the query share the variable
z
. The only variable of
\mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},w)
associated with a primary-lhs position is
w
and the only variable of
\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})
that occurs at a primary-lhs position is
z
; hence, the third condition does not hold. This also implies that the last condition does not hold, since both atoms use a variable at a primary-lhs position. Therefore, we conclude that the query
Q_{3}
is not
\Sigma
-safe.

For the CQ
Q_{4}
of Example
6
we have that

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q_{4})\ =\ \{\mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420}),\mathit{Station}(z,\mathtt{Washington})\}.

Here, we can apply the last condition of Definition
3
because there are no variables at primary-lhs positions of
\mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{16},\mathtt{BBY},z,\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{420})
, and the variable
z
occurs at a position corresponding to the attribute
\mathrm{destination}
that occurs on the right-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
w.r.t.
Q_{4}
. After replacing the variable
z
with some constant, we obtain a CQ
Q_{6}
with no variables. Thus, both
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathit{Station}}
have no primary FD and no non-primary-lhs positions w.r.t.
Q_{6}
. Hence,
Q_{6}
has an empty complex part and so it is
\Sigma
-safe. We then conclude that
Q_{4}
is
\Sigma
-safe.

We proceed to show that query safety can be checked in polynomial time.

Theorem 4
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q
. The problem of deciding whether
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe is feasible in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
.

Proof.

It is easy to verify that the recursive procedure
\mathsf{IsSafe}
(depicted in Algorithm
1
), which accepts as input
\Sigma
and
Q
, and simply implements Definition
3
, it correctly determines whether
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe. Thus, it remains to show that
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
runs in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
.
To this end, we show via an inductive argument that
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
requires polynomially many recursive calls in
||Q||
, which, together with the fact that all the conditions of the if-then statements can be checked in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
, implies that
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
runs in polynomial time in
||\Sigma||+||Q||
, as needed.
We proceed by induction on the value
n=|Q|+m
, where
|Q|
is the number of atoms in
Q
and
m
is the number of variables in
Q
. We show that the total number of recursive calls in an execution of
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
is bounded by
2|Q|-1+m
. Note that the proof is similar to the corresponding proof for primary keys from
[
25
]
.

Base Case.

The base case of the induction (for
n=1
) is the case where
Q
has a single atom
\alpha
and no variables. In this case, all the positions of
\alpha
are primary-lhs positions and
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
. Therefore, the algorithm is called once, and the condition in line 1 is satisfied. Here,
2|Q|-1+m=2\cdot 1-1+0=1
, and indeed there is a single call to
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Inductive Step.

We now assume that the claim holds for
|Q|+m=n
, and we show that it also holds for
|Q|+m=n+1
. If none of the if-conditions is satisfied by
Q
, then there is a single call to the algorithm (that returns
\mathsf{false}
) and the claim follows. Otherwise, one of the following holds:

1.

The if-condition in line 1 is satisfied.
In this case, this is again a single call to
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q)
and clearly
2|Q|-1+m>1
.

2.

The if-condition in line 3 is satisfied.
In this case, the number of recursive calls is
1+n_{1}+n_{2}
, where
n_{1}
is the number of calls in an execution of
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{1})
and
n_{2}
is the number of calls in an execution of
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{2})
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have that
n_{1}\leq 2|Q_{1}|-1+m_{1}
and
n_{2}\leq 2|Q_{2}|-1+m_{2}
, where
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are the number of variables in
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, respectively. Since
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
have no shared atoms or variables, it holds that
|Q|=|Q_{1}|+|Q_{2}|
and
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
. Hence,
1+n_{1}+n_{2}\leq 1+2|Q_{1}|-1+m_{1}+2|Q_{2}|-1+m_{2}=1+2|Q|-2+m=2|Q|-1+m
.

3.

The condition in line 5 or the if-condition in line 7 is satisfied.
In this case, the number of recursive calls is
1+n_{c}
, where
n_{c}
is the number of calls in an execution of
\mathsf{IsSafe}(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
for some arbitrary constant
c
. Since the query
Q_{x\mapsto c}
has one less variable and the same number of atoms, by the inductive hypothesis, the number of calls in this case is
1+n_{c}\leq 1+2|Q_{x\mapsto c}|-1+(m-1)=2|Q|-1+m
.

This completes our proof.

5.3
The Tractable Side

We are now ready to discuss the tractable side of the dichotomy stated in Theorem
3
. In particular, we show that safety ensures that the relative frequency can be computed in polynomial time:

Theorem 5
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe, then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is in
FP
.

The above result relies on a recursive procedure, called
\mathsf{Eval}
, that takes as input a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and a
\Sigma
-safe SJFCQ
Q
, runs in polynomial time in
||D||
, and computes the value
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
. We proceed to discuss the details of this procedure.

Three Auxiliary Lemmas.

\mathsf{Eval}
exploits three auxiliary technical lemmas, which we present next (their proofs are deferred to the appendix), that essentially tell us how the relative frequency of an SJFCQ can be computed via the relative frequency of a simpler query obtained by applying one of the three simplification steps underlying the notion of query safety.

The first one, which deals with SJFCQs that are the disjoint union of two other queries, is an easy observation directly inherited from
[
25
]
since the given proof, which we recall below for the sake of completeness, works also for arbitrary FDs.

Lemma 2
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs, and an SJFCQ
Q=Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2}
. Then

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{1})\times\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{2}).

Proof.

We consider the following three subsets of
D
:

•

D_{0}
collects every fact of
D
over a relation name that does not occur in
Q
.

•

D_{1}
collects every fact of
D
over a relation name that occurs in
Q_{1}
.

•

D_{2}
collects every fact of
D
over a relation name that occurs in
Q_{2}
.

Since
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
do not share any atoms or variables, it is rather straightforward to see that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{0})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{1})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{2})\quad

and

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{0})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{1}}(D_{1})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{2}}(D_{2}).

Therefore, we have that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D_{1},\Sigma}(Q_{1})\times\mathsf{rfreq}_{D_{2},\Sigma}(Q_{2})
, as needed.

The second auxiliary lemma deals with SJFCQs with a non-empty complex part that mentions a variable that occurs in every atom of the complex part at a primary-lhs position.
To this end, given a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
, we need to collect the facts of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
that do not affect the relative frequency of
Q
w.r.t.
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
\Sigma
.
Let
R
be a relation name in
Q
with
\alpha_{R}
being the
R
-atom of
Q
. We define
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma_{R},Q}
as the subset of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma_{R},Q}
that collects all the
R
-facts
f
of
D
such that, for some FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, and attribute
A\in X
,
f[A]\neq\alpha_{R}[A]
.
Now, assuming that
R_{1},\ldots,R_{m}
are the relation names in
Q
, we define the database

D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}\ =\ \bigcup_{R\in\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{m}\}}D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma_{R},Q}.

Example 14
.

Recall that the database
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}
for the FD set
\Sigma
of Example
3
and the query
Q_{5}
of Example
12
contains all the facts over the relation name
\mathit{Schedule}
except
f_{6},f_{7}
, and
f_{8}
; that is,
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}=\{f_{6},f_{7},f_{8},g_{1},\ldots,g_{7}\}
. Now, assume that the database contains also the fact

f_{10}\ =\ \mathit{Schedule}(\mathtt{17},\mathtt{BBY},\mathtt{NYP},\mathtt{1030},\mathtt{400}),

which mentions train
\mathtt{17}
. This fact does not belong to
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}
as it disagrees with the query on the constant associated with the attribute
\mathrm{train}
that occurs on the left-hand side of the only FD in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
w.r.t.
Q_{5}
; however, due to this reason, it does belong to the database
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}
. Since the query collects information about train number
\mathtt{16}
, there is no homomorphism from
Q_{5}
to
D
that maps the only atom of the query to the fact
f_{10}
that mentions train number
\mathtt{17}
. Moreover, the fact
f_{10}
is not in conflict with any of the facts
f_{6},f_{7}
, or
f_{8}
that are required to entail the query, as they disagree on the value of the attribute
\mathrm{train}
that occurs on the left-hand side of every FD in
\Sigma_{\mathit{Schedule}}
(as this FD set has an LHS chain). Therefore,
as we will show next
, this fact has no impact on the relative frequency of
Q_{5}
w.r.t.
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{5}}
and
\Sigma
.

With
D^{\Sigma,Q}=D\setminus\left(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}\right)
, we show in the appendix that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\ \times\ \underbrace{\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}\left(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\right)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}},

which tells us that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is the product of
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)
with the ratio
\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}
.
It is not immediate how to compute
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)
in polynomial time in
||D||
, while
\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}
is clearly computable in polynomial time in
||D||
due to Proposition
1
.

The next lemma is shown using the above equation by providing a way to compute
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)
via the relative frequency of a simplified version of
Q
w.r.t.
D^{\Sigma,Q}
and
\Sigma
.

Lemma 3
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, and there is
x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)
such that
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, then

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \left(1-\prod_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\left(1-\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\right)\right)\ \times\ \mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}.

The last auxiliary lemma considers SJFCQs with at least one atom in their complex part that does not have a variable at a primary-lhs position, but has a variable at a position associated with an attribute occurring in the right-hand side of the primary FD.

Lemma 4
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If there exists an
R
-atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
such that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and a variable
x
occurs in
\alpha
at a position of
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}
with
R:X\rightarrow Y
being the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, then

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c}).

Input:

A database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and a
\Sigma
-safe SJFCQ
Q

Output:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)

1

if

D\not\models Q

then

2

return

0
if

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset

then

3

return

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
if

Q=Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2}

then

4

return

\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{1})\times\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{2})

5

if

\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
,
and there is a variable

x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)

with

x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)

for every

\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)

then

6

return
