\left(1-\prod_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\left(1-\mathsf{Eval}\left(D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}\right)\right)\right)\times\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}

7

if

there exists an

R
-atom

\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)

such that

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset

and a variable

x

occurs in

\alpha

at a position of

\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}

with

R:X\rightarrow Y

being the primary FD of

\Sigma_{R}

w.r.t.

Q

then

8

return

\sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})

9

Algorithm 2

The recursive procedure
\mathsf{Eval}

The Recursive Procedure.

We are now ready to introduced the procedure
\mathsf{Eval}
that generalizes the corresponding procedure for primary keys
[
25
]
.

\mathsf{Eval}
, depicted in Algorithm
2
, accepts as input a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and a
\Sigma
-safe SJFCQ
Q
, and performs the following steps. It first checks if
D\not\models Q
, in which case it returns
0
. It then checks if
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, in which case it returns
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
. Then, it essentially implements the three auxiliary lemmas from above by checking which condition holds, and computing the relative frequency of the simplified queries via recursive calls.
We proceed to show that
\mathsf{Eval}
is indeed correct, i.e., it computes the value
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
, and runs in polynomial time in
||D||
.

Proposition 4
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and a
\Sigma
-safe SJFCQ
Q
. The following hold:

1.

\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
.

2.

\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)
terminates in polynomial time in
||D||
.

Proof.

Item (1) can be easily shown using
Proposition
3
and Lemmas
2
,
3
and
4
. The interesting task is to show item (2). The proof is along the lines of the corresponding proof for primary keys
[
25
]
as the structure of the algorithms is similar. However, the computations in the algorithms are different.
Let
|Q|
be the number of atoms in
Q
, and
m
be the number of variables that occur in
Q
. We prove, by induction on the value
n=|Q|+m
, that the total number of recursive calls in an execution of
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)
is bounded by
\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}
, where
N=|\mathsf{dom}(D)|
(which is bounded by
||D||
).

Base Case.

The base case of the induction (i.e., for
n=1
) is the case where
Q
has a single atom and no variables.
Thus, the algorithm is called once, and either the if-condition in line 1 (that is,
D\not\models Q
) or the if-condition in line 3 (that is,
Q
has no complex part) is triggered
. Here,
\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}=\frac{N-1}{N-1}=1
, and indeed there is a single call to
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)
.

Inductive Step.

We now assume that the claim holds for
|Q|+m=n
, and show that it also holds for
|Q|+m=n+1
. One of the following holds:

1.

The if-condition in line 1 or the if-condition in line 3 is satisfied.
In this case, there is a single call to
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)
and clearly
\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}\geq 1
.

2.

The if-condition in line 5 is satisfied.
In this case, the number of recursive calls is
1+n_{1}+n_{2}
, where
n_{1}
is the number of recursive calls in an execution of
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{1})
and
n_{2}
is the number of calls in an execution of
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{2})
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have that
n_{1}\leq\frac{(2|Q_{1}|-1)(N^{m_{1}+1}-1)}{N-1}
and
n_{2}\leq\frac{(2|Q_{1}|-1)(N^{m_{2}+1}-1)}{N-1}
, where
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are the number of variables in
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, respectively. Since
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
do not share atoms or variables, we have that
|Q|=|Q_{1}|+|Q_{2}|
and
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle 1+n_{1}+n_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+\frac{(2|Q_{1}|-1)(N^{m_{1}+1}-1)}{N-1}+\frac{(2|Q_{2}|-1)(N^{m_{2}+1}-1)}{N-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{N-1}{N-1}+\frac{(2|Q_{1}|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)+(2|Q_{2}|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{N^{m+1}-1}{N-1}+\frac{(2|Q|-2)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}.

3.

The if-condition in line 7 or the if-condition in line 9 is satisfied.
In this case, the number of recursive calls is
1+N\cdot n_{c}
, where
n_{c}
is the number of calls in an execution of
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
for some arbitrary constant
c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
. Since the query
Q_{x\mapsto c}
has one less variable and the same number of atoms, by the inductive hypothesis, the number of calls in this case is:

\displaystyle 1+N\cdot n_{c}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+N\cdot\left(\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m}-1)}{N-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N-1+N\cdot(2|Q|-1)(N^{m}-1)}{N-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N-1)+(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-N)}{N-1}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=}

\displaystyle\frac{(2|Q|-1)(N^{m+1}-1)}{N-1}.

We conclude that the number of recursive calls is polynomial in
||D||
. An easy observation is that all the if-conditions used in
\mathsf{Eval}
can be tested in polynomial time in
||D||
.
Moreover, Proposition
3
implies that if
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, then
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
can be computed in polynomial time in
||D||
.
Finally, since the FD set
\Sigma
has an LHS chain, by Proposition
1
, the number of repairs of a given database
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
can be computed in polynomial time in
||D||
; hence,
\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}
can be computed in polynomial time in
||D||
. Therefore,
\mathsf{Eval}(D,\Sigma,Q)
runs in polynomial in
||D||
.

It is clear that Theorem
5
immediately follows from Proposition
4
.

5.4
The Intractable Side

We now proceed to discuss the intractable side of the dichotomy stated in Theorem
3
. In particular, we show that non-safety implies that computing the relative frequency is an intractable problem:

Theorem 6
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is
\sharp
P
-hard.

We know from
[
25
]
that, for every set
\Sigma
of primary keys, and an SJFCQ
Q
that is not
\Sigma
-safe,
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-hard. Hence, to establish Theorem
6
, it suffices to show the following:

Theorem 7
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
that is not
\Sigma
-safe. There exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys, and an SJFCQ
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

The rest of this section is devoted to discussing the rather involved proof of Theorem
7
. This proof consists of the following three main steps:

1.

We first define certain rewrite rules for pairs consisting of a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q
. We then use those rules to define when a pair
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, namely
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe and
(\Sigma,Q)
cannot be rewritten into some other pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
with
Q^{\prime}
being an SJFCQ that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe. We then observe that from each pair
(\Sigma,Q)
where
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, we can reach a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
that is final via a sequence of rewritings. Note that the rewrite rules in question are based on the corresponding ones for primary keys from
[
25
]
.

2.

We then show that whenever a pair
(\Sigma,Q)
can be rewritten into a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, then there is a polynomial-time
Cook reduction
from
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

3.

Finally, we show that, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, there exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys and a query
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Having the above notions and results in place, we can then establish Theorem
7
as follows. According to step (1), from
(\Sigma,Q)
we can reach a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
that is final via a sequence of rewritings. Thus, due to step (2) and the fact that Cook reductions compose, we get that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
. Then, according to step (3), there exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys and a query
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. Thus, by composing reductions, we get that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
, as needed. We proceed to give details for each of the above three steps.

R1:

(\Sigma,Q)

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})

if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with

x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)

R2:

(\Sigma,Q_{1}\cup Q_{2})

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{1})

if
Q_{1}\neq\emptyset
,
Q_{2}\neq\emptyset
and

\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset

R3:

(\Sigma,Q)

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x})

if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with

x,y\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)

R4:

(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha\}})

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q)

if
(\mathsf{var}(Q)\cap\mathsf{var}(\alpha))\subseteq\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)

R5:

(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha_{1}\}})

\triangleright\,((\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{R})\cup\Sigma_{R^{\prime}},Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha_{2}\}})

if
\alpha_{1}>_{\Sigma}\alpha_{2}
and the relation name of

\alpha_{2}
does not occur in
Q

R6:

(\Sigma,Q)

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})

if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and
x\in\mathsf{prhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
,

and one of the variables of
\mathsf{var}(\alpha)

is a liaison variable of
Q

R7:

(\Sigma,Q)

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x})

if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with

x,y\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
, none of
x,y
is an

orphan variable, and
x
or
y
(or both)

is a liaison variable of
Q
that occurs

at some non-primary-lhs

position of
\alpha

R8:

(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}})

\triangleright\,((\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{R})\cup\Sigma_{R^{\prime}},Q\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}})

if
x
is an orphan variable of

Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}
, and
R^{\prime}
does not

occur in
Q

R9:

(\Sigma,Q)

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q^{\prime})

if a constant
c
occurs in
Q
,

some constant
c^{\prime}\neq c
occurs in
Q
,

and
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
Q
by

replacing all constants with
c

R10:

(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}})

\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}})

if
x
occurs at a non-primary-lhs

position and has another

occurrence at a primary-lhs

position of
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})

Figure 3:
Rewrite rules for pairs consisting of an FD set
\Sigma
with an LHS chain and an SJFCQs
Q
.

Rewrite Rules and Final Pairs.

Maslowski and Wijsen defined ten rewrite rules for CQs under primary keys
[
25
]
. In these rules, only the CQ is rewritten as the primary keys are implicitly encoded in the query. We generalize these rewrite rules for FDs with an LHS chain by rewriting pairs consisting of a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q
.
Our rules are depicted in Figure
3
. We write
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
for the fact that
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
can be obtained from
(\Sigma,Q)
by applying one of the rewrite rules. We also write
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
for the fact that there is finite sequence of pairs
s=(\Sigma_{0},Q_{0}),\ldots,(\Sigma_{n},Q_{n})
, where
(\Sigma,Q)=(\Sigma_{0},Q_{0})
and
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=(\Sigma_{n},Q_{n})
, such that
(\Sigma_{i},Q_{i})\triangleright(\Sigma_{i+1},Q_{i+1})
for each
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
; we may say that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is witnessed by
s
, and call
s
a rewriting sequence of
(\Sigma,Q)
.
Here are some further notes about the rewrite rules:

The Relation
>_{\Sigma}
.

It is a binary relation over atoms used in the rule R5 defined as follows:

1.

R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})>_{\Sigma}R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
if
x
has two or more occurrences in
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
, and

2.

R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})>_{\Sigma}R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
.

Note that
>_{\Sigma}
is relative to a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain. However, since
\Sigma
is always clear from the context, we will simply write
>
without explicitly referring to a set of FDs.

Primary-rhs Positions.

In the rule R6, we use the notation
\mathsf{prhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
to denote the set of variables occurring in
\alpha
at the positions
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y_{i}\}
, where
R
is the relation name of
\alpha
and
R:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
.

Orphan Variables.

In the rules R7 and R8, we use the notion of orphan variable. An
orphan
variable of a SJFCQ
Q
(w.r.t. a set
\Sigma
of FDs) is a variable that occurs once in
Q
,
at a non-primary-lhs position (w.r.t.
\Sigma
).

Induced FD Set.

Observe that in the rules R5 and R8, we rewrite the pair
(\Sigma,Q)
into a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
Q
by replacing an atom over a relation
R
of arity
n
with another atom over a relation
R^{\prime}
of arity
n-1
. In this case, the rewriting induces a new FD set
\Sigma^{\prime}
(for all the other rules, we have
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma
). If we replace the atom
R(\bar{z})
in
Q
with the atom
R^{\prime}(\bar{y})
(over a fresh relation
R^{\prime}
) to obtain
Q^{\prime}
, then the FD set
\Sigma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Sigma
by removing all the
R
-FDs, and adding, for each
R
-FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
, the FD
R^{\prime}:(X\setminus\mathord{\{A\}})\rightarrow(Y\setminus\mathord{\{A\}})
, where
A
is the attribute corresponding to the position of
R
that was removed to obtain
R^{\prime}
(we assume that the other attribute names are unchanged in
R^{\prime}
). We denote the FD set over
R^{\prime}
by
\Sigma_{R^{\prime}}
, and so
\Sigma^{\prime}=(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{R})\cup\Sigma_{R^{\prime}}
. If after removing the attribute
A
some FD becomes trivial, i.e., has the empty set as its right-hand side, then we assume that this FD is removed from
\Sigma_{R^{\prime}}
. Moreover, if two FDs in
\Sigma_{R^{\prime}}
have the same left-hand side (which can be the case if the attribute
A
occurs on the left-hand side of some FD in
\Sigma_{R}
), then we assume that these FDs are merged into a single FD. Hence, the resulting FD set is canonical. It is easy to to see that, if the original FD set
\Sigma
has an LHS chain, then
\Sigma^{\prime}
also has an LHS chain.

With the rewrite rules in place, we now generalize the definition of final queries of Maslowski and Wijsen
[
25
]
to pairs consisting of a set of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ.

Definition 4

(
Final Pairs
)
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. We say that the pair
(\Sigma,Q)
is final if

1.

Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, and

2.

for every set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
such that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, the query
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe.

We now show that, starting from a pair of a set of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ, we can always reach a final pair by applying rewrite rules.

Lemma 5
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe. There is a set
\hat{\Sigma}
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
\hat{Q}
such that
(\hat{\Sigma},\hat{Q})
is final and
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\hat{\Sigma},\hat{Q})
.

Proof.

We first recall two simple technical properties, already proved by Maslowski and Wijsen
[
25
]
for primary keys, that can be easily extended to FDs with an LHS chain.
In particular, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain,

•

it is
not
the case that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and

•

the set
S=\mathord{\{(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\mid(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\}}
is finite.

To prove this, for a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in S
, let
n,n^{\prime}
be the sum of arities of the relation names occurring in
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, respectively.
We also denote by
m
(resp.,
m^{\prime}
) the sum over all the atoms
\alpha
of
Q
(resp.,
Q^{\prime}
) of the number of positions
(R,A)
(where
R
is the relation name of
\alpha
) such that
\alpha[A]\in\mathbf{V}
.
If
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, then one of the following holds:

1.

n^{\prime}<n
, or

2.

n^{\prime}=n
and
m^{\prime}<m
, or

3.

n^{\prime}=n
,
m=m^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{const}(Q^{\prime})\subsetneq\mathsf{const}(Q)
,

Moreover, the set of FDs
\Sigma^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by the applied rewrite rule. This immediately implies the desired properties.
From the above discussion, we conclude that each rewriting sequence of
(\Sigma,Q)
is finite, and there are finitely many distinct rewriting sequences of
(\Sigma,Q)
. Among all these rewriting sequences of
(\Sigma,Q)
that lead to a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
such that
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, we consider the longest one denoted by
s=(\Sigma_{0},Q_{0}),\ldots,(\Sigma_{n},Q_{n})
. It is clear that the pair
(\Sigma_{n},Q_{n})
is final, and the claim follows with
(\hat{\Sigma},\hat{Q})=(\Sigma_{n},Q_{n})
.

Note that Lemma
5
only applies to non-safe queries as it is not possible to obtain a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
such that
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe via a rewriting sequence of
(\Sigma,Q)
for some query
Q
that is
\Sigma
-safe.

Rewrite Rules and Relative Frequency.

We proceed to show that whenever a pair
(\Sigma,Q)
can be rewritten via a rewrite rule into a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, then there is a polynomial-time Turing reduction (i.e., Cook reduction) from
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Lemma 6
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. For every set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
,
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
implies that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Proof.

We prove this by considering each one of the rewrite rules given in Figure
3
separately. To this end, given a database
D
, we need to construct in polynomial time a database
E
such that the value
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
can be computed from
\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)
.
The proofs for the rewrite rules R1-R4, R8, and R9, which are deferred to the appendix, are similar to the corresponding proofs for primary keys
[
25
]
, but we have to take into account the FDs in the primary prefix. To this end, we use the databases
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
.
For the remaining rules (R5, R6, R7, and R10), our proofs, which we give below, are considerably more involved. We elaborate on the differences between the primary key setting and our setting in the corresponding proof.

In what follows, we assume the following order over attributes
A,A^{\prime}
of the same relation name:

-

A=A^{\prime}
if both
A
and
A^{\prime}
do not occur in any FD of
\Sigma
, or if

A
and
A^{\prime}
occur in the same FD of
\Sigma
on the same side (i.e., both on the left-hand side or both on the right-hand side), and

-

A>A^{\prime}
if
A^{\prime}
occurs in some FD of
\Sigma
while
A
does not occur in any FD, or if both
A
and
A^{\prime}
occur in FDs of
\Sigma
, and

A
occurs later in the chain (i.e., in an FD that appears later in the chain or on the right-hand side of the FD in which
A^{\prime}
appears on the left-hand side).

Observe that an attribute might occur in more than one FD, in which case we consider the
first
occurrence of the attribute in the above conditions.

Rewrite Rule R5
:
(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha_{1}\}})\triangleright\,((\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{R})\cup\Sigma_{R^{\prime}},Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha_{2}\}})
if
\alpha_{1}>_{\Sigma}\alpha_{2}
and the relation name of
\alpha_{2}
does not occur in
Q
.

The proof for rule R5 in the case of primary keys is rather straightforward
[
25
]
, as all the variables or constants that occur at primary-lhs positions (resp., non-primary-lhs positions) correspond to attributes on the left-hand side (resp., right-hand side) of the key. This is not the case in our setting, because two non-primary-lhs positions might correspond to attributes that occur in two different FDs (possibly on two different sides – one on the left-hand side of an FD and one on the right-hand side of another FD). Hence, here, when we remove an occurrence of some variable, we have to carefully choose which occurrence of this variable to remove. We proceed by considering all the possible cases according to the definition of the binary relation
>_{\Sigma}
.

Case (1):
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})>R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
if
x
has two or more occurrences in
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
.
Let
\alpha_{1}=R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
and
\alpha_{2}=R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
. Assume that the variable
x
occurs at positions
(R,A),(R,A^{\prime})
of
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
.
Assume also, without loss of generality, that
A\geq A^{\prime}
. As mentioned above, an induced FD set might merge several FDs into a single FD; however, in the remainder of this proof for R5 we assume, for clarity of presentation, that the FDs are not merged. Clearly, this has no impact on the satisfaction of the constraints.

Given a database
D
over
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, let
E
be the database over
\mathbf{S}
that contains all the facts of
D
over relation names
R^{\prime\prime}
such that
R^{\prime\prime}\neq R^{\prime}
, and, for every
R^{\prime}
-fact
f
, the
R
-fact
f^{\prime}
defined as follows:

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A$}\\
f[A^{\prime}]&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
A
does not occur in any FD of
\Sigma_{R}
, then clearly its addition does not affect the satisfaction of the constraints. Moreover, since the value of attribute
A
in every
R
-fact of
E
is the same as the value of attribute
A^{\prime}
that is associated with the same variable in the query, the additional attribute has no impact on the entailment of the query. Therefore, in this case, we have that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{2}\})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{1}\}).

Assume now that
A
occurs in an FD of
\Sigma_{R}
and the first occurrence of
A
is in the FD
R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
. Since
A\geq A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime}
occurs in an FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
of
\Sigma_{R}
, and one of the following holds:

-

\ell<k
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
,
A\in X_{\ell}
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in Y_{\ell}
,
A\in Y_{\ell}
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
,
A\in Y_{\ell}
.

Let
f,g
be two
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
, and let
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
be the corresponding
R
-facts of
E
.
We will show that
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
if and only if
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\models\Sigma_{R}
.
If
f
and
g
violate an FD
R^{\prime}:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
, then they agree on all the attributes of
X_{p}
and disagree on at least one attribute
B\in Y_{p}
.
Then:

1.

If the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
, then clearly
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly violate this FD and
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

2.

If the FD
R:(X_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}})\rightarrow Y_{p}
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on the value of
A
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly violate this FD, and
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
. If
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
disagree on the value of attribute
A
, then they also disagree on the value of
A^{\prime}
, which implies that
f[A^{\prime}]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
. In this case, the attribute
A^{\prime}
cannot appear on the left-hand side of an FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
with
\ell\leq p
, as in this case,
f
and
g
do not agree on the left-hand side of
R^{\prime}:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
, which contradicts the fact that
f
and
g
jointly violate this FD. If
A^{\prime}
occurs on the right-hand side of
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
with
\ell<p
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on the left-hand side of this FD (since
X_{\ell}\subseteq X_{p}
) and disagree on its right-hand side, and
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.
Note that, in this case,
A\not\in X_{\ell}
since
A^{\prime}\in Y_{\ell}
and
A\geq A^{\prime}
.

3.

If the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow(Y_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}})
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
, then clearly
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on all the attributes of
X_{p}
and disagree on at least one attribute of
Y_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}}
(since
f
and
g
disagree on at least one attribute of
Y_{p}
), and
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

We conclude that if
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
, then
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

Next, assume that
\{f,g\}
satisfies all the FDs of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
. Let
R^{\prime}:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
be such an FD.
Then:

1.

If the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
, then clearly
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD.

2.

If the FD
R:(X_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}})\rightarrow Y_{p}
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
, then again
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD, as they agree on the values of all the attributes in
Y_{p}
.

3.

If the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow(Y_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}})
occurs in
\Sigma_{R}
and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
either disagree on some attribute of
X_{p}
or agree on the value of attribute
A
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD. If
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on all the attributes of
X_{p}
and disagree on the value of
A
, then they also disagree on the value of
A^{\prime}
and so
f[A^{\prime}]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
; hence, the attribute
A^{\prime}
does not appear on the left-hand side of an FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
with
\ell\leq p
(since
X_{\ell}\subseteq X_{p}
and
f
and
g
also agree on the values of all the attributes in
X_{p}
). In this case,
A^{\prime}
occurs on the right-hand side of an FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
with
\ell<p
or on the right-hand side of the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow(Y_{p}\cup\mathord{\{A\}})
; thus,
f
and
g
agree on the left-hand side of the corresponding FD in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
and disagree on its right-hand side, which contradicts the fact that
f
and
g
satisfy all the FDs of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
.

If the set of FDs
\Sigma_{R}
contains an additional FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
that does not have a corresponding FD in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
(as after removing the attribute
A
the FD becomes trivial), and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
either disagree on the value of some attribute in
X_{p}
or agree on the value of attribute
A
, then they clearly satisfy this FD. If
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on the value of all the attributes in
X_{p}
and
f^{\prime}[A]\neq g^{\prime}[A]
, then
f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]\neq g^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
and so
f[A^{\prime}]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
. Again, it cannot be the case that
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
for some
\ell\leq p
, as
X_{\ell}\subseteq X_{p}
and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on the value of all the attributes in
X_{p}
. Hence,
A^{\prime}\in Y_{\ell}
for some
\ell<p
. In this case,
f
and
g
agree on the values of all the attributes in
X_{p}
(and so also in
X_{\ell}
) as
f[B]=f^{\prime}[B]
and
g[B]=g^{\prime}[B]
for every
B\in X_{p}
(note that
A\not\in X_{p}
), and disagree on the value of the attribute
A^{\prime}
in
Y_{\ell}
; hence,
f
and
g
jointly violate the FD
R^{\prime}:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
.
We conclude that if
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
, then
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\models\Sigma_{R}
. Therefore, we have that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

Moreover, since every
R
-fact in
E
uses the same value at positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
,
every
R^{\prime}
-fact
f
of
D
is such that
f\models\alpha_{2}
iff the corresponding
R
-fact
f^{\prime}
of
E
is such that
f^{\prime}\models\alpha_{1}
. Thus, the additional attribute has no impact on the entailment of the query, and we have that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime},Q\cup\{\alpha_{2}\}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{\alpha_{1}\}}(E).

It then follows that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{2}\})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{1}\}).

Case (2):
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})>R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
.

Let
\alpha_{1}=R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
and
\alpha_{2}=R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
. As in the previous case, we denote by
\Sigma
the set of FDs over the schema
\mathbf{S}
that contains the relation name
R
, and by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the set of FDs over the schema
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
that contains the relation name
R^{\prime}
. Assume that the constant
c
occurs at the (non-primary-lhs) position
(R,A)
of
\alpha_{1}
.
Given a database
D
over
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, let
E
be the database over
\mathbf{S}
that contains all the facts of
D
over relation names
R^{\prime\prime}
such that
R^{\prime\prime}\neq R^{\prime}
, and, for every
R^{\prime}
-fact
f
of
D
, the
R
-fact defined as follows:

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A$}\\
c&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
c
is a constant (the same constant is used for all facts).
Clearly,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

This holds since all the
R
-facts of
E
agree on the value of the additional attribute
A
; hence, this attribute does not affect the satisfaction of the FDs – two
R^{\prime}
-facts
f
and
g
of
D
violate an FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
if and only if the corresponding
R
-facts
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
of
E
violate an FD of
\Sigma_{R}
. Moreover, since all the
R
-facts of
E
agree with the atom
\alpha_{1}
on the constant at the position
(R,A)
, this additional attribute
A
has no impact on the entailment of the query, and we have that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime},Q\cup\{\alpha_{2}\}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{\alpha_{1}\}}(E).

We can then conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{2}\})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha_{1}\}).

Rewrite Rule R6
:
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and
x\in\mathsf{prhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
, and one of the variables of
\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
is a liaison variable of
Q
.

In the case of primary keys, the rewrite rule R6 applies to liaison variables of
Q
; that is, if
x
is a liaison variable of
Q
, then we can rewrite
Q
into
Q_{x\mapsto c}
. In our case, the requirement that one of the variables
y
of
\alpha
is a liaison variable of
Q
remains, but we may consider a different variable
x
(that we have to carefully choose) in the rewriting, and we need to add additional requirements on the positions corresponding to the attributes that occur in the primary FD of the relation name of
\alpha
. Assume
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
. Assume also that the variable
x
occurs at the position
(R,A)
of
\alpha
, and the liaison variable
y
of
\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
occurs at the position
(R,A^{\prime})
(it can be the case that
x=y
and so
A=A^{\prime}
). We prove the following in the appendix:

Lemma 7
.

For a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
,

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}),\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

Lemma
7
implies that:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}),\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

Recall that the ratio

\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}

can be computed in polynomial time due to Proposition
1
. We denote
D^{\prime}=D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})
and show that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

for some database
E
that we define next.

Let
E
be the database that contains all the facts of
D^{\prime}
over relation names
R^{\prime}
such that
R^{\prime}\neq R
, and, for every
R
-fact
f
, the
R
-fact

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A^{\prime}$}\\
f[B]&\mbox{if $B=A^{\prime}$ and $f[A]=c$}\\
\rho(f[B])&\mbox{if $B=A^{\prime}$ and $f[A]\neq c$}\end{cases}

where
\rho
is a function that maps constants
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
to constants
\rho(b)\not\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
such that
\rho(a)\neq\rho(b)
for
a\neq b
. Recall that, for every FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
and for all
R
-facts
f
of
D^{\prime}
, we have that
f[A]=\alpha[A]
for each
A\in X\cup Y
. Hence, every pair
f,g
of
R
-facts of
D^{\prime}
satisfies all the FDs in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
. We conclude that every pair
f,g
of
R
-facts of
D^{\prime}
satisfies all the FDs in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, as the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
is contained in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
. Since in
E
we only modify values in the attribute associated with the liaison variable
y
, we also have that every pair
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
of
R
-facts of
E
satisfies all the FDs in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
.

Clearly, two
R
-facts
f
and
g
in
D^{\prime}
that disagree on the value of some attribute on the left-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, jointly satisfy
\Sigma_{R}
, as this attribute belongs to the left-hand side of every FD that appears later in the chain. Since
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, we have that
\alpha[A]=a
for some constant
a
, for every attribute
A
that occurs on the left-hand side of this FD. Since in
E
we do not modify the values of attributes associated with constants, we also have that the corresponding
R
-facts
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
in
E
jointly satisfy
\Sigma_{R}
. If
f
and
g
agree on the values of all the attributes on the left-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, and disagree on the value of attribute
A
, then they jointly violate the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, and so do the corresponding facts
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
in
E
, since we do not modify the value of attribute
A
if
A\neq A^{\prime}
, and if
A=A^{\prime}
, then we have that
f[A]=g[A]
if and only if
f^{\prime}[A]=g^{\prime}[A]
as we are only renaming constants. Finally, if
f
and
g
agree on the values of all the attributes on the left-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, and also agree on the value of attribute
A
, it is easy to verify that
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
if and only if
f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]=g^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
, and since we only modify the value of
A^{\prime}
in
E
, we have that
f
and
g
satisfy
\Sigma_{R}
if and only if
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
satisfy
\Sigma_{R}
. We conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\prime})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

We now prove that a repair
J
of
D^{\prime}
entails
Q_{x\mapsto c}
if and only if the corresponding repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
(that contains the same facts over the relation names
R
such that
R^{\prime}\neq R
as
J
, and a fact
f^{\prime}
over
R
if and only if the corresponding
R
-fact
f
is in
J
) entails
Q
.

Let
J
be a repair of
D^{\prime}
that entails
Q_{x\mapsto c}
.
Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q_{x\mapsto c}
to
J
.
It must be the case that
f=R(h(\bar{z}))
uses the value
c
at the position
(R,A)
. Therefore, the corresponding fact
f^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
is such that
f^{\prime}=f
. Moreover,
J^{\prime}
also contains every fact
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}^{\prime}))
for
R^{\prime}\neq R
; thus,
h
is a homomorphism from
Q_{x\mapsto c}
to
J^{\prime}
. For
h^{\prime}
such that
h^{\prime}(y)=h(y)
for all variables
y
that occur in
Q_{x\mapsto c}
and
h^{\prime}(x)=c
, we then have that
h^{\prime}
is a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
, and that concludes our proof of the first direction.

Next, let
J^{\prime}
be a repair of
E
that entails
Q
. Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
. Since
y
is a liaison variable, it has at least one more occurrence in
Q
, at the position
(R^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime})
of some atom
\alpha^{\prime}=R^{\prime}(\bar{z}^{\prime})
(it can be the case that
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
with
A^{\prime}\neq A^{\prime\prime}
). Moreover, the fact
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}^{\prime}))
uses a constant
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
in the attribute corresponding to this position, as we have only changed values in the position
(R,A^{\prime})
. Therefore, the fact
R(h(\bar{z}))
also uses a constant
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
at the position
(R,A^{\prime})
. The only
R
-facts
f
of
E
that use a constant from
\mathsf{dom}(D)
at the position
(R,A^{\prime})
are those satisfying
f[A]=c
; hence, we have that
h(x)=c
, and it follows that
h
is a homomorphism from
Q_{x\mapsto c}
to
J
.
We conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}(D^{\prime})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(E)

and therefore

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

Rewrite Rule R7
:
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x})
if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with
x,y\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
, none of
x,y
is an orphan variable, and
x
or
y
(or both) is a liaison variable of
Q
that occurs at some non-primary-lhs position of
\alpha
.

Similarly to the case of the rule R5, the fact that the variables
x
and
y
might appear in two different FDs makes the proof more involved than the corresponding proof for primary keys
[
25
]
. Moreover, we have added an additional condition on the variables
x
and
y
that does not appear in the corresponding rule for primary keys – none of the variables
x,y
is an orphan variable. In our case, this additional condition is necessary for the reduction.

Let
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
be an atom of
Q
such that
x,y\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
. Assume that the variables
x
and
y
occur at positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
, respectively.
As mentioned in the proof for Case 1 of R5, if one of the positions is a non-primary-lhs position and
A\geq A^{\prime}
, then the position
(R,A)
is a non-primary-lhs position. Hence, we assume, without loss of generality, that
A\geq A^{\prime}
, and we know that the position
(R,A)
is a non-primary-lhs position. Since none of
x,y
is an orphan variable, it holds that
x
is a liaison variable of
Q
.

Let
E
be the database that contains all the facts of
D
over relation names
R^{\prime}
such that
R^{\prime}\neq R
, and, for every
R
-fact
f
of
D
, the following
R
-fact:

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A$}\\
c&\mbox{if $B=A$, $f[A]=f[A^{\prime}]$, and $f[A]=c$}\\
\rho(c)&\mbox{if $B=A$, $f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]$, and $f[A]=c$}\end{cases}

where
\rho
is a function that maps constants
a\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
to constants
\rho(a)\not\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
such that
\rho(a)\neq\rho(b)
for
a\neq b
.

If
A
does not occur in any FD of
\Sigma
, then clearly its value does not affect the satisfaction of the constraints, and we have that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

Now, assume that the attribute
A
does occur in some FD
R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
of
\Sigma_{R}
. In this case, since
A\geq A^{\prime}
, the attribute
A^{\prime}
also occurs in an FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
of
\Sigma_{R}
, and, as mentioned in Case 1 of the proof for rule R5, one of the following holds:

-

\ell<k
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
,
A\in X_{\ell}
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in Y_{\ell}
,
A\in Y_{\ell}
,

-

\ell=k
and
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
,
A\in Y_{\ell}
.

Let
f
and
g
be two
R
-facts of
D
, and let
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the corresponding
R
-facts in
E
.
We will show that
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma_{R}
if and only if
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\models\Sigma_{R}
.
If
f
and
g
violate an FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
in
\Sigma_{R}
, then they agree on all the attributes of
X_{p}
and disagree on at least one attribute
B\in Y_{p}
.
Then:

1.

If
A\not\in(X_{p}\cup Y_{p})
, then clearly
\mathord{\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

2.

If
f[A]=f[A^{\prime}]
and
g[A]=g[A^{\prime}]
then, by definition,
f^{\prime}=f
and
g^{\prime}=g
, and
\mathord{\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

3.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
and
A\in Y_{p}
, then
\mathord{\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
because
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
agree on the values of all the attributes in
X_{p}
. Moreover, since
f
and
g
disagree on the value of attribute
B
, the facts
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
also disagree on the value of
B
. If
B\neq A
, then this is clearly the case, whereas if
B=A
, then this holds by the definition of
\rho
.

4.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in X_{p}
, and
f[A^{\prime}]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
, then it cannot be the case that
A^{\prime}\in X_{\ell}
for some
\ell\leq p
. Otherwise, since
X_{\ell}\subseteq X_{p}
, the facts
f,g
disagree on the value of at least one attribute of
X_{p}
, which contradicts the fact that they jointly violate the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
. Thus,
A^{\prime}\in Y_{\ell}
for some
\ell<p
. In this case,
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
jointly violate the FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
and
\mathord{\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
. Note that
A\not\in X_{\ell}
because
A\geq A^{\prime}
.

5.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in X_{p}
, and
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
, then
g[A]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
because
f,g
agree on every value of every attribute in
X_{p}
, including the value of attribute
A
. Hence, if
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
, then
g[A]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
, and by the definition of
\rho
, the fact that
f[A]=g[A]
implies that
f^{\prime}[A]=g^{\prime}[A]
. Therefore,
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
agree on the values of all the attributes in
X_{p}
and disagree on the value of attribute
B\in Y_{p}
, and thus
\mathord{\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

We conclude that if
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
, then
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.

Next, assume that
\{f,g\}
satisfies all the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
. Let
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
be such an FD.
Then:

1.

If
A\not\in(X_{p}\cup Y_{p})
, then clearly
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
also satisfy this FD.

2.

If
f[A]=f[A^{\prime}]
and
g[A]=g[A^{\prime}]
then, by definition,
f^{\prime}=f
and
g^{\prime}=g
, and again
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD.

3.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in X_{p}
, and
f[B]\neq g[B]
for some
B\in X_{p}
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD, as they also disagree on the value of attribute
B
. This clearly holds if
B\neq A
, and if
B=A
, then
f[A]\neq g[A]
, which implies that
f^{\prime}[A]\neq g^{\prime}[A]
according to the definition of
\rho
.

4.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in X_{p}
, and
f[B]=g[B]
for every
B\in X_{p}
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
jointly satisfy this FD, as they agree on the values of all the attributes in
Y_{p}
.

5.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in Y_{p}
, and
f[B]\neq g[B]
for some
B\in X_{p}
, then
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
also disagree on the value of attribute
B\in X_{p}
and satisfy this FD.

6.

If
f[A]\neq f[A^{\prime}]
,
A\in Y_{p}
, and
f[B]=g[B]
for every
B\in X_{p}
, then
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
as
A^{\prime}
is either in
X_{p}
(in which case clearly
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
) or in
Y_{\ell}
for some
\ell\leq p
(in which case
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
, as otherwise,
f
and
g
jointly violate the FD
R:X_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{\ell}
). Since
f
and
g
agree on the value of all the attributes in
X_{p}
, they also agree on the value of all the attributes in
Y_{p}
, including
A
. Hence,
f[A]=g[A]
and
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
, which implies that
f^{\prime}[A]=g^{\prime}[A]
. Thus,
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
agree on all the values of the attributes in
X_{p}\cup Y_{p}
, and satisfy this FD.

We conclude that if
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma_{R}
, then
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\models\Sigma_{R}
. Therefore,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

We now prove that a repair
J
of
D
entails
Q_{y\mapsto x}
iff the corresponding repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
entails
Q
.

Let
J
be a repair of
D
that entails
Q_{y\mapsto x}
.
Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q_{y\mapsto x}
to
J
. It must be the case that
f=R(h(\bar{z}))
uses the same value for attributes
A
and
A^{\prime}
, as both are associated with the same variable in
Q_{y\mapsto x}
. Therefore, the corresponding fact
f^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
is such that
f^{\prime}=f
. Moreover,
J^{\prime}
also contains every fact
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}^{\prime}))
for
R^{\prime}\neq R
; thus,
h
is also a homomorphism from
Q_{y\mapsto x}
to
J^{\prime}
. For
h^{\prime}
that satisfies
h^{\prime}(z)=h(z)
for every variable
z
that occurs in
Q_{y\mapsto x}
and
h^{\prime}(y)=h(x)
, we then have that
h^{\prime}
is a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
, and that concludes our proof of the first direction.

Next, let
J^{\prime}
be a repair of
E
that entails
Q
. Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
. Since
x
is a liaison variable it has at least one more occurrence in
Q
, at position
(R^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime})
of some atom
\alpha^{\prime}=R^{\prime}(\bar{z}^{\prime})
(it can be the case that
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
with
A\neq A^{\prime\prime}\neq A^{\prime}
). Moreover, the fact
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}^{\prime}))
uses a constant
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
in the attribute
A^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, the fact
R(h(\bar{z}))
also uses a constant
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
at the position
(R,A)
. The only

R
-facts in
E
that use constants from
\mathsf{dom}(D)
at position
(R,A)
are those that use the same value for attributes
A
and
A^{\prime}
; hence, we have that
h(x)=h(y)
, and it follows that
h
is a homomorphism from
Q_{y\mapsto x}
to
J
.
We conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(E)

and thus

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{y\mapsto x})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

Rewrite Rule R10
:
(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}})\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}})
if
x
occurs at a non-primary-lhs position and has another occurrence at a primary-lhs position of
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
.

This rule is a generalization of the corresponding rule for primary keys
[
25
]
. While the rule R10 for primary keys considers binary relation names where the same variable occurs at both positions, our rule considers arbitrary relation names where the variable
x
occurs both at a primary-lhs position and a non-primary-lhs position.

Suppose that the variable
x
occurs at the (non-primary-lhs) position
(R,A)
of
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
. Assume also that
x
occurs at the position
(R,A^{\prime})
, which is a primary-lhs position. Since
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
has non-primary-lhs positions, the set of FDs
\Sigma_{R}
has a primary FD. Moreover, since
(R,A^{\prime})
is a primary-lhs position,
A^{\prime}
occurs on the left-hand side of this primary FD. Now, let
E
be the database that contains all the
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
with
R^{\prime}\neq R
, and, for every
R
-fact
f
of
D
, the
R
-fact

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A$}\\
f[A^{\prime}]&\mbox{if $B=A$ and $f[A]=c$}\\
\rho(f[A])&\mbox{if $B=A$ and $f[A]\neq c$}\\
\end{cases}

where
\rho
is a function that maps constants
b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
to constants
\rho(b)\not\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
such that
\rho(b)\neq\rho(c)
for
b\neq c
.

Let
f
and
g
be two
R
-facts in
D^{\prime}
, and let
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the corresponding
R
-facts in
E
. If
f,g
jointly violate (resp., satisfy) an FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
in
\Sigma_{R}
, and
A\not\in(X_{p}\cup Y_{p})
, then clearly
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
also jointly violate (resp. satisfy) this FD as
f^{\prime}[B]=f[B]
and
g^{\prime}[B]=g[B]
for every attribute
B\neq A
. If
A\in X_{p}
or
A\in Y_{p}
, then the FD
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
is not in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
, and
A^{\prime}\in X_{p}
. In this case, if
f[A^{\prime}]\neq g[A^{\prime}]
, then clearly
f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]\neq g^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
; hence,
f,g
jointly satisfy
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
and the same holds for
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
.
If
f[A^{\prime}]=g[A^{\prime}]
, by the definition of
\rho
, it holds that
f^{\prime}[A]=g^{\prime}[A]
if and only if
f[A]=g[A]
; therefore,
f,g
jointly violate
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
if and only if
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
jointly violate
R:X_{p}\rightarrow Y_{p}
.
We conclude that
\{f,g\}\models\Sigma_{R}
if and only if
\{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\}\models\Sigma_{R}
. Hence,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\prime})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

We now prove that a repair
J
of
D
entails
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}
if and only if the corresponding repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
(that contains the same facts over the relations
R^{\prime}\neq R
as
J
, and a fact
f^{\prime}
over
R
if and only if the corresponding fact
f
is in
J
) entails
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}
.

Let
J
be a repair of
D
that entails
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}
. Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}
to
J
. It must be the case that
f=R(h(\bar{z},c,\bar{y}))
uses the value
c
at the position
(R,A)
. Thus, the corresponding fact
f^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
is such that
f^{\prime}[A]=f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
. Moreover,
J^{\prime}
also contains every fact
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}^{\prime}))
for other atoms
R^{\prime}(\bar{z}^{\prime})\in Q
; thus,
h
is a homomorphism from
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}
to
J^{\prime}
.

Next, let
J^{\prime}
be a repair of
E
that entails
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}
. Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}
to
J
. Clearly,
f^{\prime}=R(h(\bar{z},x,\bar{y}))
is a fact of
E
that satisfies
f^{\prime}[A]=f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
. The only
R
-facts
f^{\prime}
in
E
that satisfy
f^{\prime}[A]=f^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
are those for which the corresponding fact
f
in
D
satisfies
f[A]=c
. Therefore,
h
is a homomorphism from
Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}
to
J
.
We conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}(E)

and therefore

\mathsf{rfreq}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}}(D)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}(E).

This completes the proof of Lemma
6
for rules R5, R7, and R10; the proofs for all the other rules can be found in the appendix.

Final Pairs are Hard.

We now show that, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, there exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys and a query
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.
To this end, we first present four technical lemmas (Lemmas
8
-
11
) that essentially tell us the following: given a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, for every relation name
R
that occurs in
Q
, the set
\Sigma_{R}
of FDs either consists of a single key, or is a set of FDs of one of six specific forms; the proofs are deferred to the appendix.
In what follows, for every
R
-atom
\alpha_{R}
of
Q
, assuming that
R:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, we refer to the positions
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y_{i}\}
as the
primary-rhs
positions of
\alpha_{R}
. We refer to the rest of the positions of
\alpha_{R}
as
non-primary-rhs
positions, and to the positions outside
\{(R,A)\mid A\in(X_{i}\cup Y_{i})\}
as
non-primary-FD
positions.

In the statements of Lemmas
8
-
11
, for brevity, let
\Sigma
be a set of FDs with an LHS chain, and
Q
be an SJFCQ such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final.

Lemma 8
.

If the
R
-atom of
Q
occurs in
Q\setminus\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, then
\Sigma_{R}
consists of a single key.

Lemma 9
.

If the
R
-atom of
Q
has a liaison variable
x
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is also associated with the variable
x
.

Lemma 10
.

If the
R
-atom
\alpha_{R}
of
Q
has a constant
c
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a liaison variable
x
.

Lemma 11
.

If the
R
-atom
\alpha
of
Q
has an orphan variable
x
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a liaison variable
y
, or

3.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a constant
c
, or

4.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there are only two non-primary-FD positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
that are associated with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in
\alpha
, or

5.

\Sigma_{R}
has two FDs
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
and
R:(X\cup\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{A^{\prime\prime}\}}
such that the first is the primary FD, there are no non-primary-lhs positions besides
(R,A)
,
(R,A^{\prime})
,
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
(where
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
are also non-primary-rhs positions), and the positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
are associated with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in the atom
\alpha
.

Having the six specific forms of sets of FDs provided by Lemmas
8
-
11
, we present a series of six technical lemmas (Lemmas
12
-
17
), one for each specific form, that will allow us to devise the desired reduction; the proofs of those lemmas are deferred to the appendix. In the statements of Lemmas
12
-
17
, we assume that
\Sigma
is a set of FDs with an LHS chain, and
Q
is an SJFCQ that is not
\Sigma
-safe. We further assume that
n>0
atoms of
Q
are
not
associated with a single key in
\Sigma
, and we refer to such atoms as
non-single-key
atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma
.

Lemma 12
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
are both associated with a liaison variable
x
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 13
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with a constant
c
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a liaison variable
x
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 14
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a liaison variable
y
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 15
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a constant
c
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 16
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there are only two non-primary-FD positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
, and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime}),(R,A^{\prime\prime})
with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in
\alpha
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma 17
.

If for the
R
-atom
\alpha
of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has two FDs
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,
R:(X\cup\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{A^{\prime\prime}\}}
with the first being the primary FD,

2.

there are no non-primary-lhs positions besides
(R,A)
,
(R,A^{\prime})
,
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
(where
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
are also non-primary-rhs positions), and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime}),(R,A^{\prime\prime})
with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in the atom
\alpha
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Having the above technical lemmas in place, we are now ready to establish the desired result concerning final pairs consisting of a set of FDs and an SJFCQ.

Lemma 18
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. There exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys, and an SJFCQ
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Proof.

The proof is by a simple induction on the number
n\geq 0
of non-single-key atoms of
Q
.

Base Case.

For
n=0
, we have that every relation name that occurs in
Q
is associated with a single key, in which case the claim follows with
\Sigma_{PK}=\Sigma
and
Q_{PK}=Q
.

Inductive Step.

For
n>0
, we know that for every non-single-key
R
-atom of
Q
w.r.t.
\Sigma
, the set
\Sigma_{R}
is of one of six forms (Lemmas
8
-
11
), and once we apply the reduction of the corresponding lemma (among Lemmas
12
-
17
), we obtain a query
Q^{\prime}
that has
n-1
non-single-key atoms w.r.t. the FD set
\Sigma^{\prime}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there is a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys, and an SJFCQ
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. The composition of the reduction from
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
and the reduction from
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is a Cook reduction from
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
, and the claim follows.
It is important to say that in the queries
Q^{\prime}
and sets
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs obtained in Lemmas
12
-
17
, all the non-single-key atoms remain in one of these six specific forms. That is, every liaison (resp., orphan) variable remains a liaison (resp., an orphan) variable.

Having Lemmas
5
,
6
and
18
in place, it is easy to establish Theorem
7
.
Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe. By Lemma
5
, from
(\Sigma,Q)
we can reach a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
that is final via a rewriting sequence. Thus, by Lemma
6
and the fact that Cook reductions compose, we get that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
. Since, by Lemma
18
, there exists a set
\Sigma_{PK}
of primary keys and a query
Q_{PK}
that is not
\Sigma_{PK}
-safe such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, by composing reductions, we get that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma_{PK},Q_{PK})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
, and the claim follows.

6
Approximate Counting

We now focus on approximate counting. As already discussed in Section
3
, the ultimate goal is whenever
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
or
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is intractable, where
\Sigma
is a set of FDs and
Q
an SJFCQ, to classify it as approximable (i.e., it admits an FPRAS), or as inapproximable (i.e., it does not admit an FPRAS, possibly under some widely accepted complexity assumptions).
However, as we explain below, obtaining an approximability/inapproximability dichotomy is a highly non-trivial task that remains open. We provide results that form crucial steps towards such a dichotomy. We show that FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) form an island of approximability. On the other hand, unlike primary keys, there are simple cases for which the existence of an FPRAS is not guaranteed, unless
\text{\rm NP}\subseteq\text{\rm BPP}
;
BPP
is the class of decision problems that are efficiently solvable via a randomized algorithm with a bounded two-sided error
[
4
]
.

Theorem 8
.

The following hold:

1.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and a CQ
Q
.
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS.

2.

Let
\Sigma=\{R:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2},R:A_{3}\rightarrow A_{4}\}
, where
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4})
is the tuple of attribute names of
R/4
.
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
does not admit an FPRAS, unless
\text{\rm NP}\subseteq\text{\rm BPP}
.

3.

Let
\Sigma=\{R:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2},R:A_{3}\rightarrow A_{4}\}
, where
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4})
is the tuple of attribute names of
R/4
. For every SJFCQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
does not admit an FPRAS, unless
\text{\rm NP}\subseteq\text{\rm BPP}
.

Notice that item (1) holds for arbitrary CQs (even with self-joins); in fact, we can easily extend item (1) to unions of CQs.
Let us also observe that, due to Lemma
1
, to establish item (3) it suffices to establish item (2).
The rest of the section is devoted to discussing the proofs of items (1) and (2) of Theorem
8
. But let us first briefly discuss the difficulty underlying a pure dichotomy result. In our discussion, we focus on the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, where
\Sigma
is a set of FDs and
Q
a SJFCQ, but it will be clear that the same argument applies also for the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
.

The Difficulty Underlying a Dichotomy.

Let
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
be the counting problem that accepts as input a bipartite graph
G
, and asks for the number of maximal matchings in
G
. It is known that
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
is
\sharp\text{\rm P}
-complete
[
29
]
, whereas the question whether it admits an FPRAS is a challenging open problem
[
18
]
.
Due to item (1) of Theorem
8
, to get a dichotomy result it suffices to classify, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs
without
an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and an SJFCQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
as approximable or inapproximable.
We proceed to show the following result, which essentially states that an approximability/inapproximability dichotomy for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
will resolve the open question whether
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
admits an FPRAS.

Proposition 5
.

The following are equivalent.

1.

There exists a set
\Sigma
of FDs without an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS.

2.

\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
admits an FPRAS.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
. Assume that there is a set
\Sigma
of FDs without an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS. We know from
[
23
]
that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
is
\sharp
P-hard, which is shown via a polynomial-time parsimonious reduction from
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
.
We further know, by Lemma
1
, that there is a polynomial-time parsimonious reduction from
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
to
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
. Therefore, by composing reductions, we get a polynomial-time parsimonious reduction from
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
to
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
. But since, by hypothesis,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS, we conclude that
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
also admits an FPRAS.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
. Assume now that, for every set
\Sigma
of FDs without an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and an SJFCQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
does not admit an FPRAS. Therefore, this will be also the case for
\Sigma^{\star}=\{R:A\rightarrow B,R:B\rightarrow A\}
with
(A,B)
being the tuple of attribute names of
R/2
, and every SJFCQ
Q^{\star}
that is trivially entailed by every repair. It is implicit, however, in
[
23
]
that there exists a polynomial-time parsimonious reduction from
\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma^{\star},Q^{\star})
to
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
, which in turn implies that
\sharp\mathsf{MaxMatch}
does not admit an FPRAS.

6.1
Approximability Result

We proceed with item (1) of Theorem
8
: given a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and a CQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS. This is done by exploiting classical results from
[
19
]
.

FPRAS via Union of Sets.

We observe that
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
is an instantiation of the so-called
union of sets
problem
[
19
]
, which takes as input a succinct encoding of
n\geq 1
sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
, and asks for
|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}S_{i}|
.
Let us first recall the formal definition of the union of sets problem; as usual, for a set
S
, we write
\mathcal{P}(S)
for the powerset of
S
.

Consider some alphabet
\Gamma
(i.e., a finite set of symbols). A
union of sets specification
is a pair
\mathcal{U}=(f,\mu)
, where
f
is a function
\Gamma^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, and
\mu
is a function
\Gamma^{*}\times\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\Gamma^{*})
. Roughly speaking, using a string
x\in\Gamma^{*}
we can succinctly encode
n=f(x)
sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
, and each set
S_{i}
coincides with
\mu(x,i)
.

We are now ready to recall the union of sets problem; in what follows, let
\mathcal{U}=(f,\mu)
be a union of sets specification:

PROBLEM
:

\mathsf{UnionOfSets}(\mathcal{U})

INPUT
:

A string
x\in\Gamma^{*}

OUTPUT
:

\left|\bigcup_{i\in[f(x)]}\mu(x,i)\right|

Interestingly, for certain union of sets specifications, the above problem admits an FPRAS. Such union of sets specifications are the so-called tractable ones, which we now recall.
The union of sets specification
\mathcal{U}=(f,\mu)
is
tractable
if the following conditions on
f
and
\mu
hold:

-

f
is polynomial-time computable, and there is a polynomial function
\mathsf{pol}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
f(x)\leq\mathsf{pol}(|x|)
, for each
x\in\Gamma^{*}
, and

-

for each
x\in\Gamma^{*}
and
i\in[f(x)]
, (1)
|\mu(x,i)|
can be computed in polynomial time in
|x|
, (2) an element from
\mu(x,i)
can be sampled uniformly at random in polynomial time in
|x|
, and (3) checking whether
y\in\mu(x,i)
, for a given
y\in\Gamma^{*}
, can be done in polynomial time in
|x|
.

The following is well-known approximability result:

Theorem 9

(
[
19
]
)
.

For a tractable union of sets specification
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathsf{UnionOfSets}(\mathcal{U})
admits an FPRAS.

Hence, to prove that for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and CQ
Q
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS, it suffices to show that for every set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and CQ
Q
, there exists a tractable union of sets specification
\mathcal{U}_{\Sigma,Q}=(f,\mu)
such that, for every database
D
, with
n=f(D)
,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mu(D,i)|
.

Fix a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and a CQ
Q
. Now, consider the set

\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
of all homomorphic images of
Q
in
D
that are consistent w.r.t.
\Sigma
. Formally,
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is the set of sets of facts

\{h(Q)\mid h\text{ is a homomorphism from }Q\text{ to }D\text{ such that }h(Q)\models\Sigma\}.

Clearly, if
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is empty, then
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=0
. On the other hand, if
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is non-empty, then
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=\left|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H_{i}}(D)\right|
assuming that
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}\}
; by abuse of terminology, we treat each set
H_{i}
as a CQ consisting of only facts.
It is not difficult to show that checking whether
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is empty or not is feasible in polynomial time in
||D||

(recall that, in data complexity,
||Q||
is an integer constant)
.
Therefore,
w.l.o.g., we focus on the problem of computing

|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H_{i}}(D)|
when
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
.
The definition of the union of sets specification
\mathcal{U}_{\Sigma,Q}=(f,\mu)
, in this case, is straightforward. In particular,
\mathcal{U}_{\Sigma,Q}=(f,\mu)
is such that, for every database
D
, assuming
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}\}
,
f(D)=n
, and
\mu(D,i)=\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H_{i}}(D)
, for each
i\in[n]
. Clearly,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=\left|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H_{i}}(D)\right|=\left|\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mu(D,i)\right|
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
f
is computable in polynomial time and that
f(D)
is bounded by a polynomial.

From the above discussion, and from
Theorem
9
, we conclude that showing the existence of an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
boils down to showing the following result:

Proposition 6
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and a CQ
Q
. Given a database
D
with
\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, the following hold for each
H\in\mathsf{hom}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
:

1.

We can compute
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
in polynomial time in
||D||
.

2.

We can sample elements of
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
uniformly at random in polynomial time in
||D||
.

3.

Given a database
D^{\prime}
, we can check whether
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
in polynomial time in
||D||
.

Proof (Sketch).
We prove the rather easy items (1) and (3), whereas for item (2) we sketch the high-level idea and the full proof is deferred to the appendix.

Item (1).

We observe that
H
is a set of facts such that
H\models\Sigma
and
H\subseteq D
. Hence,
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)\neq\emptyset
, and each of its repairs contains
H
; recall that we may treat
H
as a CQ.
Thus,
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
is precisely the set of repairs of
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
that do not contain any fact
f\in D
such that
H\cup\{f\}\not\models\Sigma
, i.e.,
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)=\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D^{\prime})
, where
D^{\prime}=\{f\in D\mid H\cup\{f\}\not\models\Sigma\}
. The database
D^{\prime}
is clearly computable in polynomial time in
||D||
, and thus, by Proposition
1
, we can compute
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D^{\prime})
in polynomial time in
||D||
, as needed.

Item (2).

We first show that we can sample elements of
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
uniformly at random in polynomial time in
||D||
.
In particular, we show that there is a randomized algorithm
\mathsf{Sample}
that takes as input
D
and
\Sigma
, runs in polynomial time in
||D||
, and produces a random variable
\mathsf{Sample}(D,\Sigma)
such that

\Pr(\mathsf{Sample}(D,\Sigma)=D^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)},

for every
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
.
This exploits the fact that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
can be computed in polynomial time in
||D||
, and that for each relation name
R
, the set
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma_{R}}(D_{R})
has a convenient recursive definition based on the
(R,\Lambda)
-blocktree of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
, where
\Lambda
is the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
; we can assume that
\Sigma
is canonical, and thus,
\Lambda
is well-defined.
Now, by using the same idea as for item (1), we convert the sampler for
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
into a sampler for
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
. The details are in the appendix.

Item (3).

This item holds trivially since checking whether
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,H}(D)
boils down to checking whether
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
and
H\subseteq D^{\prime}
.

As previously discussed, our blocktrees align with the cotrees of cographs. Consequently, the proof of point (2) of Proposition
6
establishes that we can efficiently sample, uniformly at random, maximal independent sets from a cograph.

Monte Carlo Sampling.

At this point, one may wonder whether Monte Carlo sampling would lead to an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
. In other words, given a database
D
,
\epsilon>0
, and
0<\delta<1
, sample uniformly at random, by using the sampler for
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
discussed above, a certain number
N
of repairs from
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
, and output the number
\frac{S}{N}\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
, where
S
is the number of sampled repairs that entail
Q
. For this approach to lead to an FPRAS, the number of samples
N
must be large enough to provide the desired error and probabilistic guarantees, but should be also bounded by a polynomial of
||D||
in order to ensure that the running time is polynomial.
To this end, we need a polynomial
\mathsf{pol}
such that, for every database
D
,
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\geq\frac{1}{\mathsf{pol}(||D||)}
whenever
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)>0
. However, we can show that this is not always the case:

Theorem 10
.

There exists a set of FDs
\Sigma
with an LHS chain, an atomic CQ
Q
, and a family of databases
\{D_{n}\}_{n>0}
with
|D_{n}|=2n+1
such that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D_{n},\Sigma}(Q)=\frac{1}{2^{n}+1}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a relation name with attributes
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4})
. We define the set of FDs

\Sigma\ =\ \{R:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2},R:\{A_{1},A_{3}\}\rightarrow A_{4}\},

which clearly has an LHS chain, and the atomic Boolean query

Q\ =\ \textrm{Ans}()\ \text{:-}\ \exists x\exists y\exists z\,R(x,x,y,z).

For each
n>0
, we define
D_{n}
as the following database:

\{R(a,a,a,a)\}\cup\{R(a,b,c_{i},d_{1}),R(a,b,c_{i},d_{2})\}_{i\in[n]},

where
a
,
b
,
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
, and
c_{i}
, for
i\in[n]
, are distinct constants. Clearly, the database
D_{n}
contains
2n+1
facts. Moreover, it is not difficult to verify that the set of repairs
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{n})
contains only the database
D^{\prime}=\{R(a,a,a,a)\}
, and a database of the form

\{R(a,b,c_{i},d_{\mu(i)})\}_{i\in[n]},

for each function
\mu:[n]\rightarrow\{1,2\}
. Hence,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{n})=2^{n}+1
. Moreover,
D^{\prime}
is the only repair in
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{n})
such that
D^{\prime}\models Q
. Therefore,
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D_{n},\Sigma}(Q)=\frac{1}{1+2^{n}}
, and the claim follows.

6.2
Inapproximability Result

We now discuss the proof of item (2) of Theorem
8
. Recall that by proving item (2), we immediately get item (3) of Theorem
8
due to Lemma
1
.
The proof employs the technique of
gap amplification
used to prove inapproximability of optimization problems. The idea is to consider an
NP
-complete decision problem
\Pi
, and define a polynomial-time computable function
\mathsf{db}
that maps instances of
\Pi
to databases such that the following holds: for any
yes
instance
I_{Y}
of
\Pi
, and any
no
instance
I_{N}
of
\Pi
, the “gap” between the numbers
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}(I_{Y}))
and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}(I_{N}))
is so large that an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
could be used, by employing a small enough error
\epsilon
, to distinguish between
yes
and
no
instances of
\Pi
with high probability, and thus, placing the
NP
-complete problem
\Pi
in
BPP
.

The GapSAT Problem.

We are going to exploit the problem
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
for some fixed
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
. This is a promise problem that takes as input a Boolean formula
\varphi
in 3CNF with exactly three literals in each clause, where each variable appears at most once in each clause, and asks whether
\varphi
is satisfiable.
The promise is that if
\varphi
is unsatisfiable, then every truth assignment makes at most
\frac{7}{8}+\gamma
of the clauses of
\varphi
true.
We know that, for
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
,
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
is
NP
-complete. This is a consequence of Håstad’s 3-bit PCP Theorem, and it is explicitly stated in
[
16
, Theorem 6.5]
.

From 3CNF Formulae to Databases.

Our goal is to show, via the technique of gap amplification, that the existence of an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
implies that
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
is in
\mathsf{BPP}
, for some
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
, which in turn establishes item (2) of Theorem
8
.
To this end, we first define a polynomial-time computable function
\mathsf{db}_{k}
, for some
k>0
, that maps 3CNF formulas to databases. The integer
k
is what we call the “growing factor”, which we are going to choose later.

Consider a 3CNF formula
\varphi=C_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge C_{m}
, where
C_{i}=\ell^{1}_{i}\vee\ell^{2}_{i}\vee\ell^{3}_{i}
, for each
i\in[m]
. The intention underlying the database
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
is as follows: the number of repairs of
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
w.r.t.
\Sigma
should be proportional to the number of clauses of
\phi
that can be satisfied by a truth assignment. We would further like to control by “how much” the number of repairs grows for each clause that is satisfied. Let
\mathsf{var}(\ell^{j}_{i})
be the Boolean variable of
\ell^{j}_{i}
. Moreover, for a truth value
v\in\{0,1\}
, we write
\mathsf{lval}_{v}(\ell^{j}_{i})
for the truth value of
\ell^{j}_{i}
, when its variable is assigned the value
v
; for example, if
v=0
, then
\mathsf{lval}_{v}(\neg x)=1
.
The database
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
is defined as the union of the two databases
D^{k}_{T}
and
D^{k}_{C}
over
R
shown below.
Note that, for the sake of presentation, we rename the attribute names of
R
;
A_{1}
becomes
Var
,
A_{2}
becomes
VValue
,
A_{3}
becomes
Clause
, and
A_{4}
becomes
LValue
. Under this new naming scheme, the FDs of
\Sigma
are

R:\text{\rm Var}\rightarrow\text{\rm VValue}\qquad R:\text{\rm Clause}\rightarrow\text{\rm LValue}.

The databases
D^{k}_{T}
and
D^{k}_{C}
follow, where
\star
is an arbitrary constant:

Var

VValue

Clause

LValue

\mathsf{var}(\ell^{1}_{i})

0

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{1}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{1}_{i})

\left\}\vbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}\textnormal{
{
$i\in[m]$, $j\in[k]$
}
}\right.

\mathsf{var}(\ell^{1}_{i})

1

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{1}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{1}_{i})
