\mathsf{var}(\ell^{2}_{i})

0

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{2}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{2}_{i})

\mathsf{var}(\ell^{2}_{i})

1

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{2}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{2}_{i})

\mathsf{var}(\ell^{3}_{i})

0

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{3}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{0}(\ell^{3}_{i})

\mathsf{var}(\ell^{3}_{i})

1

\langle C^{j}_{i},\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{3}_{i})\rangle

\mathsf{lval}_{1}(\ell^{3}_{i})

The Database

D^{k}_{T}

Var

VValue

Clause

LValue

\star

\star

\langle C^{j}_{i},1\rangle

0

\left\}\vbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}\textnormal{
{
$i\in[m]$, $j\in[k]$
}
}\right.

The Database

D^{k}_{C}

Intuitively, for each clause
C_{i}
of
\varphi
, the database
D^{k}_{T}
stores
k
copies
C^{1}_{i},\ldots,C^{k}_{i}
of
C_{i}
. Each copy is stored via 6 tuples, where, for each literal of
C_{i}
, its variable is stored twice, specifying one of two possibles values it can get assigned (i.e.,
0
or
1
). Moreover, the actual truth value that the literal has w.r.t. to the particular value given to its variable is also stored, together with the clause copy. This same literal value is replicated under the attribute
LValue
; its purpose is explained below. The set of 6 tuples in
D^{k}_{T}
mentioning a particular copy
C^{j}_{i}
of a clause
C_{i}
is called a
C^{j}_{i}
-gadget
.

Regarding
D^{k}_{C}
, it stores one tuple for each copy
C^{j}_{i}
of a clause
C_{i}
. Each tuple in
D^{k}_{C}
containing
C^{j}_{i}
causes a conflict when a truth assignment of
\varphi
“chooses” to assign
1
to at least one literal of the
C^{j}_{i}
-gadget. Due to this, the number of repairs of
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
w.r.t.
\Sigma
is proportional to the number of clauses satisfied by the truth assignment, and the growing factor is controlled by
k
.

Inapproximabilty via Gap Amplification.

We now discuss that there are
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
and
k>0
such that, for every two 3CNF formulae
\varphi_{Y}
and
\varphi_{N}
that are
yes
and
no
instances of
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
, respectively, the “gap” between the numbers
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi_{Y}))
and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi_{N}))
is indeed large enough that allows us to place
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
in
BPP
by exploiting an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
. This relies on a crucial property that intuitively can be described as follows.

Consider a 3CNF formula
\varphi
. The FD
R:\text{Var}\rightarrow\text{VValue}
of
\Sigma
simply states that a Boolean variable of
\varphi
must be assigned only one of the two values
0
or
1
. If only this FD was considered, we would have an one-to-one correspondence between the truth assignments of
\varphi
and the repairs of
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
w.r.t.
\Sigma
. The FD
R:\text{\rm Clause}\rightarrow\text{\rm LValue}
forces a truth assignment
\tau
to induce an exponential number of repairs in the number of clauses that are satisfied by
\tau
due to the inconsistencies between the tuples of
D^{k}_{T}
and
D^{k}_{C}
w.r.t. the FD
R:\text{\rm Clause}\rightarrow\text{\rm LValue}
.
This property is formalized by the next technical lemma; its proof can be found in the appendix. For a truth assignment
\tau
of
\varphi
, we denote by
c_{\tau}
the number of clauses of
\varphi
that are true w.r.t.
\tau
.

Lemma 19
.

Consider a 3CNF formula
\varphi
. There exists a function
\mathsf{Map}
from truth assignments of
\varphi
to sets of databases such that the following hold:

1.

For every truth assignment
\tau
of
\varphi
,
\mathsf{Map}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{k}_{\varphi})
.

2.

For every
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{k}_{\varphi})
, there is a truth assignment
\tau
of
\varphi
such that
D^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Map}(\tau)
.

3.

For every truth assignment
\tau
of
\varphi
,
|\mathsf{Map}(\tau)|=2^{k\cdot c_{\tau}}
.

Consequently, each truth assignment
\tau
of
\varphi
corresponds to
2^{k\cdot c_{\tau}}
repairs of
D^{k}_{\varphi}
, and every repair has a corresponding truth assignment. With Lemma
19
in place, it is easy to establish the following key technical result.
We write
\varphi\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
(resp.,
\varphi\not\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
) to indicate that
\varphi
is a
yes
(resp.,
no
) instance of
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 20

(
Bounds
)
.

Consider a 3CNF formula
\varphi
with
n>0
variables and
m>0
clauses. For every
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
and
k>0
:

1.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
, then
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\geq 2^{k\cdot m}
.

2.

If
\varphi\not\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
, then
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\leq 2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}
.

Proof.

We first establish item (1). Assume that
\varphi\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
, and let
\tau
be a truth assignment that satisfies
\varphi
. By item (1) of Lemma
19
,
|\mathsf{Map}(\tau)|\leq\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))
, and since
\tau
satisfies all
m
clauses of
\varphi
, by item (3) of Lemma
19
,
|\mathsf{Map}(\tau)|=2^{k\cdot m}
. Hence,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\geq 2^{k\cdot m}
.

We now establish item (2). Assume that
\varphi\not\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
. By items (1) and (2) of Lemma
19
,
\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))=\bigcup\limits_{\tau}\mathsf{Map}(\tau)
. Hence, by item (3) of Lemma
19
, it holds that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\ \leq\ \sum\limits_{\tau}2^{k\cdot c_{\tau}}.

Since
\varphi
is a no instance, for any truth assignment
\tau
,
c_{\tau}\leq(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot m
. Consequently, we that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\ \leq\ \sum\limits_{\tau}2^{k\cdot(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot m}=2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m},

and the claim follows.

We now show that the“gap” between the bounds from Lemma
20
can be sufficiently large.

Lemma 21

(
Gap
)
.

Consider a 3CNF formula with
n>0
variables and
m>0
clauses. There are
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and an integer
k>0
such that
(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k
is an integer, and

\dfrac{2^{k\cdot m}}{2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}}>\dfrac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}.

Proof.

The claim holds, e.g., for
\gamma=\frac{1}{16}
,
\epsilon=\frac{1}{3}
, and
k=112
; note that
0<n\leq 3\cdot m
.

We now have all the ingredients for concluding the proof of item (2) of Theorem
8
.
Let
\gamma
,
\epsilon
and
k
be the numbers provided by Lemma
21
.
Assume
\mathsf{A}
is an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma)
. We can place
\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
in
\mathsf{BPP}
via the randomized algorithm
\mathsf{BPPAlgo}_{\gamma,\epsilon,k}
that takes as input a 3CNF formula
\varphi
with
n>0
variables and
m>0
clauses, and does the following:

-

if
\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})>(1+\epsilon)\cdot 2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}
, then
Accept
;

-

otherwise,
Reject
.

It is clear that
\mathsf{BPPAlgo}_{\gamma,\epsilon,k}(\varphi)
runs in polynomial time in
||\varphi||
since both the database
\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)
and
2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}
can be computed in polynomial time in
||\varphi||
; recall that, by Lemma
21
,
(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k
is an integer.
It remains to prove that the algorithm is correct, for which we exploit Lemmas
20
and
21
.
We only consider the case when
\varphi\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
, as the case
\varphi\not\in\mathsf{Gap3SAT}_{\gamma}
is similar. The probability that
\mathsf{BPPAlgo}_{\gamma,\epsilon,k}
is wrong, i.e.,
rejects

\varphi
is

p\ =\ \Pr\left(\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot 2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}\right).

We can conclude, by combining Lemma
21
and Lemma
20
, that

(1+\epsilon)\cdot 2^{n}\cdot 2^{(\frac{7}{8}+\gamma)\cdot k\cdot m}\ <\ (1-\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi)),

and therefore,

p\ \leq\ \Pr\left(\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})<(1-\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\right).

Recall that
\Pr(E_{1})\leq\Pr(E_{1}\cup E_{2})
, for any two events
E_{1},E_{2}
. Thus,
p\leq\Pr(E_{1}\cup E_{2})
, where
E_{1}
is the event
\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})<(1-\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))
, and
E_{2}
is the event
\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})>(1+\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))
.
Thus,
\Pr(E_{1}\cup E_{2})
is the probability that the event

(1-\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))\leq\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi),\epsilon,\frac{1}{3})\ \leq\ (1+\epsilon)\cdot\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(\mathsf{db}_{k}(\varphi))

does not hold. Therefore, by definition of
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{BPPAlgo}_{\gamma,\epsilon,k}
rejects
\varphi
with probability at most
\frac{1}{3}
, i.e., accepts with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, and the claim follows.

6.3
The Relative Frequency Problem

Before concluding this section, let us discuss the relative frequency problem, which is interesting in its own right.
In Section
5
, for establishing Theorem
2
, we focused on the problem of computing the relative frequency of a CQ w.r.t. a database and a set of FDs.
Recall that the relative frequency of a CQ
Q
w.r.t. a database
D
and a set
\Sigma
of FDs is the ratio that computes the percentage of repairs that entail it, that is, the ratio

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)},

and the relative frequency problem for a set
\Sigma
of FDs and a CQ
Q
is defined as follows:

PROBLEM
:

\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)

INPUT
:

A database
D

OUTPUT
:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)

Unlike Theorem
2
, Theorem
8
(the main result of Section
6
) was shown by directly considering the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
, without relying on the relative frequency problem.
An interesting question that comes up is whether the relative frequency problem is approximable or not; the notion of FPRAS for
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
is defined in the obvious way.
Interestingly, by exploiting Theorem
8
, we can show the following result concerning the approximability of the relative frequency problem:

Theorem 11
.

The following hold:

1.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence), and a CQ
Q
.
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS.

2.

There exists a set
\Sigma
of FDs and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
does not admit an FPRAS, unless
\text{\rm NP}\subseteq\text{\rm BPP}
.

Proof (Sketch).
Item (1) is a consequence of item (1) of Theorem
8
, which states that
\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS, and Proposition
1
, which states that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
is computable in polynomial time in
||D||
. Indeed, since the problem of computing the numerator admits an FPRAS, and the problem of computing the denominator can be solved in polynomial time, it is easy to convert the FPRAS for computing the numerator (i.e.,
\mathsf{Repairs}(\Sigma,Q)
) to an FPRAS for
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

We now present the main ideas underlying the proof of item (2); the details can be found in the appendix. By item (2) of Theorem
8
,
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
with
\hat{\Sigma}=\{R:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2},R:A_{3}\rightarrow A_{4}\}
, where
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4})
is the tuple of attribute names of
R/4
, does not admit an FPRAS (unless
\text{\rm NP}\subseteq\text{\rm BPP}
). Therefore, to establish item (2), it suffices to provide an approximation-preserving reduction from
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some set
\Sigma
of FDs and an SJFCQ
Q
. The latter means that
\Sigma
and
Q
should be devised in such a way that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
admits an FPRAS implies
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
admits an FPRAS.
To this end, we are going to define
\Sigma
and
Q
over
\{R^{\prime}/6\}
in a way that the following holds: for every database
D
over
\{R\}
, we can construct in polynomial time in
||D||
a database
D^{\prime}
over
\{R^{\prime}/6\}
such that the following equation (which we dubbed
\blacklozenge
) holds:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \frac{1}{1+\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(D)}.

Then, by exploiting the equation
\blacklozenge
, the fact that
D^{\prime}
can be constructed in polynomial time, and the assumption that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
admits in FPRAS, we are going to devise an FPRAS for the problem
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
, which will complete the proof of item (2).

Assume that
(A,B,A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4})
is the tuple of attribute names of
R^{\prime}/6
. Let
\Sigma
be the set

\{R^{\prime}:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2},\,\,R^{\prime}:A_{3}\rightarrow A_{4},\,\,R^{\prime}:A\rightarrow B\}.

Now, the SJFCQ
Q
is defined as the Boolean query

\textrm{Ans}\ \text{:-}\ R^{\prime}(x,x,x,x,x,x).

In simple words,
Q
asks whether there exists a fact such that all the attributes have the same value.
We proceed to show that
\Sigma
and
Q
enjoy the desired property.

Consider an arbitrary database
D
over
\{R\}
. We define the database
D^{\prime}
over
\{R^{\prime}\}
as

\{R^{\prime}(a,b,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\mid R(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in D\}\ \cup\ \{R^{\prime}(a,a,a,a,a,a)\}

with
a,b
being constants not in
\mathsf{dom}(D)
.
It is clear that
D^{\prime}
can be constructed in polynomial time in
||D||
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that, by construction,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\prime})=1+\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(D)
, and only one of the repairs of
D^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
entails the Boolean CQ
Q
, that is, the repair consisting only of the fact
R^{\prime}(a,a,a,a,a,a)
. Therefore, the desired equation
\blacklozenge
holds.

We proceed to devise an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
by exploiting the equation
\blacklozenge
, the fact that
D^{\prime}
can be constructed in polynomial time, and the FPRAS
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
, which we assume it exists.
Given a database
D
over
\{R\}
,
\epsilon>0
, and
0<\delta<1
, we define
\mathsf{A}
as the procedure:

-

compute the database
D^{\prime}
from
D
;

-

let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}
;

-

let
r=\max\left\{\frac{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}{2\cdot(1+2^{|D|})},\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(D^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\delta)\right\}
;

-

output
\frac{1}{r}-1
.

It clear that the above procedure runs in polynomial time in
||D||
,
1/\epsilon
, and
\log(1/\delta)
.
It can be also shown that
\mathsf{A}
correctly approximates the integer
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(D)
, which in turn implies that
\mathsf{A}
is an FPRAS for
\sharp\mathsf{Repairs}(\hat{\Sigma})
, as needed.
The latter heavily exploits the fact that
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
is an FPRAS for
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
, and the equation
\blacklozenge
shown above; the full proof can be found in the appendix.

7
Conclusions and Future Work

This work focused on the exact and approximate counting of database repairs.
Concerning the exact version of the problem, we have lifted the
FP
/
\sharp
P
-complete dichotomy in the case of primary keys and self-join-free CQs from
[
25
]
to the more general case of FDs and self-join-free CQs (Theorem
2
).
Concerning the approximate version of the problem, although we have not provided a complete approximability/inapproximability classification, we established that (i) the problem admits an FPRAS in the case of FDs with an LHS chain (up to equivalence) and CQs (even with self-joins), but (ii) it does not admit an FPRAS (under a standard complexity assumption) in the case of arbitrary FDs; note that the latter holds for
every
self-join-free CQ (Theorem
8
). These are crucial steps towards a complete approximability/inapproximability classification. Recall that establishing such a classification is highly non-trivial as it will resolve the challenging open problem of whether counting maximal matchings in a bipartite graph admits an FPRAS.
Note that on our way to understand the problem of counting database repairs, we also established analogous result for the relative frequency problem, which is interesting in its own right.

The research problems in the context of counting repairs that remain open are the following:

1.

Lift the dichotomy of Theorem
2
to arbitrary CQs with self-joins.

2.

Establish an approximability/inapproximability dichotomy for FDs and CQs (with or without self-joins).

These are highly non-trivial problems that deserve our attention.

Appendix

We provide full proofs and additional explanations for the technical results given in the main body of the paper that need to be proven. For the sake of readability, we repeat the statements.

Appendix A
Proofs of Section
5

In this section, we provide the missing proofs of Section
5
. To this end, we first establish some auxiliary technical lemmas that will be used throughout the proofs.
Recall that we consider canonical FD sets
\Sigma
that have a single LHS chain
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1},\ldots,R:X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
for every relation name
R
of the schema such that:

1.

X_{i}\subsetneq X_{i+1}
for each
i\in[n]
,

2.

X_{i}\cap Y_{j}=\emptyset
for each
i,j\in[n]
, and

3.

Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}=\emptyset
for each
i,j\in[n]
with
i\neq j
.

We use the following notation. Let
\Sigma
be an FD set with an LHS chain and let
Q
be an SJFCQ. For every atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
, and for every FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the LHS chain
R:X_{1}\rightarrow Y_{1},\dots,R:X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
of
\Sigma_{R}
, we denote by
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
the positions corresponding to the attributes of
X_{j}
, i.e.,
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}=\{(R,A)\mid A\in X_{j}\}
, and by
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
the positions that correspond to the attributes of
Y_{j}
, i.e.,
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}=\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y_{j}\}
. We also denote by
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
.

A.1
Auxiliary Technical Lemmas

We first show that the facts of
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
have no impact on the number of repairs that entail
Q
. Recall that for a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
, the database
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
contains all the
R
-facts
f
of
D
such that for some FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
:

1.

f[A]=\alpha[A]
for every
A\in X_{j}
,

2.

f[A]\neq\alpha[A]
for some
A\in Y_{j}
,

where
\alpha
is the
R
-atom of
Q
.

Lemma 22
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. Then,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

Proof.

If
D\not\models Q
, then
(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})\not\models Q
, and therefore,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})=0
. Hence, from now on, we focus on the case where
D\models Q
. We show that a database
E
is a repair of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
if and only if
E
is a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
E
be a repair of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
. Thus, there exists a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D
such that
R(h(\bar{z}))\in E
for every atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
. Let
f=R(h(\bar{z}))
for some atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
. Clearly,
f
agrees with
R(\bar{z})
on the constants in the positions of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
and
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
for every FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
. On the other hand, for every
R
-fact
g\in D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, there exists an FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
such that
g
agrees with
R(\bar{z})
on the constants in the positions of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
, but disagrees with
R(\bar{z})
on a constant in some position of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Therefore,
f
and
g
agree on all the values of the attributes in
X_{j}
but disagree on the value of at least one attribute in
Y_{j}
. We conclude that
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
. Since
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
contains only facts over relations that occur in
Q
and the above argument holds for each such relation, it holds that
E\cap D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}=\emptyset
, and
E
is also a consistent subset of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
. Since every fact of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
also occurs in
D
, it is rather straightforward that if
E
is maximal in
D
then it is also maximal in
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
. Thus,
E
is a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
that entails
Q
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Conversely, let
E
be a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
. Clearly,
E
is also a consistent subset of
D
that entails
Q
; hence, it is only left to show that it is maximal. If we could add a fact from
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
to
E
without violating consistency, we would get a contradiction to the fact that
E
is a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
. Moreover, the same argument used in the proof of the first direction shows that we cannot add a fact of
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
to
E
without violating consistency; since
E
entails
Q
, there exists a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D
such that
R(h(\bar{z}))\subseteq E
for every atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
, and every
R
-fact
g\in D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
violates
\Sigma_{R}
jointly with the fact
R(h(\bar{z}))
. Hence,
E
is a maximal consistent subset of
D
.

We next show that the facts of
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
have no impact on the relative frequency of
Q
w.r.t.
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
\Sigma
. Recall that for a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
, the database
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
contains all the
R
-facts
f
of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
such that for some FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
,
f[A]\neq\alpha[A]
for an attribute
A\in X_{j}
,
where
\alpha
is the
R
-atom of
Q
.

Lemma 23
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. Then,

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}),\Sigma}(Q).

Proof.

Since every
R
-fact
f
of
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
disagrees with the atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
on at least one constant (that occurs in a position of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
for some FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
), there is no homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
such that
f=R(h(\bar{z}))
. We now show that no two
R
-facts
g\in(D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}))
and
f\in D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
jointly violate
\Sigma_{R}
. Assume, towards a contradiction, that
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
.
Since
f\in D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, for some FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
, it holds that
f
disagrees with
R(\bar{z})
on a constant at a position of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
. Since
g\not\in(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
, the fact
g
agrees with the atom
R(\bar{z})
on all the constants at the positions of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
. Therefore,
f
and
g
disagree on the value of at least one attribute of
X_{j}
, and for every
k\geq j
, it holds that
\{f,g\}\models R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
, as
X_{j}\subseteq X_{k}
. Therefore, the only possible case is that
\{f,g\}\not\models R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
for some
k<j
. Note that the FD
R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
also belongs to the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
.

As said above,
g
agrees with the atom
R(\bar{z})
on all the constants at the positions of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
. Since
X_{k}\subset X_{j}
,
g
agrees with
R(\bar{z})
on all the constants at the positions of
I_{R,k}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
. Since
g\not\in D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, we get that
g
also agrees with
R(\bar{z})
on all the constants at the positions of
I_{R,k}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Hence,
f
agrees with
R(\bar{z})
on all the constants at the positions of
I_{R,k}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
, but disagrees with
R(\bar{z})
on a constant at a position of
I_{R,k}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, which implies that
f\in D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
. This is a contradiction since
f\in D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cap D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}=\emptyset
.

We have shown that for every atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
and every homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, the fact
R(h(\bar{z}))
belongs to
D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
. Moreover, no fact of
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
is in conflict with a fact of
D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
. Therefore, it holds that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}))\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})

and

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}))\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

We can then conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}),\Sigma}(Q),

and the claim follows.

The following is an immediate corollary of Lemmas
22
and
23
.

Lemma

7
.

For a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
,

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}),\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

The next lemma generalizes a property of primary keys: two
R
-facts that disagree on the value of an attribute in the left-hand side of the key of
R
cannot jointly violate the constraints.

Lemma 24
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. For every relation name
R
that occurs in
Q
, if two
R
-facts
f,g\in D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
are such that
f[A]\neq g[A]
for some primary-lhs position
(R,A)
, then
\mathord{\{f,g\}}\models\Sigma_{R}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, the
R
-facts
f
and
g
agree with the atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
on all the constants that occur at the positions of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}\cup I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
for every FD
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
.
Hence,
f
and
g
do not jointly violate any of these FDs. If
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary FD, these are the only FDs in
\Sigma_{R}
, and the claim follows.
If
\Sigma_{R}
does have a primary FD, for every FD
R:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k}
outside the primary prefix, i.e., every FD that occurs after the primary FD
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
,
f
and
g
disagree on the value of at least one attribute of
X_{k}
since
X_{i_{R}}\subseteq X_{k}
and
f
and
g
disagree on the value of some primary-lhs position, and thus,
\mathord{\{f,g\}}\models\Sigma_{R}
.

We finish the section with the following lemma.

Lemma 25
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
with
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
. If
D\models Q
, then

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

Proof.

We will show that a database
E
is a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entails
Q
if and only if
E
is a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
. The “only if” direction is straightforward; hence, we focus on the other direction.
Let
E
be a repair of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
w.r.t.
\Sigma
. By contradiction, assume that
E\not\models Q
. Since
D\models Q
, there is a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
D
. Let
R(\bar{z})
be an atom of
Q
. By definition,
R(\bar{z})
associates a constant with every position of
I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{lhs}}\cup I_{R,j}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
for the FDs
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
. Therefore, the fact

R(h(\bar{z}))
of
D
uses these constants for the corresponding attributes. Since every fact of
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
disagrees with
R(\bar{z})
on at least one of those constants,
R(h(\bar{z}))\in(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})
, and
h
is a homomorphism from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
.

Now, assume that the atoms of
Q
are
R_{1}(\bar{z}_{1}),\dots,R_{n}(\bar{z}_{n})
and let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
such that
|\mathord{\{R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})\mid R_{t}(h(\bar{z}_{t}))\in E\}}|=k
for some
k
, and there is no other homomorhism
h^{\prime}
from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
such that
|\mathord{\{R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})\mid R_{t}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{t}))\in E\}}|>k
. Note that
k<n
due to our assumption that
E\not\models Q
. Let
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
be an atom of
Q
such that
R_{t}(h(\bar{z}_{t}))\not\in E
. We denote
f=R_{t}(h(\bar{z}_{t}))
. Since we know that
f\in(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})
and
f\not\in E
, and
E
is a maximal consistent subset of
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, there exists a fact
g\in E
such that
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R_{t}}
.

For every
R_{t}:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R_{t}}
, we know that
f
agrees with the atom
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
of
Q
on the constants associated with the attributes of
X_{j}\cup Y_{j}
. Since
g\not\in D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
, it is either the case that
g
also agrees with
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
on these constants, or
g
disagrees with
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
on some value in an attribute of
X_{j}
. In both cases, we have that
\{f,g\}\models(R_{t}:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j})
. Note that if
R_{t}
has no primary FD, we can conclude at this point that there is no
g\in E
such that
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R_{t}}
(because all the FDs of
\Sigma_{R_{t}}
belong to the primary prefix), and we immediately get a contradiction to the fact that
E
is a repair (hence, maximal). Thus, in the rest of the proof, we consider the case where
R_{t}
has a primary FD. In this case,
\{f,g\}\not\models(R_{t}:X_{k}\rightarrow Y_{k})
for some FD outside the primary prefix, and since
X_{i_{R_{t}}}\subseteq X_{k}
for each such FD, we conclude that
f
and
g
agree on the values of all the attributes in
X_{i_{R_{t}}}
. Note that in this case,
f
and
g
also agree on the values of all the attributes of
X_{j}
for each FD
R_{t}:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R_{t}}
since
X_{j}\subset X_{i_{R_{t}}}
. Therefore,
g
agrees with the atom
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
on the constants associated with the attributes of
X_{j}\cup Y_{j}
for each such FD.

Let
h^{\prime}:\mathsf{var}(Q)\cup\mathsf{const}(Q)\rightarrow\mathsf{dom}(D)
be a function such that
h^{\prime}(c)=c
for every constant
c
and
h^{\prime}(x)=h(x)
for every variable
x
except those that occur at non-primary-lhs positions of
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
. For every variable
y
that occurs at a non-primary-lhs position
(R_{t},A)
of
R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})
, we define
h^{\prime}(y)=g[A]
. Since
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, each such variable occurs only once in the query. Therefore, for every
\ell\neq t
, it holds that
R_{\ell}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{\ell}))=R_{\ell}(h(\bar{z}_{\ell}))
and
R_{\ell}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{\ell}))\in(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})
. Moreover,
R_{t}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{t}))\in(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})
because
R_{t}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{t}))=g
; thus,
h^{\prime}
is a homomorphism from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
. Clearly, we have that
R_{\ell}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{\ell}))\in E
whenever
R_{\ell}(h(\bar{z}_{\ell}))\in E
, and it also holds that
R_{t}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{t}))\in E
because
g\in E
. Therefore, we obtained a homomorphism from
Q
to
D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
such that
|\mathord{\{R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t})\mid R_{t}(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}_{t}))\in E\}}|=k+1>k
, which is a contradiction to our assumption.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
3

Proposition

3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of FDs, and
Q
an SJFCQ with
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
. Given a database
D
, it holds that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
is computable in polynomial time.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma
has an LHS chain,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)
can be computed in polynomial time in
||D||
, and the computation of
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)
boils down to the computation of
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)
. Hence, it remains to show that
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)
can be computed in polynomial time if
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
. If
D\not\models Q
, then clearly no repair of
D
entails
Q
, and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=0
. Therefore, in the rest of the proof, we focus on the case where
D\models Q
. Our proof for this case is by a reduction to the problem of counting repairs w.r.t.
\Sigma
.
In particular,
Lemma
22
implies that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

Moreover, Lemma
25
implies that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

Hence, we conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}).

The claim then follows from Proposition
1
.

Observe that Proposition
3
also captures the case where the atoms of
Q
use only constants (and no variables), since, by definition, all the positions are primary-lhs positions, and
Q
has no complex part. In the corresponding algorithm for primary keys, the case of a single atom with no variables is considered separately
[
25
]
.

A.3
Proof of Lemma
3

Let us first recall that

D^{\Sigma,Q}\ =\ D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}=\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}\left(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\right)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

Lemma

3
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, and there is
x\in\mathsf{var}(Q)
such that
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, then

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \left(1-\prod_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\left(1-\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\right)\right)\ \times\ \mathsf{R}_{D,\Sigma,Q}.

Proof.

We prove that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ 1-\prod_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\left(1-\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\right)

and then the desired result follows from Lemma
7
.

Let
Q_{1}=Q\setminus\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, and let
E_{1}
be the subset of
D^{\Sigma,Q}
that contains all the facts over the relations of
Q_{1}
. Let
E_{2}
be the subset of
D^{\Sigma,Q}
that contains all the facts over the relations that do not occur in
Q
. Finally, let
E_{3}
be the subset of
D^{\Sigma,Q}
that contains all the facts over the relations that occur in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
. We denote these relations by
R_{1},\dots,R_{k}
. For simplicity, we assume that the variable
x
occurs at the left-most position of each one of the atoms
R_{1}(\bar{z}_{1}),\dots,R_{k}(\bar{z}_{k})
of
Q
, and that this position is a primary-lhs position. (Recall that
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(R_{t}(\bar{z}_{t}))
for every
t\in[k]
.)

For every
c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
, let
E_{3}^{c}
be the subset of
E_{3}
that contains all the facts over the relations
R_{1},\dots,R_{k}
that use the value
c
at the left-most position. Note that two
R_{t}
-facts
f\in E_{3}^{c}
and
g\in E_{3}^{b}
for
c\neq b
cannot jointly violate
\Sigma_{R_{t}}
. This is because
f,g\in D\setminus\left(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}\right)
and they use a different constant at some primary-lhs position; hence, Lemma
24
implies that
\mathord{\{f,g\}}\models\Sigma_{R_{t}}
.

Now, consider the query
Q_{1}
. Since it holds that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q_{1})=\emptyset
and
D^{\Sigma,Q}
contains no facts of
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
,
Lemma
25
implies that all the repairs of
E_{1}
entail
Q_{1}
. Moreover, if
J_{1},\dots,J_{m}
are the repairs of
E_{1}
and
K
is a repair of
E_{3}
, it holds that if
J_{\ell}\cup K
entails
Q
for some
\ell\in\mathord{\{1,\dots,m\}}
, then
J_{\ell}\cup K
entails
Q
for all
\ell\in\mathord{\{1,\dots,m\}}
. This holds since
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q_{1})=\emptyset
; thus, the only variables of
Q_{1}
that occur more than once in
Q
appear at primary-lhs positions of the atoms in
Q_{1}
, and the same holds for the constants that occur in
Q_{1}
. Moreover, Lemma
24
implies that for every relation name
R
in
Q
, two
R
-facts in
D^{\Sigma,Q}
are in conflict only if they agree on all the attributes corresponding to primary-lhs positions of the atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
. Hence, if there exists a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
J_{\ell}\cup K
for some
\ell\in\mathord{\{1,\dots,m\}}
, then for every
\ell\in\mathord{\{1,\dots,m\}}
, there exists a homomorphism
h^{\prime}
from
Q
to
J_{\ell}\cup K
(that only differs from
h
on the values of the variables that occur once in
Q_{1}
). Therefore, we first limit our discussion to the repair
J_{1}
.

In what follows, for a database
K
, we denote by
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(K)
the number of repairs of
K
w.r.t.
\Sigma
that do not entail
Q
(i.e.,
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(K)=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(K)-\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(K)
), and by
\mathsf{rfreq}_{K,\Sigma}(\neg Q)
the ratio
\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(K)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(K)}
. For every
c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
, let

m_{c}=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(E_{3}^{c}\cup J_{1})
and
n_{c}=\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E_{3}^{c}).
Since all the facts of
E_{3}^{c}
use the constant
c
at their left-most position, associated with the variable
x
in the corresponding atom, we have that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(E_{3}^{c}\cup J_{1})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q_{x\mapsto c}}(E_{3}^{c}\cup J_{1}).

Clearly, it holds that:

D^{\Sigma,Q}\ =\ E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup\left(\bigcup_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}E_{3}^{c}\right).

Therefore, if
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E_{1})=n_{1}
and
\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E_{2})=n_{2}
, then we have that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}n_{c}

since, as explained above, there are no conflicts among facts from
E_{3}^{c}
and
E_{3}^{b}
for
c\neq b
. Moreover,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q_{x\mapsto c}}(D^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ m_{c}\times n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{b\in\mathsf{dom}(D),b\neq c}n_{b}.

This holds since the repairs of
E_{2}
do not affect the entailment of the query
Q_{x\mapsto c}
(as
E_{2}
contains only facts over relation names that do not occur in
Q
), and so do not the repairs of
E_{3}^{b}
for
b\neq c
. Moreover, as aforementioned, if
K\cup J_{1}
entails
Q_{x\mapsto c}
for some repair
K
of
E_{3}^{c}
, then
K\cup J_{\ell}
also entails
Q_{x\mapsto c}
for all
\ell\in\mathord{\{2,\dots,m\}}
.

By employing similar arguments we can show that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}m_{c}.

Thus, we have that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \frac{m_{c}\times n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{b\in\mathsf{dom}(D),b\neq c}n_{b}}{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{b\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}n_{b}}=\frac{m_{c}}{n_{c}}

and

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q)\ =\ \frac{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}m_{c}}{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}n_{c}}=\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\frac{m_{c}}{n_{c}}.

By combining these two equations we get that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q)\ =\ \Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q_{x\mapsto c}).

Now,

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})-\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,\neg Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(\neg Q_{x\mapsto c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\left(1-\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\right).

This concludes our proof.

A.4
Proof of Lemma
4

Recall that this lemma deals with SJFCQs with a non-empty complex part that contains at least one atom
R(\bar{z})
such that:
(1)

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(R(\bar{z}))=\emptyset
, and
(2)

R(\bar{z})
has a variable
x
at a position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
.
Note that we have added here a condition on the variable
x
that does not occur in the corresponding lemma of Maslowski and Wijsen
[
25
]
—the variable
x
is associated with an attribute on the right-hand side of the primary FD. In the case of primary keys, every position of an
R
-atom
\alpha
in
Q
is associated with either an attribute that occurs on the left-hand side of the key of
R
or an attribute that occurs on the right-hand side of the key. In our setting, a position of
\alpha
may be associated with an attribute that does not occur at all in the primary FD; hence, this additional condition is needed to establish the correctness of the lemma in question.

Lemma

4
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. If there exists an
R
-atom
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
such that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and a variable
x
occurs in
\alpha
at a position of
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}
with
R:X\rightarrow Y
being the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
, then

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c}).

Proof.

Assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
. It clearly holds that

\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \bigcup_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}(D).

We now prove that for all
a\neq b
we have that

\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto a}}(D)\cap\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto b}}(D)\ =\ \emptyset.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is some repair
J
of
D
such that
J\in(\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto a}}(D)\cap\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto b}}(D))
for some
a\neq b
. Let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q_{x\mapsto a}
to
J
and let
h^{\prime}
be a homomorphism from
Q_{x\mapsto b}
to
J
.
Let
f=R(h(\bar{z}))
and
g=R(h^{\prime}(\bar{z}))
.

Since
h
maps the atoms of
Q_{x\mapsto a}
to facts of
J
, the fact
f
uses the constant
a
at each position of
\alpha
that uses the variable
x
. Similarly, the fact
g
uses the constant
b
at each position that uses the variable
x
. Moreover, the facts
f
and
g
agree with the atom
\alpha
on all the constants that occur in this atom. Since for each
A\in X
(where
X
is the left-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
) the position
(R,A)
is a primary-lhs position and all these positions are associated with constants in
\alpha
, the facts
f
and
g
agree on the values of all the attributes in
X
, but disagree on the value of at least one attribute in
Y
(the attribute corresponding to the position of
x
in
\alpha
). We conclude that
\{f,g\}\not\models\Sigma_{R}
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
J
is a repair.
Hence, it holds that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}(D)

and

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\frac{\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)}{\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}\ =\ \sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\frac{\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}}(D)}{\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}=\sum_{c\in\mathsf{dom}(D)}\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c}),

and the claim follows.

A.5
Proof of Lemma
6

We first prove two auxiliary lemmas that will be used in the proof of Lemma
6
. Similar lemmas have been used in the proof of hardness for primary keys, but here we have to take into account not only the primary FDs, but all the FDs of
\Sigma
. To this end, we use
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
.

Lemma 26
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. For an atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
, and a set
E
of
R
-facts such that (1)
E\not\models R(\bar{z})
, and (2) for all
R
-facts
f\in E
and
g\in D
,
there is a primary-lhs position
(R,A)
such that
f[A]\neq g[A]
, it holds that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D\cup E,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}((D\cup E)\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}))}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup E)}.

Proof.

Let
D^{\Sigma,Q}=D\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
and
E^{\Sigma,Q}=E\setminus(E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q})
.
Lemma
24
implies that there are no conflicts among
R
-facts
f\in D^{\Sigma,Q}
and
g\in E^{\Sigma,Q}
, and therefore:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D^{\Sigma,Q})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E^{\Sigma,Q}).

Since
E\not\models R(\bar{z})
(and so
E^{\Sigma,Q}\not\models R(\bar{z})
as well), it also holds that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E^{\Sigma,Q})\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D^{\Sigma,Q})\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E^{\Sigma,Q}).

We can then conclude that:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)

Now, Lemma
7
implies that:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}

and

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D\cup E,\Sigma}(Q)=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}((D\cup E)\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}))}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup E)}.

By combining these three results we obtain that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfreq}_{D\cup E,\Sigma}(Q)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}((D\cup E)\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}))}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup E)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\Sigma,Q},\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}((D\cup E)\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}))}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup E)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}((D\cup E)\setminus(D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}\cup E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}))}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\cup E)}

This concludes our proof.

The next technical lemma is rather straightforward.

Lemma 27
.

Consider a database
D
, a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. For
E\subseteq D
that contains all the facts of
D
over relations that occur in
Q
we have that:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)

Proof.

Clearly, it holds that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E)\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus E)

Moreover, since the relations of the facts in
D\setminus E
do not occur in
Q
, we have that:

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(E)\times\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus E)

We conclude that:

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q),

and the claim follows.

We are now ready to give the proof of the lemma in question, which we recall here:

Lemma

6
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
. For every set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
,
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
implies that
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

Proof.

We consider each one of the rewrite rules given in Figure
3
separately. To this end, given a database
D
, we need to construct in polynomial time a database
E
such that the value
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
can be computed from
\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)
.
The proofs for the rewrite rules R5, R7, and R10, have been already given in the main body of the paper. We now proceed to give the proofs for the remaining rewrite rules, which are similar to the corresponding proofs for primary keys
[
25
]
, but we have to take into account the FDs in the primary prefix. To this end, we use
D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q}
and
D_{\mathsf{ind}}^{\Sigma,Q}
.

Rewrite Rule R1
:
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha).

Let
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
be an atom of
Q
, and let
x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
x
occurs at the first position of
\alpha
. Let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
be the set of
R
-facts that use a constant
b\neq c
at the first position. Let
E=D\setminus D^{\prime}
.
Lemma
26
implies that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E\setminus E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

Note that
\Sigma_{R}
may have one primary FD w.r.t.
Q
and another primary FD w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
. This is the case, for example, if the position
(R,A)
of the variable
x
corresponds to the only attribute of the primary FD that is associated with a variable. In this case, the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
might be another FD that occurs later in the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
or
\Sigma_{R}
might have no primary FD w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
. However, if
(R,A)
is a primary-lhs position w.r.t.
Q
, then it is also a primary-lhs position w.r.t.
Q_{x\mapsto c}
.
Since every
R
-fact of
E
uses the value
c
at the first position, we get that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

Therefore, we can conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{x\mapsto c})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E\setminus E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

(The ratios in the above equation can be computed in polynomial time due to Proposition
1
.)

Rewrite Rule R2
:
(\Sigma,Q_{1}\cup Q_{2})\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{1})
if
Q_{1}\neq\emptyset
,
Q_{2}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset
.

Let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
be the subset of
D
that contains every fact over a relation name
R
that occurs in
Q_{1}
. Let
D^{\prime\prime}
be a set of facts over the relation names that occur in
Q_{2}
that is obtained via some function
h
from the variables of
Q_{2}
to constants (that is, for every atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q_{2}
, the database
D^{\prime\prime}
will contain the
R
-fact
R(h(\bar{z}))
). Clearly,
D^{\prime\prime}
is a consistent database (as it contains one fact per relation name) that entails
Q_{2}
. Let
E=D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
. Lemma
27
implies the following:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{1})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{1})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{2})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{2})\ =\ 1.

By Lemma
2
, we get that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{1})\times\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{2})=\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{1})\times\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{2})

By combining these above results, we conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{1})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{1})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{1})\times\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime\prime},\Sigma}(Q_{2})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

Rewrite Rule R3
:
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x})
if there is an atom
\alpha\in Q
with
x,y\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
.

Let
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
be an atom of
Q
, and let
x,y\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
x
and
y
occur at the first and second positions of
\alpha
, respectively. Let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
be the set of
R
-facts that use different constants at the first and second positions (i.e., the facts of the form
R(a,b,\bar{w})
for
a\neq b
). Let
E=D\setminus D^{\prime}
. Lemma
26
implies that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{y\mapsto x})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{y\mapsto x})\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E\setminus E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x}})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

(Note that, since every
R
-fact of
D\setminus E
uses different constants for the first two positions, and every
R
-fact of
E
uses the same constant for both positions, for all
R
-facts
f\in E
and
g\in D\setminus E
, the facts
f
and
g
disagree on at least one primary-lhs position.)
Since all the
R
-facts of
E
are of the form
R(a,a,\bar{w})
for some constant
a
, we have that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q_{y\mapsto x})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

Therefore, we can conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q_{y\mapsto x})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E)}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E\setminus E_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x}})}\times\frac{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D\setminus D_{\mathsf{conf}}^{\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x}})}{\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(D)}.

Rewrite Rule R4
:
(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{\alpha\}})\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q)
if
(\mathsf{var}(Q)\cap\mathsf{var}(\alpha))\subseteq\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
.

Assume
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
.
Let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
be the database that contains all the facts of
D
over the relation names that occur in
Q
. If
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, then
\mathsf{var}(Q)\cap\mathsf{var}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, and the rule R2 implies

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha\}),

where
E=D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime}
, and
D^{\prime\prime}
has a single fact that is obtained via a function
h
from the variables of
\alpha
to constants. If
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)\neq\emptyset
, let
K
be the database that contains, for every function
h
from
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
to
\mathsf{dom}(D)
, an
R
-fact
f
such that:

f[A]=\begin{cases}h(x)&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{if $\alpha[A]=x$ and $x\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)$}}\\
c&\mbox{if $\alpha[A]=c$ (where $c$ is a constant)}\\
\odot&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
K
is a consistent database since all the facts of
K
agree on the values of all attributes in
X_{j}\cup Y_{j}
for the FDs
R:X_{j}\rightarrow Y_{j}
in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
(as all these attributes are associated with constants in
\alpha
). Moreover, no two facts of
K
agree on the values of all primary-lhs positions; hence, no two facts violate an FD that occurs after the primary FD in the LHS chain of
\Sigma_{R}
. In addition, for every
f\in K
, we clearly have that
f\models\alpha
. Let
E=D^{\prime}\cup K
.
Lemma
27
implies that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q)

and

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D^{\prime},\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q).

By the definition of the database
K
, we have that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha\}).

This is because the only shared variables among
Q
and
\alpha
occur in
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
, and
K
contains a corresponding fact for every possible function from
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
to
\mathsf{dom}(D)
. Moreover, the database
K
is consistent; hence, every repair of
E
contains all the facts of
K
.
From the above results,

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma}(Q)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q\cup\{\alpha\}).

Rewrite Rule R8
:
(\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}})\triangleright\,((\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{R})\cup\Sigma_{R^{\prime}},Q\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}})
if
x
is an orphan variable of
Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}
, and
R^{\prime}
does not occur in
Q
.

Assume that the variable
x
occurs at the position
(R,A)
of
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
. As in rule R5, we denote by
\Sigma
the set of FDs over the schema
\mathbf{S}
that contains the relation
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
, and by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the set of FDs over the schema
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
that contains the relation
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
. We have that
\Sigma_{R^{\prime\prime}}=\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime\prime}}
for every relation name of
\mathbf{S}\cap\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is obtained from
\Sigma_{R}
by removing the attribute
A
from every FD of
\Sigma_{R}
, and then removing trivial FDs and merging FDs with the same left-hand side.
Given a database
D
over
\mathbf{S}
, let
E
be the database that contains all the facts of
D
over relation names
R^{\prime\prime}
such that
R^{\prime\prime}\neq R^{\prime}
, and, for every fact
f
over
R^{\prime}
, the following
R
-fact

f^{\prime}[B]\ =\ \begin{cases}f[B]&\mbox{if $B\neq A$}\\
\odot&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Clearly, we have that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

This holds since all the
R
-facts of
E
agree on the value of the additional attribute
A
; hence, this attribute does not affect the satisfaction of the FDs. Moreover, since the variable
x
occurs only once in the query, the value of this variable has no impact on the entailment of the query, and thus

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime},Q\cup\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}(E).

We conclude that

\mathsf{rfreq}_{\Sigma^{\prime},Q\cup\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}(D)\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{\Sigma,Q\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}}(E).

Rewrite Rule R9
:
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright\,(\Sigma,Q^{\prime})
if a constant
c
occurs in
Q
, some constant
c^{\prime}\neq c
occurs in
Q
, and
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
Q
by replacing all constants with
c
.

The proof for this rule is straightforward, as we are only renaming constants.

A.6
Proofs of Lemmas
8
–
11

We start by proving a series of basic properties of final pairs (Lemmas
28
–
42
) that are crucial for showing Lemmas
8
–
11
. Note that similar properties have been proved for primary keys
[
25
]
.
Recall that a pair
(\Sigma,Q)
, where
\Sigma
is an FD set with an LHS chain and
Q
is an SJFCQ, is final if: (1)
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, and (2) for every set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
such that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright^{+}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, the query
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe. As the proofs rely on the conditions of the procedure
\mathsf{IsSafe}
, we give the algorithm again below for the sake of readability.

See
1

The first property applies to any unsafe SJFCQ.

Lemma 28
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
that is not
\Sigma
-safe. It holds that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
has at least two atoms.

Proof.

We give a proof by contradiction. Assume that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
is empty or consists of a single atom.
If
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
, then
Q
is clearly
\Sigma
-safe, which contradicts our hypothesis that
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe.
If
Q
is such that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\mathord{\{\alpha\}}
, we proceed to show, by induction on the number of variables occurring in
\alpha
, that it is
\Sigma
-safe, which again contradicts our hypothesis that
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe.
Note that, since
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, it cannot be the case that
\alpha
has no variables; otherwise, all the positions of
\alpha
are primary-lhs positions by definition.

Base Case.

If
\alpha
mentions a single variable
x
at a primary-lhs position, then the condition of line 5 is satisfied by
Q
, and since
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe (as it has no complex part),
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe as well. If
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and
Q
uses a single variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position, then the condition of line 7 is satisfied by
Q
, and, again, since
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe,
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.

Inductive Step.

We now prove that if
\alpha
uses
n+1
variables, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe. We proceed by considering the following two cases:

1.

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)\neq\emptyset
. In this case, the condition of line 5 of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied. By the inductive hypothesis, the query
Q_{x\mapsto c}
that contains
n
variables is
\Sigma
-safe; hence,
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe as well.

2.

\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
. In this case, the condition of line 7 of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied. By the inductive hypothesis, the query
Q_{x\mapsto c}
that contains
n
variables is
\Sigma
-safe; hence,
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe as well.

We can therefore conclude that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
contains at least two atoms.

We next prove that the queries of final pairs falsify all the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
. This is a crucial property that will be heavily used in our proofs.

Lemma 29
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. It holds that
Q
falsifies all the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that
Q
satisfies one of the conditions of the algorithm.

1.

If
Q
satisfies the condition of line 1, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe.

2.

If
Q
satisfies the condition of line 3, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe if both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are
\Sigma
-safe. By the rewrite rule R2, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q_{1})
and
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q_{2})
, and since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, we get that both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are
\Sigma
-safe.

3.

If
Q
satisfies the condition of line 5, then
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe if
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe for an arbitrary constant
c
. By the rule R1,
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
, and since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe.

4.

If
Q
satisfies the condition of line 7, then there is an
R
-atom
\alpha
in
Q
with
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, and a variable
x
that occurs at a position of
\{(R,A)\mid A\in Y\}
, with
R:X\rightarrow Y
being the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
.
If there exists a liaison variable
y\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
, then by the rewrite rule R6, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q_{x\mapsto c})
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q_{x\mapsto c}
is
\Sigma
-safe, and according to the condition of line 7,
Q
is also
\Sigma
-safe.
If there is no liaison variable
y\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
, Lemma
28
implies that
Q\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha\}}
is not empty, and since
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
and there are no liaison variables in
\alpha
,
\mathsf{var}(Q\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha\}})\cap\mathsf{var}(\alpha)=\emptyset
. By the rewrite rule R2, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha\}})
and
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,\mathord{\{\alpha\}})
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is
\Sigma
-final, both
Q\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha\}}
and
\mathord{\{\alpha\}}
are
\Sigma
-safe; hence,
Q
is
\Sigma
-safe according to the condition of line 3.

Since in all the cases we obtain a contradiction to the fact that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, we conclude that
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, and the claim follows.

For the next lemma, recall that an orphan variable of
Q
is a variable that occurs once in
Q
, at a non-primary-lhs position.

Lemma 30
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and a an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. For every atom
\alpha\in Q
,
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

If
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
and
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, then the condition of line 3 of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q
, since a variable at a non-primary-lhs position of an atom outside the complex part is an orphan variable. Note that, by Lemma
28
,
Q\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha\}}\neq\emptyset
.
If
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
and
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
, then
Q
satisfies the condition of line 7 of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
.
This holds since every atom in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
has a primary FD (as it has a non-primary-lhs position), and if all the primary-lhs positions of the atom are associated with constants, then at least one of the primary-rhs positions must be associated with a variable.
In both cases, we get a contradiction to Lemma
29
.

We now use the above lemma to prove the following technical property of final pairs. Recall that, for every relation name
R
, we denote by
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
, and by
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{lhs}}
(resp.,
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
) the positions corresponding to the attributes of
X_{i_{R}}
(resp.,
Y_{i_{R}}
).

Lemma 31
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. There is no
R
-atom of
Q
that uses an orphan variable
x
at a position
(R,A)\not\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that
Q
contains an atom
\alpha=R(\bar{y},x,\bar{z})
such that
x
is an orphan variable that occurs at position
(R,A)\not\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Let
Q^{\prime}
be the query obtained from
Q
by replacing the atom
\alpha
with an atom
\alpha^{\prime}=R^{\prime}(\bar{y},\bar{z})
. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the FD set that is obtained from
\Sigma
by removing all the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
and adding, for each such FD
R:X\rightarrow Y
, the FD
R^{\prime}:(X\setminus\{A\})\rightarrow(Y\setminus\{A\})
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
(unless it is trivial). Note that it cannot be the case that
A\in X_{i_{R}}
or
A\in Y_{i_{R}}
; hence, the FD
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
.
Also note that since the variable
x
occurs only once in
Q
, we have that for every atom
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
,
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
. Moreover, it holds that
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
, as we have only removed an orphan variable from
\alpha
to obtain
\alpha^{\prime}
.

By the rule R8,
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, the query
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. We proceed by considering all possible cases:

1.

As explained above, we have that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)=\emptyset
if and only if
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Lemma
29
implies that the condition of line 1 is falsified by
Q
; hence,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)\neq\emptyset
, and this condition is falsified by
Q^{\prime}
as well.

2.

If the condition of line 3 is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
, the same condition is satisfied by
Q
, since the only additional variable in
Q
is an orphan variable. Thus, if
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}^{\prime}\cup Q_{2}^{\prime}
with
Q_{1}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1}^{\prime})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, it also holds that
Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
with
Q_{1}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset
, where
Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{2}=Q_{2}^{\prime}\setminus\mathord{\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}\cup\mathord{\{\alpha\}}
, assuming, without loss of generality, that
\alpha^{\prime}\in Q_{2}^{\prime}
. This cannot be the case due to Lemma
29
; thus, the condition of line 3 is falsified by
Q^{\prime}
.

3.

If the condition of line 5 is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
, then there is a variable
z
that occurs in
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\beta)
for every atom
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
. As said above,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
also contains all such atoms with
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, and it contains
\alpha
if and only if
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
contains
\alpha^{\prime}
. If this is the case, then
z\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\alpha^{\prime})
implies that
z\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)
(as we have not changed variables that occur at primary-lhs positions), and the same condition is satisfied by
Q
. Therefore, by Lemma
29
, we get that the condition of line 5 is falsified by
Q^{\prime}
.

4.

If the condition of line 7 is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
, then there exists
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
such that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\beta)=\emptyset
and
z\in\mathsf{var}(\beta)
. If
\beta\neq\alpha^{\prime}
, then clearly the same holds for
Q
. If
\beta=\alpha^{\prime}
, then it also holds that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)=\emptyset
(as we have not changed the primary-lhs positions of this atom), and
z\in\mathsf{var}(\alpha)
. Lemma
30
implies that
\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\alpha)\neq\emptyset
; hence, this cannot be the case, and the condition of line 7 is falsified by
Q^{\prime}
.

We have shown that all the conditions are falsified by
Q^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and the claim follows.

We next prove another technical property of final pairs
(\Sigma,Q)
that will allow us to show that atoms outside the complex part of
Q
are associated with a single key in
\Sigma
.

Lemma 32
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. For every
R
-atom of
Q
, it holds that
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix w.r.t.
Q
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that for some
R
-atom
\alpha
of
Q
,
\Sigma_{R}
has a primary prefix w.r.t.
Q
. Let
R:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
be an FD in the primary prefix of
\Sigma_{R}
w.r.t.
Q
. By the definition of primary prefix, we have that
\alpha[A]\in\mathbf{C}
for every
A\in(X_{i}\cup Y_{i})
; hence, at least once constant
c
occurs in
\alpha
. Assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{x},c,\bar{y})
. If
\alpha[A]=c
for some
A\in Y_{i}
, then
c
occurs at a non-primary-lhs position, and we can apply case (2) of R5. If
\alpha[A]=c
for some
A\in X_{i}
, then
c
also occurs on the left-hand side of the primary FD, that is, it occurs at a primary-lhs position. In this case, Lemma
30
implies that there is at least one primary-lhs position in
\alpha
that is associated with a variable; hence, we can apply case (4) of R5. In both cases, by rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{x},c,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\}})
, where
R^{\prime}
is a new relation name that does not occur in
Q
, and
\Sigma^{\prime}
is the FD set induced by the rewriting.

Let
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{x},c,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\}}
.
Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe; hence, one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. The position of
c
in
\alpha
is a non-primary-rhs position, as we consider canonical FD sets, thus it cannot appear in some FD in the primary prefix and on the right-hand side of the primary FD; hence, if
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
, then
R^{\prime}:(X_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A\}})\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
is the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
(and if
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary FD, then so does
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
). Moreover, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
for every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, and
R(\bar{x},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
R^{\prime}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
. This is because constants at positions associated with attributes of the primary prefix have no impact on the complex part of the query. Hence, a condition satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
is also satisfied by
Q
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

At this point, we have all the ingredients for showing Lemma
8
.

Lemma

8
.

If the
R
-atom of
Q
occurs in
Q\setminus\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, then
\Sigma_{R}
consists of a single key.

Proof.

By Lemma
32
,
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix.
Since the
R
-atom
\alpha
of
Q
is not in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, there are no constants or liaison variables at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
. Moreover, Lemma
31
implies that every atom of
Q
uses orphan variables only at positions of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
.
Since there are no variables at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
,
\Sigma_{R}
has no FDs, i.e., it contains a single trivial key
R:\mathsf{att}(R)\rightarrow\emptyset
. If there are (orphan) variables at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
, then they occur on the right-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
, and
\Sigma_{R}
has a single key
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
.

We now know that for relation names
R
that occur in atoms outside the complex part of a final query, the set of FDs
\Sigma_{R}
consists of a single key. We proceed to show that for atoms over a relation name
R
in the complex part of the query,
\Sigma_{R}
either consists of a single key, or it is a set of FDs of one of six specific forms (Lemmas
9
–
11
). To this end, we continue proving crucial technical properties of final pairs ((Lemmas
33
–
42
)). The proofs of most properties are similar and take the following general form. We assume, towards a contradiction, that the property is not satisfied by a final pair
(\Sigma,Q)
, where
\Sigma
is a set of FDs with an LHS chain and
Q
an SJFCQ. We then show that
Q
can be rewritten into another query
Q^{\prime}
using one of the rewrite rules, and then show that
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, which contradicts the fact that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final.

In most proofs, we use the rules R5 and R8, and the proofs are mainly based on the following observations (assuming that in the rewriting we remove an atom over a relation name
R
and add an atom over
R^{\prime}
). Whenever we rewrite a pair
(\Sigma,Q)
into a pair
(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
using one of these two rules, the set of FDs changes accordingly as we have already explained. It might be the case that the set of FDs
\Sigma_{R}
has a certain primary FD, while the set
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
has a different primary FD or no primary FD at all. This is the case, for example, when the
R^{\prime}
-atom of
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from the
R
-atom of
Q
by removing an attribute
A
, and the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is of the form
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
. In this case, the corresponding FD in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is trivial; hence, it is removed from
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
. This is also the case when we remove the only attribute
A
in the primary FD that is associated with a variable in the atom (Lemma
30
implies that
A
corresponds to a primary-lhs position). Here, the FD in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
that corresponds to the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
becomes part of the primary prefix.

In such cases, the primary-lhs (and non-primary-lhs) positions might significantly change, which, in turn, heavily affects the complex part of the query.
However, as long as we make sure that we do not remove in the rewriting the only attribute in the right-hand side of the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
or the only attribute of the primary FD that is associated with a variable, the FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
that is obtained from the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
remains the primary FD, and the set of non-primary-lhs positions of
R^{\prime}
is the same as the set of non-primary-lhs positions of
R
(except for the position corresponding to the removed attribute
A
). If we also make sure that all the liaison variables in the atoms of
Q
remain liaison variables in the corresponding atoms of
Q^{\prime}
, then both queries will have a similar complex part. This will allow us to prove that if
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, then
Q^{\prime}
is also not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe and obtain the desired contradiction.

Lemma 33
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
where
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
has a variable that occurs at a primary-lhs position and a non-primary-lhs position.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that
Q
has an atom
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
that uses the variable
x
at both a primary-lhs position and a non-primary-lhs position. Then, by the rewrite rule R10, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}}
, i.e.,
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
Q
by replacing the occurrence of the variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position with the constant
c
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
since the atom
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
uses a constant at a non-primary-lhs position; hence,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.

2.

If
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then
Q
also satisfies the condition of line 3, since the variable
x
still occurs at
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every atom
\alpha\neq R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
we have that
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
. This holds since the variable
x
still occurs at
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
; hence, if it is a liaison variable of
\alpha
in
Q
it is also a liaison variable of
\alpha
in
Q^{\prime}
. Moreover,
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
because it uses the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position, and
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
because it uses a constant at a non-primary-lhs position. Therefore, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5, then clearly
Q
also satisfies this condition (with the same variable that occurs at a primary-lhs position of every atom in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
), which contradicts Lemma
29
.

4.

It is rather straightforward that if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 7, then so does
Q
, which again contradicts Lemma
29
, as we have not changed the primary-lhs positions of any atom of
Q
, and, as said above, for every
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
we have that
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
. Note that it cannot be the case that
Q^{\prime}
satisfies this condition due to the atom
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
