as it has the variable
x
at a primary-lhs position.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 34
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
associates two non-primary-lhs positions with constants.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses a constant
c
at position
(R,A)
and a constant
c^{\prime}
(that might be the constant
c
) at position
(R,A^{\prime})
; both are non-primary-lhs positions. Lemma
32
implies that
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix w.r.t.
Q
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
A^{\prime}\geq A
. Thus, if one of these positions occurs in
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then
(R,A)\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Also, assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},c^{\prime},\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c^{\prime},\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
not in
Q
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. Observe that if the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
because
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
is not empty (if
A\in Y_{i_{R}}
, then
A\in Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
, and if
A\not\in Y_{i_{R}}
then, by our assumption,
A^{\prime}\not\in Y_{i_{R}}
and
Y_{i_{R}}
has an attribute
B
such that
B\neq A
and
B\neq A^{\prime}
).

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position; thus,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as we have only removed a constant from an atom of
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
since both atoms use the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position. Thus, it is clear that if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 35
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
associates two non-primary-lhs positions with orphan variables.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses orphan variables
x
and
w
at positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
, respectively; both are non-primary-lhs positions. Assume, without loss of generality, that
A^{\prime}\geq A
; hence, if one of these positions occurs in
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then
(R,A)\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Also assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})
. By rule R8, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
that does not occur in
Q
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. As in Lemma
34
, if the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
since
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}\neq\emptyset
, and Lemma
30
implies that an attribute of
X_{i_{R}}
is associated with a variable in
\alpha
.

1.

If the condition of line 1 is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
, then it is also satisfied by
Q
as we have only removed an orphan variable, which does not affect the complex part of the query. This is a contradiction to Lemma
29
.

2.

For the same reason, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, as
w
is an orphan variable and for all the non-primary-lhs positions of
R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})
besides
(R,A^{\prime})
, the corresponding positions of
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
are also non-primary-lhs positions. This holds because, as aforementioned,
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
is the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
. Hence, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 36
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom
\alpha
of
Q
associates two non-primary-lhs positions with the same liaison variable
x
if
x
also occurs in another atom
\alpha^{\prime}
of
Q
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses the same liaison variable
x
at two non-primary-lhs positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
, and there is another atom
\alpha^{\prime}
that also uses the variable
x
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
A^{\prime}\geq A
; thus, if one of these positions occurs in
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then
(R,A)\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Also assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
that does not occur in
Q
(and we have removed the occurrence of
x
in the position
(R,A^{\prime})
to obtain
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
). Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
due to Lemma
30
and the fact that
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
is not empty.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
as the atom
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position (and
x
remains a liaison variable as it has another occurrence in the atom
\alpha^{\prime}
).

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as the variable
x
still occurs in
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, as
x
is a liaison variable that occurs at a non-primary-lhs position in both atoms. Hence, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 37
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
associates three non-primary-lhs positions with the same liaison variable
x
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses the same liaison variable
x
at three non-primary-lhs positions
(R,A)
,
(R,A^{\prime})
,
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
A^{\prime}\geq A
; hence, if one of these positions occurs in
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then
(R,A)\in I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Also assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
that does not occur in
Q
(and we have removed the occurrence of
x
in position
(R,A^{\prime})
to obtain
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
). Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
due to Lemma
30
and since
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}\neq\emptyset
.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
as
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position, and
x
remains a liaison as it has two more occurrences in
\alpha
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as the variable
x
still occurs in
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},x,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, as
x
is a liaison variable in both cases. Hence, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 38
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
has a liaison variable at a position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
and a constant at a non-primary-lhs position.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses a liaison variable
x
at the position
(R,A)
of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, and a constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position
(R,A^{\prime})
. Assume also that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
not in
Q
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. Note that if the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
because
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
contains the attribute
A
that is associated with a variable.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position; thus,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as we have only removed a constant from an atom of
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
since both atoms use the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position. Thus, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 39
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom of
Q
has a liaison variable or a constant at a position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
and an orphan variable at a non-primary-lhs position.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses a liaison variable
x
or a constant
c
at the position
(R,A)
of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
and an orphan variable
w
at a non-primary-lhs position
(R,A^{\prime})
. Also, assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R8, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
that does not occur in
Q
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
because
A\in Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
, and Lemma
30
implies that an attribute of
X_{i_{R}}
is associated with a variable.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
x
or the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position; thus,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as we have only removed an orphan variable from an atom of
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
since both atoms use the liaison variable
x
or the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position. Thus, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 40
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. No atom
\alpha
of
Q
has two distinct liaison variables
with at least one of them occurring at a non-primary-lhs position of
\alpha
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha=R(\bar{z})
of
Q
that uses a liaison variable
x
at position
(R,A)
, and a liaison variable
y\neq x
at position
(R,A^{\prime})
. Since none of the variables is an orphan variable and at least one of them occurs at a non-primary-lhs position of
\alpha
, by the rewrite rule R7 we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma,Q_{y\mapsto x})
. Let
Q^{\prime}=Q_{y\mapsto x}
. For every atom
\beta\in Q
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\beta_{y\mapsto x}\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
, where
\beta_{y\mapsto x}
is obtained from
\beta

by replacing every occurrence of the variable
y
with the variable
x
. This is clearly the case if
\beta
has a constant or a liaison variable
z\neq y
at a non-primary-lhs position. This is also the case if
y
is a liaison variable of
\beta
, since
x
is a liaison variable of
\beta_{y\mapsto x}
.
Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because the variable
x
is a liaison variable that occurs at a non-primary-lhs position of
\alpha_{y\mapsto x}
; hence,
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.

2.

If
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}^{\prime}\cup Q_{2}^{\prime}
for
Q_{1}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1}^{\prime})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Assume, without loss of generality, that all the atoms that use the variable
x
are in
Q_{1}^{\prime}
. Then, we have that
Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
, where
Q_{1}
contains every atom that uses either the variable
x
or the variable
y
and
Q_{2}=Q_{2}^{\prime}
. Clearly, we have that
Q_{1}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset
; thus,
Q
satisfies the condition of line 3, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

As said above, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\beta_{y\mapsto x}\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
. If there is a variable
z
that appears at a primary-lhs position of every atom in
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
, then it must be the case that
z\neq x
, since Lemma
33
implies that none of the variables
x
or
y
occurs at a primary-lhs position of
\alpha
and
\alpha_{y\mapsto x}\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q^{\prime})
. Therefore, we have that
z\in\mathsf{plhsvar}_{\Sigma}(\beta)
for every
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
and
Q
satisfies the condition of line 5, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

4.

If the condition of line 7 is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
, then it is also satisfied by
Q
as we have not changed constants or removed variables from primary-lhs positions, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 41
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. If the
R
-atom of
Q
uses a liaison variable
x
or a constant
c
at a position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then any liaison variable has at most one occurrence at a non-primary-lhs position outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses a liaison variable
x
or a constant
c
at the position
(R,A)
of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, and there is a liaison variable
w
that occurs at two distinct non-primary-lhs positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
of
\alpha
outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
A^{\prime}\geq A^{\prime\prime}
. Also assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5, we have that
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},w,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
that does not occur in
Q
(and we have removed the occurrence of
w
at position
(R,A^{\prime})
to obtain
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
). Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
because
A\in Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
, and Lemma
30
implies that an attribute of
X_{i_{R}}
is associated with a variable.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
x
or the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position; thus,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
. Note that
x
is a liaison variable of
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
even if
w=x
, as in this case, there is another occurrence of the variable
x
at position
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as the variable
w
still occurs in
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa (if the variable
w
occurs at a non-primary-lhs position of
\beta
, then it is still a liaison variable as it has another occurrence in
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
). Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
since both atoms use the liaison variable
x
or the constant
c
at a non-primary-lhs position. Therefore, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

Lemma 42
.

Consider a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final. If the
R
-atom of
Q
uses an orphan variable
x
at a position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, then non-primary-lhs positions outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
are associated only with constants or only with liaison variables.

Proof.

Assume, towards a contradiction, that there is an atom
\alpha
of
Q
over a relation name
R
that uses an orphan variable
x
at the position
(R,A)
of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, and there is another non-primary-lhs position
(R,A^{\prime})
outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
that is associated with a constant
c
and a non-primary-lhs position
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
that is associated with a liaison variable
y
. Assume that
\alpha=R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})
. By the rewrite rule R5,
(\Sigma,Q)\triangleright(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, where
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\}}\cup\mathord{\{R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\}}
for a relation name
R^{\prime}
not in
Q
. Since
(\Sigma,Q)
is final,
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
is satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
. If the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow Y_{i_{R}}
, then the primary FD of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is
R^{\prime}:X_{i_{R}}\rightarrow(Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})
because
Y_{i_{R}}\setminus\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}}
contains the attribute
A
that is associated with a variable in
\alpha
.

1.

The condition of line 1 is not satisfied by
Q^{\prime}
because
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})
uses the liaison variable
y
at a non-primary-lhs position; thus,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
.

2.

Clearly, if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 3, then so does
Q
, as we have only removed a constant from an atom of
Q
to obtain
Q^{\prime}
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every
\beta\in(Q\cap Q^{\prime})
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
, since every liaison variable of
Q^{\prime}
is also a liaison variable of
Q
and vice versa. Moreover,
R^{\prime}(\bar{z},\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
and
R(\bar{z},c,\bar{y})\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
since both atoms use the liaison variable
y
at a non-primary-lhs position. Thus, it is clear that if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then
Q
satisfies the same condition, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

That concludes our proof.

We now have all the ingredients for proving Lemmas
9
–
11
, which essentially tell us that, for a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, all the
R
-atoms of
\mathsf{comp}_{Q}(\Sigma)
are such that
\Sigma_{R}
either has a single key or is a set of FDs of one of six specific forms.

Lemma

9
.

If the
R
-atom of
Q
has a liaison variable
x
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is also associated with the variable
x
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be the
R
-atom of
Q
. Lemma
32
implies that
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix.
Lemmas
38
,
39
and
40
imply that there are no constants, orphan variables, or other liaison variables at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
. Moreover, Lemma
41
implies that there are at most two occurrences of the variable
x
at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
. If there is one occurrence of the variable
x
, then clearly
\Sigma_{R}
has a single key (with one attribute on the right-hand side, i.e., the attribute
A
), and if there are two occurrences, then either
\Sigma_{R}
has a single key (with two attributes on the right-hand side) or
\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD and the second occurrence of
x
is at a position corresponding to an attribute that does not occur in any FD. This is because there is only one attribute that does not occur at the primary FD (as there are only two occurrences of
x
), and if there is another FD in
\Sigma_{R}
, then this attribute occurs in its left-hand side, which means that its right-hand side is empty. In this case, the additional FD is a trivial FD that can be removed from
\Sigma_{R}
.

Lemma

10
.

If the
R
-atom
\alpha_{R}
of
Q
has a constant
c
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a liaison variable
x
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
9
. Let
\alpha
be the
R
-atom of
Q
.
Lemma
32
implies that
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix. Lemmas
34
,
39
and
40
imply that there are no constants, orphan variables, or two distinct liaison variables at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
(besides the position of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
that is associated with the constant
c
). Moreover, Lemma
41
implies that there is at most one occurrence of some liaison variable
x
at non-primary-lhs positions of
\alpha
. If there are no occurrences of a liaison variable
x
, then clearly
\Sigma_{R}
has a single key (with the attribute
A
in its right-hand side). If there is one occurrence of such a variable, then Lemma
38
implies that it occurs in a position outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, in which case
\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD and the occurrence of
x
is at a position corresponding to an attribute that does not occur in any FD. As for Lemma
9
, there is no additional FD in the set, as there is only one non-primary-lhs position outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
.

Lemma

11
.

If the
R
-atom
\alpha
of
Q
has an orphan variable
x
at a primary-rhs position
(R,A)
, then

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single key, or

2.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a liaison variable
y
, or

3.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
that is associated with a constant
c
, or

4.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
, and there are only two non-primary-FD positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
that are associated with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in
\alpha
, or

5.

\Sigma_{R}
has two FDs
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
and
R:(X\cup\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{A^{\prime\prime}\}}
such that the first is the primary FD, there are no non-primary-lhs positions besides
(R,A)
,
(R,A^{\prime})
,
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
(where
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
are also non-primary-rhs positions), and the positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
are associated with the same liaison variable
y
that occurs only in the atom
\alpha
.

Proof.

Lemma
32
implies that
\Sigma_{R}
has no primary prefix.
Lemma
39
implies that there are no constants or liaison variables at positions of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Lemma
35
implies that there are no other orphan variables at the positions of
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. Hence, there is a single position in
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
and this position is associated with the orphan variable
x
. Then, the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
is
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
.

Lemma
35
implies that no non-primary-lhs positions outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
are associated with orphan variables. Lemma
42
implies that the non-primary-lhs positions outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
are associated either with constants or liaison variables, but not both. If the non-primary-lhs positions outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
are associated with constants, then Lemma
34
implies that there is only one such position
(R,A^{\prime})
. Thus, the only possible case is that this position is associated with an attribute that does not occur in any FD. There cannot be an additional FD that occurs after the primary FD in the chain as
(R,A^{\prime})
is the only position that does not correspond to an attribute of the primary FD, and as explained in the previous lemmas, at least two additional positions are required to add a non-trivial FD. Moreover, as aforementioned, the primary prefix is empty.

If the non-primary-lhs positions outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
are associated with liaison variables, by Lemma
40
they are all associated with the same liaison variable
x
. Lemma
36
implies that if
x
occurs also at another atom of the query, then it has only one occurrence at a non-primary-lhs position outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
, in which case the corresponding attribute does not occur in any FD (as explained above, there are not enough positions that are not associated with attributes of the primary FD for an additional FD). Lemma
37
implies that if
x
occurs only in one atom, then it has two occurrences at non-primary-lhs positions
(R,A^{\prime})
and
(R,A^{\prime\prime})
outside
I_{R,i_{R}}^{\mathsf{rhs}}
. In this case, either the two corresponding attributes do not occur in any FD or there is an FD
R:(X\cup\mathord{\{A^{\prime}\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{A^{\prime\prime}\}}
.

A.7
Proofs of Lemmas
12
–
17

For a set
\Sigma
of FDs with an LHS chain, and an SJFCQ
Q
such that
(\Sigma,Q)
is final, Lemmas
8
–
11
imply that for every relation name
R
that occurs in
Q
,
\Sigma_{R}
consists of a single key, a single FD, or two FDs. We now prove that if
Q
has
n>0
non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma
, then there is another set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of FDs and query
Q^{\prime}
such that:

•

n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
,

•

Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, and

•

\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
.

We prove this separately for each one of the six cases that we have identified in Lemmas
9
,
10
, and
11
.
The proofs for the different cases are similar; hence, we provide the full proof once, and only the essential details for all the other cases. The idea is the following. We replace an atom
R(\bar{z})
of
Q
for which
\Sigma_{R}
does not consist of a single key with two atoms
P(\bar{y})
and
T(\bar{w})
in
Q^{\prime}
. The atom
P(\bar{y})
has the primary FD of
\Sigma_{R}
as its single key, and the atom
T(\bar{w})
is similar to the original atom, except that its associated set of FDs is empty, or equivalently, consists of a single trivial key (hence,
T(\bar{w})
does not belong to the complex part of
Q^{\prime}
). Using the atom
P(\bar{y})
we ensure that there is a one-to-one correspondence between repairs w.r.t.
\Sigma
and repairs w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and using the atom
T(\bar{w})
we ensure that:
(1)
there is one-to-one correspondence between repairs w.r.t.
\Sigma
that entail
Q
and repairs w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
that entail
Q^{\prime}
, and
(2)

P(\bar{y})
is in the complex part of
Q^{\prime}
(as
R(\bar{z})
is in the complex part of
Q
). This implies that since
Q
is not
\Sigma
-safe, the query
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe.
In what follows, we write
\mathbf{S}
for the schema of
\Sigma
and
Q
, and
\mathbf{S^{\prime}}
for the schema of
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
.

Lemma

12
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the positions
(R,A)
and
(R,A^{\prime})
are both associated with a liaison variable
x
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume, without loss of generality, that the
R
-atom of
Q
is
R(\bar{z},x,x)
. Let
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,x)\}}\cup\mathord{\{P(\bar{z},x),T(\bar{z},x,x)\}}
, where
P,T
are fresh relation names not in
Q
. We denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the set of FDs obtained from
\Sigma
by removing the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
and adding the FDs
P:(\mathsf{att}(P)\setminus\mathord{\{B\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{B\}}
and
T:\mathsf{att}(T)\rightarrow\emptyset
, where
B
is the attribute associated with the variable
x
in
P(\bar{z},x)
.

Let
D
be a database over the schema
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Lemma
27
implies that the only relations that affect the relative frequency of
Q^{\prime}
w.r.t.
D
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are those that occur in
Q^{\prime}
. Hence, we assume, without loss of generality, that
D
contains only such relations. Given the database
D
, we construct a database
E
over the schema
\mathbf{S}
as follows. For every relation
R^{\prime}
that occurs in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we add all the
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
to
E
. Moreover, for every fact
P(\bar{a},b)
in
D
we add to
R
the fact
R(\bar{a},b,\odot)
, where
\odot
is a constant not in
D
, if
T(\bar{a},b,b)\not\in D
and the fact
R(\bar{a},b,b)
if
T(\bar{a},b,b)\in D
.

Given a subset
J
of
D
, the corresponding subset
J^{\prime}
of
E
is defined as follows. For every relation
R^{\prime}
that occurs in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, the subsets
J
and
J^{\prime}
contain the same
R^{\prime}
-facts. Moreover, for every fact
P(\bar{a},b)\in J
, the subset
J^{\prime}
contains the fact
R(\bar{a},b,*)
, where
*
is either
\odot
or
b
. It is rather straightforward that
J
is a repair of
D
if and only if
J^{\prime}
is a repair of
E
. Clearly, for every relation name
R^{\prime}
that occurs in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we have that
J
satisfies
\Sigma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
if and only if
J
satisfies
\Sigma_{R^{\prime}}
as both repairs contain the same facts over these relation names and the sets of FDs are the same. Moreover,
J
satisfies
\Sigma^{\prime}_{P}
if and only if
J^{\prime}
satisfies
\Sigma_{R}
because for every
\bar{a}
,
J
contains a single fact of the form
P(\bar{a},b)
(and satisfies
\Sigma^{\prime}_{P}
) if and only if
J^{\prime}
contains a single fact of the form
P(\bar{a},b,*)
for the same
b
(hence, it satisfies
\Sigma_{R}
). Clearly,
J
is maximal if and only if
J^{\prime}
is maximal, since we could add another fact
R(\bar{a},b,*)
to
J^{\prime}
without violating consistency (if no fact of the form
R(\bar{a},*,*)
already occurs in
J^{\prime}
) if and only if we could add the fact
R(\bar{a},b)
to
J
without violating consistency (if no fact of the form
R(\bar{a},*)
already occurs in
J
). Note that the only FD in
\Sigma^{\prime}_{T}
is trivial; hence,
J
contains all the
T
-facts of
D
. We conclude that there is one-to-one correspondence between the repairs of
D
w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
and the repairs of
E
w.r.t.
\Sigma
. Therefore,

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma}(E).

Next, we show that a repair
J
of
D
entails
Q^{\prime}
if and only if the corresponding repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
entails
Q
. Let
J
be a repair of
D
that entails
Q^{\prime}
, and let
h
be a homomorphism from
Q^{\prime}
to
J
. We claim that
h
is a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
. Clearly, for every relation name
R^{\prime}
that occurs in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, the fact that
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}))\in J
, where
R^{\prime}(\bar{z})
is the atom of
Q^{\prime}
over the relation name
R^{\prime}
, implies that
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}))\in J^{\prime}
, as the two repairs contain the same facts over these relation names. Moreover, by the definition of the repair
J^{\prime}
, the fact that
P(h(\bar{z},x))\in J
and
T(h(\bar{z},x,x))\in J
implies that
R(h(\bar{z},x,x))\in J^{\prime}
. Therefore,
h
is indeed a homomorphism from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
.
We can similarly show that if
J^{\prime}
is a repair of
E
that entails
Q
, then the corresponding repair
J
of
D
entails
Q^{\prime}
. For a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to
J^{\prime}
, we clearly have that
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}))\in J
if and only if
R^{\prime}(h(\bar{z}))\in J^{\prime}
for relation names
R^{\prime}
that occur in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, if
R(h(\bar{z},x,x))\in J^{\prime}
, then
P(h(\bar{z},x))\in J
, and since
J
contains all the
T
-facts of
D
, we also have that
T(h(\bar{z},x,x))\in J
, and
h
is a homomorphism from
Q^{\prime}
to
J
. We conclude that

\sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime}}(D)\ =\ \sharp\mathsf{rep}_{\Sigma,Q}(E)

and

\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})\ =\ \mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q).

It is only left to show that
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe. Assume, towards a contradiction, that
Q^{\prime}
is
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, which in turn implies that it satisfies one of the conditions of
\mathsf{IsSafe}
.

1.

We have that
\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q)\neq\emptyset
because
P(\bar{z},x)
has the liaison variable
x
at a non-primary-lhs position (it is a liaison variable as it also occurs in
T(\bar{z},x,x)
); thus,
Q^{\prime}
does not satisfy the condition of line 1.

2.

If
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}^{\prime}\cup Q_{2}^{\prime}
with
Q_{1}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1}^{\prime})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, it also holds that
Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
with
Q_{1}\neq\emptyset\neq Q_{2}
and
\mathsf{var}(Q_{1})\cap\mathsf{var}(Q_{2})=\emptyset
, where
Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{\prime}
and
Q_{2}=Q_{2}^{\prime}\setminus\mathord{\{P(\bar{z},x),T(\bar{z},x,x)\}}\cup\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,x)\}}
, assuming, w.l.o.g, that
P(\bar{z},x),T(\bar{z},x,x)\in Q_{2}^{\prime}
. In this case,
Q
also satisfies the condition of line 3, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

3.

For every atom
\beta
that occurs in both
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, we have that
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
if and only if
\beta\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
because every liaison variable in
Q
is also a liaison variable in
Q^{\prime}
and vice versa. Moreover,
R(\bar{z},x,x)\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma}(Q)
and
P(\bar{z},x)\in\mathsf{comp}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})
, and since both atoms have the same variables at primary-lhs positions, it is rather clear that if
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the condition of line 5 or the condition of line 7, then so does
Q
, which contradicts Lemma
29
.

This concludes our proof.

Lemma

13
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with a constant
c
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a liaison variable
x
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume, without loss of generality, that the
R
-atom of
Q
is
R(\bar{z},c,x)
. Let
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},c,x)\}}\cup\mathord{\{P(\bar{z},c),T(\bar{z},c,x)\}}
, where
P
and
T
are two fresh relation names not in
Q
. We denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the set of FDs obtained from
\Sigma
by removing the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
and adding the FDs
P:(\mathsf{att}(P)\setminus\mathord{\{B\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{B\}}
and
T:\mathsf{att}(T)\rightarrow\emptyset
, where
B
is the attribute associated with the constant
c
in
P(\bar{z},c)
.

Given a database
D
over the schema
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we construct a database
E
over
\mathbf{S}
by adding to
E
all the
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
for relations
R^{\prime}
that occur in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Moreover, for every fact
P(\bar{a},b)
such that either
(1)

b\neq c
or
(2)

b=c
and there is no fact of the form
T(\bar{a},c,*)
in
D
, we add a fact
R(\bar{a},b,\odot)
to
E
for a constant
\odot
that does not occur in
D
. For every fact
P(\bar{a},c)
such that there is a fact of the form
T(\bar{a},c,d)
in
D
, we add the fact
R(\bar{a},c,d)
to
E
. (If there are multiple facts of the form
T(\bar{a},c,*)
for some
\bar{a}
we also add multiple facts to
R
. These facts are not in a conflict, as they agree on the value of the attribute in the right-hand side of the FD of
\Sigma_{R}
.)

We can again show that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})=\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)
and
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe. The proof is similar to that of Lemma
12
and we do not give it again. Observe that a homomorphism
h
from
Q^{\prime}
to a repair
J
of
D
is such that
P(h(\bar{z},c))\in J
and
T(h(\bar{z},c,x))\in J
, in which case, by the definition of
J^{\prime}
,
R(h(\bar{z},c,x))\in J^{\prime}
. A homomorphism
h
from
Q
to a repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
is such that
R(h(\bar{z},c,x))\in J^{\prime}
; hence,
P(h(\bar{z},c))\in J
. Moreover, the variable
x
has at least one more occurrence in
Q
; hence,
h(x)\in\mathsf{dom}(D)
and
h(x)\neq\odot
. This implies that
T(h(\bar{z},c,x))\in J
by the definition of
J^{\prime}
.

Lemma

14
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only one non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a liaison variable
y
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume, without loss of generality, that the
R
-atom of
Q
is
R(\bar{z},x,y)
. Let
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,y)\}}\cup\mathord{\{P(\bar{z},x),T(\bar{z},x,y)\}}
, where
P
and
T
are two fresh relation names not in
Q
. We denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the FD set obtained from
\Sigma
by removing the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
and adding the FDs
P:(\mathsf{att}(P)\setminus\mathord{\{B\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{B\}}
and
T:\mathsf{att}(T)\rightarrow\emptyset
, where
B
is the attribute associated with the variable
x
in
P(\bar{z},x)
.

Given a database
D
over the schema
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we construct a database
E
over
\mathbf{S}
by adding to
E
all the
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
for the relation names
R^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{S}\cap\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Moreover, for every fact
P(\bar{a},b)
such that there is no fact of the form
T(\bar{a},b,*)
in
D
we add
R(\bar{a},b,\odot)
to
E
for a constant
\odot
that does not occur in
D
. For every fact
P(\bar{a},b)
such that there is a fact of the form
T(\bar{a},b,c)
in
D
, we add the fact
R(\bar{a},b,c)
to
E
. (If there are multiple facts of the form
T(\bar{a},b,*)
for some
\bar{a}
we also add multiple facts to
R
, and these facts do not jointly violate
\Sigma_{R}
.)

We can show that
\mathsf{rfreq}_{D,\Sigma^{\prime}}(Q^{\prime})=\mathsf{rfreq}_{E,\Sigma}(Q)

and
Q^{\prime}
is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe. Note that, similarly to Lemma
13
, a homomorphism
h
from
Q
to a repair
J^{\prime}
of
E
is such that
R(h(\bar{z},x,y))\in J^{\prime}
, and since
y
is a liaison variable of
Q
,
h(y)\neq\odot
. This implies that
T(\bar{z},x,y)\in J
by the definition of
J
.

Lemma

15
.

If for the
R
-atom of
Q
for some relation name
R
it holds that

1.

\Sigma_{R}
has a single FD
R:X\rightarrow\mathord{\{A\}}
,

2.

there is only non-primary-FD position
(R,A^{\prime})
, and

3.

the position
(R,A)
is associated with an orphan variable
x
and
(R,A^{\prime})
with a constant
c
,

then
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is Cook reducible to
\mathsf{RelFreq}(\Sigma,Q)
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}
and an SJFCQ
Q^{\prime}
that is not
\Sigma^{\prime}
-safe, where
n-1
atoms of
Q^{\prime}
are non-single-key atoms w.r.t.
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume, without loss of generality, that the
R
-atom of
Q
is
R(\bar{z},x,c)
. Let
Q^{\prime}=Q\setminus\mathord{\{R(\bar{z},x,c)\}}\cup\mathord{\{P(\bar{z},x),T(\bar{z},x,c)\}}
, where
P
and
T
are two fresh relation names not in
Q
. We denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the FD set obtained from
\Sigma
by removing the FDs of
\Sigma_{R}
and adding the FDs
P:(\mathsf{att}(P)\setminus\mathord{\{B\}})\rightarrow\mathord{\{B\}}
and
T:\mathsf{att}(T)\rightarrow\emptyset
, where
B
is the attribute associated with the variable
x
in
P(\bar{z},x)
.

Given a database
D
over
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we construct a database
E
over
\mathbf{S}
by adding to
E
all the
R^{\prime}
-facts of
D
for relations
R^{\prime}
that occur in both
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Moreover, for every fact
P(\bar{a},b)
such that there is no fact
T(\bar{a},b,c)
in
D
we add a fact
R(\bar{a},b,\odot)
to
E
for a constant
\odot
not in
